Light-cones in many-body open quantum systems with memory
Xie-Hang Yu, Mark Rudner, Rahul Trivedi

Abstract
Memory effects in many-body quantum systems interacting with an environment significantly impact their behavior. Nevertheless, much of our understanding of many-body open quantum systems is based on models within the Born-Markov approximation where these effects are neglected. In this paper, we consider a general class of spatially local open quantum lattice models with a non-Markovian environment that is approximated as Gaussian. Our main result is a set of Lieb-Robinson bounds for such models. For models where the environment has independent local baths, we show that the Lieb-Robinson velocity is controlled by one-norm of the memory kernel within the evolution time-interval, yielding a linear light cone whenever the memory kernel has a bounded one-norm. For models where excitations can propagate through the environment, we prove a polynomial light cone that captures bath-mediated acceleration of information spreading. In both of these cases, we show that the derived Lieb-Robinson bounds are tight by also constructing lattice models saturating these bounds. Finally, we consider the problem of approximating a non-Markovian model by a larger Markovian model, which contains the system spins together with only a finite number of bosonic environment modes. We establish that as a consequence of our Lieb-Robinson bounds, the number of environment modes per system site needed to accurately capture local observables is independent of the size of the system.



I
Introduction

A lattice of quantum spins that only interact with each other locally are physically expected to have only a finite velocity at which correlations can propagate from one spin to another. This qualitative expectation is formalized in the form of Lieb-Robinson bounds
[
1
]
: Given two spatially local operators
A
and
B
, they typically upper bound the norm of the commutator
{[A(t),B]}
, where
A(t)
is the observable
A
in the Heisenberg picture at time
t
. These bounds were originally derived for quantum spin lattices with only Hamiltonian interactions, with the initial results requiring an assumption of strict spatial locality
[
2
,
3
]
. Subsequent results relaxed this requirement to exponentially decaying interactions
[
4
,
5
]
, and more recently, to polynomially decaying interactions
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
]
.
Lieb-Robinson bounds for lattice Hamiltonians have found an immense number of applications in establishing quasi-locality properties of dynamics
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
and characterizing ground state properties for gapped Hamiltonians
[
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
]
, as well as in developing classical and quantum algorithms for simulation of lattice Hamiltonians
[
23
,
24
]
.

An important question in the study of quantum many-body systems was then to generalize Lieb-Robinson bounds to open quantum systems, where the system evolution cannot be described by a Hamiltonian. Within the Markovian approximation, the open quantum dynamics of a lattice of quantum spins can often be described by a spatially local Lindbladian
[
25
]
. Lieb Robinson bounds for such models have also been extensively studied — in particular, they have been developed for strictly local Lindbladians
[
26
,
14
,
27
,
28
]
, as well as Lindbladians with exponentially and polynomially decaying interactions
[
29
]
. These bounds have subsequently lent rigorous insights into many-body physics of open quantum systems.
In particular, they have been used to study the stability of Lindbladian fixed points
[
30
,
31
,
32
]
, and developing protocols for simulating open quantum lattice models on both digital quantum computers
[
28
,
27
]
and analog quantum simulators
[
33
]
.

However, the existence and nature of Lieb-Robinson bounds for open quantum systems, beyond the Markovian regime, remain important open questions. A key challenge in developing Lieb-Robinson bounds for non-Markovian open quantum systems is the difficulty in describing the reduced system dynamics.
In the Markovian limit, the environment can be traced out to yield a time-local Lindblad master equation for the reduced state of the system.
In the non-Markovian setting, the environment state stores the history of the system, which can in turn influence the system’s later evolution
[
34
,
35
,
36
]
.

Importantly, the environment, which is generally taken to be large, can essentially have an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space and the system-environment interaction Hamiltonian can be unbounded (as for example when the system is coupled to a bosonic field). This creates a significant challenge in obtaining a Lieb-Robinson bound. Indeed, it has been recognized for quite some time that lattice models with infinite local Hilbert space dimension need not even have a Lieb-Robinson bound.
For example, it is possible to construct Hamiltonian lattice models with unbounded local Hilbert space dimension that permit supersonic propagation of correlations
[
37
]
. In some cases, a Lieb-Robinson bound can be derived despite this issue — in particular, for models of spins interacting with a lattice of linearly coupled bosonic modes
[
38
]
, as well as for lattice of interacting bosonic modes
[
39
,
40
,
41
,
42
,
43
]
.

In this paper, we study Lieb-Robinson bounds for non-Markovian open quantum systems. The only assumption we make on the environment is that it, in the absence of interaction with the system, is non-interacting and can thus be described by a free quantum field.
We show that if the environment is locally independent and has finite memory (a notion that we formalize), then the system obeys a Lieb-Robinson bound with a linear light cone. By contrast, if the environment itself has a finite propagation speed for excitations, the system may exhibit supersonic transport.
In both cases, we prove that the channel on the system generated by the non-Markovian dynamics is quasi-local: over finite times, it maps local operators to approximately local, or quasi-local, operators.

We next revisit the problem of Markovian dilations of Gaussian non-Markovian environments. For numerical simulations of non-Markovian models, as well as for theoretically characterizing the “amount” of memory that the non-Markovian environment retains of the system, a commonly followed approach is to attempt to approximate the non-Markovian environment by a finite number of bosonic modes
[
44
,
45
,
46
]
. A standard approach to constructing these bosonic modes is the star-to-chain transformation
[
45
]
— this transformation has been extensively studied from a theoretical standpoint, and it has been shown that it can well approximate a wide variety of non-Markovian environments
[
47
,
48
,
49
,
50
]
. However, in current analyses of this transformation the number of environment modes per system site needed to approximate the state of a many-body open quantum system scales polynomially with the total system size. For many-body systems that are geometrically local, it can be physically expected that to well approximate only local observables, the number of environment modes per system site needed should be independent of the system size and depend instead only on the evolution time since the local observable dynamics is not expected to depend on the entire many-body system. As an application of the Lieb Robinson bounds that we develop in this paper, we make this qualitative expectation precise.

Figure 1:
Schematic depiction of the non-Markovian many-body model considered in this paper. The many-body system is defined on a
d-
dimensional lattice,
\Lambda
, and interacts locally with a non-Markovian bath via the system operators
R_{\alpha}
and memory kernels
K_{\alpha,\beta}
.

II
Setup: Open system with memory

We consider a many-body system of
n
qudits arranged on a
d-
dimensional lattice
\Lambda
(Fig.
1
). These qudits interact with each other locally either through a system
Hamiltonian or through interaction mediated by an environment. We will model the system-environment dynamics via the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H(t)=H_{\text{S}}(t)+H_{\text{SE}}(t),

(1)

where
H_{\text{S}}(t)
describes the dynamics of the system in the absence of the environment and
H_{\text{SE}}(t)
describes the system-environment interaction.
We will assume that
H(t)
is already written in the interaction picture with respect to the free environment Hamiltonian.

The system Hamiltonian
H_{S}(t)
will be assumed to be geometrically local, i.e.,

H_{\text{S}}(t)=\sum_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(t),

where each
h_{\alpha}(t)
is a geometrically local operator supported in the region
{S}_{\alpha}\subseteq\Lambda
. The system-environment interaction Hamiltonian
H_{\text{SE}}(t)
will be assumed to be of the form

\displaystyle H_{\text{SE}}(t)=\sum_{\alpha}R_{\alpha}(t)B_{\alpha}(t),

(2)

where
R_{\alpha}(t)
are geometrically local system operators supported on
regions
{S}_{\alpha}
, taken in the Schrödinger picture, while

B_{\alpha}(t)
are environment operators in the interaction picture with
respect to the free environment evolution. Throughout this paper, we will restrict ourselves to Gaussian and bosonic environments
[
51
,
52
]
, although our conclusions would also straightforwardly apply to Gaussian fermionic enviroments. More specifically,
the operators
B_{\alpha}(t)
will be assumed to be continuous or discrete linear superpositions of the environment’s canonical annihilation and creation operators and can correspond to different quadratures of different linear combinations of modes in the Bosonic environment.
For the system-operators appearing in the Hamiltonian,
h_{\alpha}(t),R_{\alpha}(t)
, we will only assume that they are bounded, i.e.,
\left\|{h_{\alpha}(t)}\right\|,\left\|{R_{\alpha}(t)}\right\|\leq 1
, and do not have infinite time-variation, i.e.,
\left\|{h^{\prime}_{\alpha}(t)}\right\|,\|{R_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t)}\|<\infty
. Our analysis will not hinge on any further assumptions on the system-environment interaction: For instance, in contrast to previous works that have addressed Lieb-Robinson bounds in lattice Bose-Hubbard models, we will not require the system-environment interaction to be excitation number conserving (i.e., we allow for non-rotating terms) or have any other specific structue or conservation-laws
.

Assuming the environment to
be stationary
and to initially (at
t=0
) be in a Gaussian state
\rho_{E}
, the dynamics of the system can be described entirely in terms of the two-point correlation functions

\displaystyle K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(t-t^{\prime})=\text{Tr}_{{E}}(B_{\alpha}(t)B_{\alpha^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\rho_{E}).

(3a)

We note that if
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(\tau)\sim\delta(\tau)
, the reduced system dynamics is Markovian. As a generalization to the non-Markovian setting, we will assume that

\displaystyle K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(\tau)=K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime};c}(\tau)+\sum_{j}k_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}[j]\delta(\tau-T_{j}),

(3b)

where
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},c}
are continuous functions and
k_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},j}
are complex constants.
These classes of memory kernels captures almost all physically relevant models studied in quantum optics and solid-state physics.
For instance, an Ohmic bath (with a high-frequency cut-off) is a case with
a continuous
{K}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
, and no
\delta-
function contributions
[
53
]
. As another example, models in quantum optics such as lossy Jaynes Cummings models, where the environment can be described by a finite number of lossy bosonic environment modes, admit a similar description
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
. A bath which has reflections (for example, an atom coupled to a half-cavity with the mirror at the end) and time-delayed feedback can be described by
\delta-
function contributions in Eq. (
3b
), with the times
T_{j}
capturing the corresponding sharp delay times
[
57
,
58
,
59
,
60
,
61
]
.

In addition to being physically relevant, the dynamics resulting from the model in Eq. (
1
) can be rigorously defined despite the
\delta-
function divergences in the kernels
{K}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}

[
48
,
62
]
.
This makes Eq. (
1
) a suitable starting point for developing a Lieb-Robinson bound for non-Markovian open quantum systems.

Finally, we remark that we assume that the environment itself is stationary (or time-independent), which is why the memory kernel in Eq. (
22
) can be assumed to depend only on the time-differences. However, we will allow the system Hamiltonian as well as the system operators
R_{\alpha}(t)
coupling to the environment to be time dependent. Furthermore, much of the framework in this paper can be straightforwardly extended to non stationary environments i.e., when the memory kernel in Eq. (
22
) depends individually on the two time instants and not just on the time difference.

III
Lieb Robinson bound

Consider a bounded local system obervable
O_{X}
supported on a subregion
X
of the lattice
\Lambda
. We will consider the Heisenberg picture evolution of
O_{X}
from time
t^{\prime}
to time
t>t^{\prime}
under two Hamiltonians: (i) the full system-environment Hamiltonian
H(t)
[Eq. (
1
)], and (ii) the Hamiltonian
H_{X[l]}(t)
obtained by restricting
H(t)
to just the terms acting within a distance
l
of
X
:

H_{X[l]}=\sum_{\alpha:{S}_{\alpha}\cap X[l]\neq\emptyset}\bigg(h_{\alpha}(t)+B_{\alpha}(t)R_{\alpha}(t)\bigg),

where
X[l]
is the set of sites that are within distance
l
from
X

1
1

1

Here the distance is the graph (Manhattan) distance on the unit-spaced lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
.
In particular, we define

\displaystyle\,(\textrm{i})\ \,O_{X}(t,t^{\prime})=U^{\dagger}(t,t^{\prime})O_{X}U(t,t^{\prime}),

\displaystyle(\textrm{ii})\ O_{X}(t,t^{\prime};l)=U_{X[l]}^{\dagger}(t,t^{\prime})O_{X}U_{X[l]}(t,t^{\prime}),

where
U_{X[l]}(t_{f},t_{i})=\mathcal{T}\exp(-i\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{f}}H_{X[l]}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime})
. We will characterize the deviation
O_{X}(t,t^{\prime})-O_{X}(t,t^{\prime};l)
between these two Heisenberg picture evolutions. If we only considered the system Hamiltonian without any system-environment interaction (i.e.,
R_{\alpha}(t)=0
), then the usual Lieb-Robinson bounds for geometrically local lattice Hamiltonians would yield that
\left\|{O_{X}(t,t^{\prime})-O_{X}(t,t^{\prime};l)}\right\|\leq e^{O(v_{\text{LR}}t-l)}
for some system-size independent constant
v_{\text{LR}}

[
4
]
. Physically, this can be understood from the local observable’s support spreading at a constant velocity
v_{\text{LR}}
and thus its evolution being well approximated by the Hamiltonian restricted to its vicinity.

However, as mentioned above, the system-environment Hamiltonian is unbounded — the interaction terms in the Hamiltonian can be arbitrarily large
depending on the system-environment state
.
We therefore do not expect a similar bound to hold in general on the operator norm
\left\|{O_{X}(t,t^{\prime})-O_{X}(t,t^{\prime};l)}\right\|
, which is taken on the full system–environment Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E}
. We instead consider the following expectation value of

O_{X}(t,t^{\prime})-O_{X}(t,t^{\prime};l)
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)=\left|\mathrm{Tr}_{SE}\!\left[\bigl(O_{X}(t,t^{\prime})-O_{X}(t,t^{\prime};l)\bigr)\rho(t^{\prime})\right]\right|,

(4)

where
\rho(t^{\prime})
denotes the system–environment state obtained by evolving
the initial product state
\sigma_{S}\otimes\rho_{E}
for a time
t^{\prime}
under the
full system–environment Hamiltonian,

\displaystyle\rho(t^{\prime})=U(t^{\prime},0)\bigl(\sigma_{S}\otimes\rho_{E}\bigr)U^{\dagger}(t^{\prime},0),

(5)

with
\sigma_{S}
an arbitrary initial system state and

\displaystyle U(t_{f},t_{i})=\mathcal{T}\exp\!\left[-i\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{f}}H(s)\,ds\right]

(6)

the unitary propagator generated by the Hamiltonian
H(t)
defined in
Eq. (
1
). Note that even though
\rho_{E}
is a Gaussian environment state,
\rho(t^{\prime})
need not be a Gaussian in the environment.

III.1
Independent baths

The first setting that we will consider is the case where the environment is composed of a set of independent baths that locally interact with the system (Fig.
2
). More specifically, the environment Hilbert space will be a tensor product of
N
independent baths labeled by
i\in\{1,2,\dots N\}
. Each bath itself can be rather general: In the simplest case, it can be either one or a few bosonic modes or it could be a continuum of bosonic modes i.e., a bosonic quantum field. The
i^{\text{th}}
bath will interact only with system sites in a geometrically local region
S_{i}
and the system-environment interaction Hamiltonian will be re-written as:

\displaystyle H_{\text{SE}}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\nu=1}^{L}R_{i}^{(\nu)}(t)B_{i}^{(\nu)}(t),

(7)

where
R_{i}^{(\nu)}(t)
is a system-operator supported in
S_{i}
and
B_{i}^{(\nu)}(t)
, for
\nu\in\{1,2\dots L\}
, is a bath operator in the
i^{\text{th}}
bath. Note that this is a special case of the model in Eq. (
2
) with the index
\alpha
being expressed as
\alpha=(i,\nu)
and setting
B_{\alpha}(t)=B_{i}^{(\nu)}(t),R_{\alpha}(t)=R_{i}^{(\nu)}(t)
. Again, for different
\nu
, the bath operators
B_{i}^{(\nu)}(t)
can correspond to different superpositions of annihilation and creation operators of the
i^{\text{th}}
bath, capturing the system interacting with different quadratures and modes of the
i^{\text{th}}
bath. Finally, we also assume that initially the environmental state factorizes as
\rho_{E}=\otimes_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{E,i}
, where
\rho_{E,i}
is the initial state of the
i^{\text{th}}
bath. Under these assumptions, the memory kernels introduced in Eq. (
3a
) can be rewritten as

K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(t-t^{\prime})=\delta_{i,j}K_{i,\nu,\nu^{\prime}}(t-t^{\prime}),

(8a)

where
\alpha=(i,\nu),\beta=(j,\nu^{\prime})
and

K_{i,\nu,\nu^{\prime}}(t-t^{\prime})=\mathrm{Tr}_{{E,i}}(B_{i}^{{(\nu)}}(t)B_{i}^{{(\nu^{\prime})}}(t^{\prime})\rho_{E,i}).

(8b)

While the independent bath model would appear to make a somewhat restrictive assumption on the environment, they are nevertheless an interesting starting point for understanding many-body non-Markovian systems. First, we remark that taking the Markovian limit of this model (i.e.,
K_{i,\nu,\nu^{\prime}}(\tau)\to\delta(\tau)
) yields a Lindblad master equation with geometrically local jump operators which have also been previously studied in the context of deriving Lieb Robinson bounds
[
26
]
. The independent-bath model can thus be considered to be a natural non-Markovian generalization of geometrically local Lindbladians.

Furthermore, these models also arise in several physically relevant settings: For instance, quantum-optical systems describing arrays of atomic or solid-state quantum emitters interacting with free or structured electromagnetic-field
[
64
,
65
]
can be described with the independent bath model when the emitter separations are larger than the dominant wavelength of the electromagnetic field. When the atomic or solid-state systems additionally interact directly with each (e.g., with Rydberg interactions as described in Refs.
[
66
,
67
]
), we obtain a many-body system described by the independent bath model. These models are also commonly used for describing site-local phonon-exciton transport
[
68
]
as well as for spin-boson systems with uncorrelated bosonic baths at each site
[
69
]
.

Figure 2:
Schematic depiction of an environment with independent baths. Each local bath
i
can itself be highly multi-mode (or even a continuum of modes) and can support multiple bath operators
B_{i}^{(\nu)}(t)
, for
\nu\in\{1,2\dots L\}
, that couple locally to the system, while distinct baths have vanishing cross-correlators.

Lieb-Robinson bound
. We now provide a Lieb-Robinson bound for such models—the Lieb Robinson velocity will depend on both the supports
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
of the operators
h_{\alpha}(t),R_{\alpha}(t)
, as well as the memory kernels
K_{i}^{(\nu,\nu^{\prime})}(t)
. We define
a_{0}
(the support diameter) and
\mathcal{Z}
(the support coordination number) as positive constants satisfying

\text{diam}(\mathcal{S}_{\alpha})\leq a_{0},

i.e.,
a_{0}
is an upper bound on the diameters of the regions
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
and

\left|{\{\alpha^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset\}}\right|\leq\mathcal{Z},

i.e., the number of sub-regions
S_{\alpha^{\prime}}
that intersect with any one sub-region
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
is upper bounded by
\mathcal{Z}
. The existence of such constants
a_{0},\mathcal{Z}
which are also system-size independent is direct consequence of the geometrical locality of the system-operators
h_{\alpha}(t),R_{\alpha}(t)
. Furthermore, for
\tau>0
, we will also define
m(\tau)
via

\displaystyle m(\tau)=\max_{i,\nu,\nu^{\prime}}\bigg(\int_{-\tau}^{\tau}|{K_{i,\nu,\nu^{\prime};c}(s)}|ds+\sum_{j:T_{j}\in[-\tau,\tau]}|{k_{i,\nu,\nu^{\prime}}[j]}|\bigg),

(9)

where
K_{i,\nu,\nu^{\prime};c}(s)
and
k_{i,\nu,\nu^{\prime}}[j]
are the continuous and
\delta
-function parts of the memory kernel
K_{i,\nu,\nu^{\prime}}(s)
as per Eq. (
3b
).
m(\tau)
captures how the
1-
norm of the memory kernels grows with the time-difference
\tau
and can be viewed as a measure of the integrated memory of the environment. As we show below,
m(t)
is a key quantity governing the Lieb-Robinson velocity in these models.

Proposition 1

(Lieb Robinson bounds for independent bath model)
.

Given a local observable
O_{X}
supported in
X\subseteq\Lambda
with
\left|{X}\right|\leq O(1)
and times
t>t^{\prime}>0
, for all initial states
\sigma_{S}
of the system qudits,
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)
defined in Eq. (
4
) satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|f(l)\exp(-l/a_{0})(\exp(v_{\textnormal{LR}}(t)\left|{t-t^{\prime}}\right|/a_{0})-1),

where, for large
l
,
f(l)\leq O(l^{d-1})
, and

v_{\textnormal{LR}}(t)=ea_{0}\mathcal{Z}L+14L^{2}ea_{0}\mathcal{Z}m(t).

Recall that
L
denotes the number of local channels and is independent of both the total system size and the evolution time.

The proof of this proposition departs significantly from the derivation of a Lieb-Robinson bound for finite-dimensional systems due to the unbounded system-environment model. Although Lieb-Robinson bounds for systems with unbounded Hamiltonians have been previously derived, most significantly for Bose Hubbard models
[
42
,
43
]
, their proof strategy has been to carefully derive bounds on local excitation numbers and their moments, and then use these bounds to approximate the model by an effective finite-dimensional model. However, such a strategy cannot directly be applied to the models that we consider in this paper—the underlying reason is that in our setting each local site may be coupled to a continuous bosonic field, and the couplings are possibly
\delta-
function divergent. Thus, even within the single excitation subspace of the environment, the system-environment Hamiltonian is still unbounded. If we were to only consider models without the
\delta
-function divergences in its memory kernel and with the continuous parts being sufficiently rapidly decaying, there could be an alternative strategy to deriving Lieb-Robinson bounds by discretizing the bosonic field and truncating the particle number. However, even then, this approach would yield an overestimated Lieb-Robinson velocity that would depend on the
2-
norm of the memory kernels as opposed to its
1-
norm. As we show below with an explicit model, the dependence of the Lieb-Robinson velocity on the
1-
norm of the memory kernel, as provided in Proposition
1
, is tight and can be saturated.

Our key insight to deal with the unbounded and divergent system-environment interaction, which we detail in the supplement, is to use the Gaussian nature of the environment. Intuitively, upon performing a Taylor expansion of the evolution operators, the unbounded environment terms can be contracted with one another via Wick’s theorem and thereby replaced by
c
-numbers for “suitable” initial states. More mathematically, we first identify spaces of “suitable” system-environment states that contain states of the form

\rho_{S}\otimes\rho_{E}
for every system state
\rho_{S}
and each fixed Gaussian state
\rho_{E}
satisfying our assumptions, and we show that this “suitable” space remains closed under evolution by the system-environment dynamics as well as on application of the environment operators
B_{\alpha}(t)
themselves.
Having identified and restricted ourselves to these state spaces, we then proceed with an analysis similar to that of finite-dimensional lattice models with bounded interactions.

Figure 3:
(a)Schematic depicting a time-dependent 1D model with supersonic transport that violates a Lieb-Robinson bound with a linear light cone.
The model is constructed by interleaving three different time-dependent system-environment Hamiltonians — for
0\leq t\leq T
, the bath modes are continuously driven by a constant field and accumulate
T^{2}
particles on average. Then, from
T\leq t\leq T+1
, the first qubit in the system is excited and from
T+1\leq t\leq 2T+1
, the bath oscillators, which have
T^{2}
particles on average in each, are used to mediate a transport of this excitation at a velocity
\sim T
. (b)Numerical simulations of the above-mentioned protocol for different values of
T
. The heat map shows the final occupation probability distribution of excitations. The wave front is observed to propagate as
T^{2}
, implying a propagation velocity proportional to
T
.

The explicit Lieb–Robinson velocity
v_{\text{LR}}(t)
that we obtain in Proposition
1
increases with
a_{0}
,
\mathcal{Z}
, and
L
, which is expected since larger values of these parameters increase the effective strength and range of interactions in the model. The Lieb–Robinson velocity additionally depends on
m(t)
, the
1
-norm measure of the memory kernels defined in Eq. (
9
), which in turn depends on the initial environment state. The quantity
m(t)
characterizes the cumulative strength of the bath correlations over the evolution interval. The dependence of
v_{\text{LR}}
on
m(t)
is physically expected
since, if, due to the environment, the system state can be
correlated with its past, then this can effectively increase the
strength of the local interactions by a factor proportional
to
m(t)
.

Finally, we remark that the estimate of the Lieb-Robinson velocity can be improved if it is apriori known that some of the local baths only couple to a single site i.e., have coupling operators
R_{i}^{(\nu)}(t)
that act on the same single site (for all
\nu
) and thus cannot cause transport through the system. Formally, this can be easily seen by going into the interaction picture with respect to these system-environment terms and repeating our analysis. As a consequence, in such cases, we can re-define
m(t)
to exclude the baths which couple only to a single site.

Supersonic transport
. Proposition
1
suggests that for models where
\mu(t)
continues to grow without bound, the model can exhibit supersonic transport, i.e., in time
t
, the support of a local observable can grow to a distance
\propto t^{\alpha}
for
\alpha>1
in the long-time limit. We now illustrate such supersonic transport explicitly in a time-dependent system-environment model and show that in time
t
, the model can transport excitations to a distance
\propto t\mu(t)
, thus asymptotically saturating the Lieb-Robinson bound
2
2

2

We remark that our construction differs from that of Ref.
[
37
]
, which also developed a lattice of infinite-dimensional systems with supersonic transport, in that we explicitly consider models that have a Hamiltonian of the form in Eq. (
1
) while the construction of Ref.
[
37
]
required more complex nearest neighbour interactions and did not satisfy the template of Eq. (
1
)
.

We consider a one-dimensional chain of
N
qubits as the system and a chain of
N-1
harmonic oscillators as the environment. Each pair of qubits couples to an independent harmonic oscillator: In particular, the
i^{\text{th}}
bosonic mode interacts with qubits
i
and
i+1
during the protocol [Fig.
3
(a)].

We assume that the initial joint state is
\ket{0}_{S}^{\otimes N}\otimes\ket{0}_{E}^{\otimes(N-1)}
and we take the environment operators
B_{i}^{{(\nu)}}(t)
in Eq. (
8
) to be given by
B_{i}^{(1)}(t)=a_{i}
and
B_{i}^{(2)}{(t)}=a{{}_{i}}^{\dagger}
. With this choice, we obtain that

{K_{i,\nu,\nu^{\prime}}(t)}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }\nu=1,\nu^{\prime}=2\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Therefore, for this model, the
1-
norm measure defined in Eq. (
9
) is given by
m(t)=t
, and consequently Proposition
1
would suggest that
v_{\text{LR}}(t)\sim t
, i.e., this model should be able to exhibit transport across distances
\sim t^{2}
in time
t
.

To explicitly design a protocol that exhibits supersonic transport in this model, we
consider a total evolution time of
2T+1
where
T
is an integer.

The protocol is divided into three stages [Fig.
3
(b)]:

(1)

In the first stage, which occurs during the time-interval
[0,T]
, we use the system for time
T
to pump each environment mode into a coherent state with amplitude
T
. This is achieved by starting all the system qubits in the state
\ket{0}
, and applying a system-environment Hamiltonian
H_{\text{SE}}=i\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\sigma_{j}^{z}(a_{j}^{\dagger}-a_{j})
.

(2)

In the second stage, which occurs during the time-interval
[T,T+1]
, we switch off the system-environment interaction and drive the first system qubit to
\ket{1}
. This is accomplished with the system Hamiltonian
H_{\text{S}}=\pi\sigma_{1}^{x}/2
.

(3)

In the last stage, corresponding to the time-interval
[T+1,2T+1]
, we use the excited environment modes to mediate a fast transport of the excitation in the system via environment-assistend particle exchange. Concretely, this is accomplished by using the system-environment Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{\text{SE}}(t)=\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}g_{j}(t{-}(T+1))h_{j,j+1},

(10a)

where

\displaystyle h_{j,j+1}=\ket{0_{j}1_{j+1}}\!\bra{1_{j}0_{j+1}}a_{j}+\text{h.c.},

(10b)

and

\displaystyle g_{j}(t)=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }t\in[(j-1)/T,j/T],\\
0&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

(10c)

i.e., we first switch on an interaction between the first environment mode, and the first two qubits for a total time
\Delta t=1/T
, then between the second environment mode and the next two qubits and so on. When the
j^{\text{th}}
interaction is active, it removes one photon from bath mode
j
while implementing an approximate excitation transfer from qubit
j
to qubit
j+1
.

Since the environment was initially in the vacuum state and all the qubits in
\ket{0}
, at the end of the first stage (i.e., at time
T
), the system-environment state is
\ket{0}^{\otimes N}\otimes\ket{\alpha=T}^{\otimes(N-1)}
, where
\ket{\alpha}
is a coherent state with amplitude
\alpha
. In the second-stage, we simply excite the first qubit while keeping the environment modes unchanged, yielding a system-environment state
\sigma_{1}^{\dagger}\ket{0}^{\otimes N}\otimes\ket{\alpha=T}^{\otimes(N-1)}
with
\sigma^{\dagger}=\ket{1}\bra{0}
.

In the last stage, this excitation is propagated through the chain of system qubits. The velocity at which the excitation is propagated can be estimated by approximating the environment modes classically: Since the environment modes are in a coherent state with amplitude
T
, this amounts to setting
a_{j}\to T

h_{j,j+1}\approx\frac{\pi T}{2}(\ket{0_{j}1_{j+1}}\!\bra{1_{j}0_{j+1}}+\text{h.c.})

Thus, in time
1/T
,
h_{j,j+1}
can map
\ket{1_{j}0_{j+1}}\to\ket{0_{j}1_{j+1}}
i.e., transfer the excitation from the
j^{\text{th}}
qubit to the
(j+1)^{\text{th}}
qubit. This yields a velocity of the excitation that grows as
T
, and in time
T
, the excitation can be transported to a distance of
T^{2}
thus exhibiting
supersonic transport
. This is numerically exhibited in Fig.
3
(c) where the expected excitation number
\langle\sigma_{i}^{\dagger}\sigma_{i}\rangle
is shown as a function of the site
i
at the end of the protocol for different choices of
T
. We see that the excitation is indeed propagated to a distance of
T^{2}
: However, we observe that the transfer of excitation is not perfect i.e., the qubits at
i<T^{2}
are not exactly in
\ket{0}
at the end of the protocol. This can be attributed to an imperfect environment-mediated excitation transfer by
h_{j,j+1}
due to quantum fluctuations in the environment mode around its classical approximation. In the Supplement Sec.
XII.1
, we rigorously analyze the impact of these fluctuations and show that they can be neglected in the limit of large
T
.

We remark that all the time-dependent terms in the system-environment Hamiltonian can be switched on and off smoothly to ensure that they are differentiable with respect to time as required in our setup [see text below Eq. (
2
)]—this is addressed in the Supplement Sec.
XII.1
. In that section, we also explicitly consider the observable
O=\sigma_{T^{2}+1}^{\dagger}\sigma_{T^{2}+1}
, which measures the number of excitations at the
(T^{2}+1)^{\text{th}}
qubit, and show that
\Delta_{O}(2T+1,0;l)
[as defined in Eq. (
4
)], is a non-zero constant if
l<T^{2}
, thus exceeding the conventional linear light cone and saturating the Lieb-Robinson bound provided in Proposition 1.

III.2
Coupled memory

Figure 4:
Schematic depiction of an environment with coupled Bath operators. In this setting, bath operators such as
B_{\alpha}(t)
and
B_{\beta}(t)
are embedded in a shared environment and are associated with positions
\text{x}_{\alpha}
and
\text{x}_{\beta}
. Excitations in the shared environment can propagate with velocity
v_{E}
, producing correlations between different system regions.

We next consider the case in which the environment does not have independent baths and can, due to its own dynamics, propagate excitations even in the absence of coupling to the system. More specifically, as depicted in Figure
4
, we associate the environment operators
B_{\alpha}(t)
[defined in Eq. (
2
)] which interact with system sites in
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
with a position
\text{x}_{\alpha}
in the environment. We will assume the distance between two such positions
\text{x}_{\alpha},\text{x}_{\beta}
scales with the lattice distance between with regions
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha},\mathcal{S}_{\beta}
i.e., we will assume that there are system-size independent constants
p_{\pm},q_{\pm}>0
such that

\displaystyle d(\mathcal{S}_{\alpha},\mathcal{S}_{\beta})\leq p_{+}d(\text{x}_{\alpha},\text{x}_{\beta})+q_{+}\text{ and},

(11a)

\displaystyle d(\text{x}_{\alpha},\text{x}_{\beta})\leq p_{-}d(\mathcal{S}_{\alpha},\mathcal{S}_{\beta})+q_{-}.

(11b)

Unlike the independent bath model previously considered, we allow the kernels
K_{\alpha,\beta}(\tau)\neq 0
for generic
\alpha,\beta
.
However, we assume that the free environment dynamics itself has a finite propagation velocity: This is formalized by assuming that the kernels
K_{\alpha,\beta}
satisfy the bound

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\left|{K_{\alpha,\beta}(s)}\right|\,ds\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad K_{0}\min\left\{\exp\left(\frac{v_{E}|\tau|-d(\text{x}_{\alpha},\text{x}_{\beta})}{a_{E}}\right),1\right\},

(12)

where
a_{E},K_{0}>0
. In this model, the kernels have a bounded one-norm i.e.,
\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|{K_{\alpha,\beta}(\tau)}\right|d\tau\leq K_{0}<\infty
. Furthermore, over time
\tau
, the environment can only significantly propagate excitations over a distance
v_{E}\tau
, and the memory kernel
K_{\alpha,\beta}(\tau)
exhibit at-least an exponential decay if
d(\text{x}_{\alpha},\text{x}_{\beta})\gg v_{E}\tau
. We remark that
v_{E},a_{E}
and
K_{0}
may depend on the initial environment state
\rho_{E}
.

This environment model captures physical setting where, for instance, the environment is a bosonic field and is interacting with the system at different spatial points. Physical examples of such models could be an array of closely spaced atoms coupling to a free or structured electromagnetic field without making the approximation that the photons emitted by one of the atoms is not absorbed by a different atom.

Our next result provides a Lieb-Robinson bound for this model. Surprisingly, the propagation in the environment itself can dramatically modify the nature of the light-cone in the system, which is captured in the following Proposition.

Proposition 2
.

Given a local observable
O_{X}
supported in
X\subseteq\Lambda
with
\left|{X}\right|\leq O(1)
and with the memory-kernels
K_{\alpha,\beta}(\tau)
satisfying Eq. (
12
), then for any fixed
0<\kappa_{E}<1/a_{E}
and all initial states
\sigma_{S}
of the system qudits,
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)
defined in Eq. (
4
) satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)\leq

(13)

\displaystyle\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|f_{c}(t,l)\exp(\kappa_{E}(v_{\textnormal{LR,c}}(t)(t-t^{\prime})-l)/p_{+}),

where
p_{+}
is the constant defined in Eq. (
11
),
f_{c}(t,l)\leq\mathcal{O}(t^{3d}l^{d})
,
v_{\textnormal{LR,c}}(t)\leq{O}(t^{d})
as
t,l\to\infty
.

The proof of this proposition follows a similar strategy of identifying relevant system-environment states as in the proof of proposition
1
. However, unlike proposition 1, the environment is not composed of locally independent baths and its coupled structure has to be taken into account: In the Supplement Sec.
XI
, we show how to handle this additional complication and derive a Lieb-Robinson bound. We remark that Proposition 2 provides a super-linear upper envelope, i.e., in
d-
dimensions, a local observable’s support can grow to a distance
l\sim t^{d+1}
instead of
\sim t
as would be predicted by Proposition 1 for memory kernels with a bounded 1-norm. Physically, this is a consequence of the long-range interactions between the system qudits mediated by the environment. More specifically, since the environment propagates excitation at a velocity
v_{E}
, at time
t
excitations at any spatial location in the environment can in principle coherently accumulate from a region of size
t^{d}
. This can then possibly enhance the effective environment-mediated interaction between the system qudits by at most a factor of
t^{d}
, giving rise to a time-dependent Lieb-Robinson velocity
v_{\textnormal{LR,c}}\sim t^{d}
. In the Supplement Sec.
XII.2
, we make this argument precise and construct a
model that explicitly saturates a Lieb-Robinson bound,
with exponential tails outside the superlinear light-cone,
with a Lieb-Robinson velocity scaling as
t^{d}
.

IV
Implication on bath approximations

For a many-body system, if we are interested in approximating the full system state at a given time and to a target precision, then the number of modes in the environment is expected to scale with the system size since we effectively demand a high precision on high-order correlators in the system. However, for a geometrically local many-body system interacting locally with independent baths, it is physically expected that local observables or few-point correlation functions in the system can be approximated well by a number of modes in each local bath that is independent of the system size, and depends only on the evolution time and the target precision.

Figure 5:
Schematic depiction of the star-to-chain transformation used to approximate an independent non-Markovian environment by a discrete set of bosonic modes. Each bath is replaced with
N_{m}
modes, where
N_{m}
controls the accuracy of this approximation, and these modes themselves 1D nearest-neighbour coupled lattice.

This physical expectation can be made precise by applying the Lieb Robinson bounds from Proposition 1. We will analyze the star-to-chain transformation as the method to construct the discrete-mode approximation of each independent bath
[
45
,
49
,
48
]
.
As depicted in Fig.
5
, each bath is approximated by a 1D chain of
N_{m}
discrete bosonic modes with nearest neighbour coupling, where
N_{m}
controls the accuracy of the approximation. For simplicity, we consider the case of
L=2
, while the generalization is straightforward
[
71
,
72
,
73
]
. Recall that
L
defined above Eq. (
2
) is the number of terms that act on the same subregion. Now, the system-environment Hamiltonian in Eq. (
2
) can be written as

H_{\text{SE}}(t)=\sum_{i}\left(A_{i}^{\dagger}(t)R_{i}(t)+\text{h.c.}\right),

(14)

Equation (
14
) corresponds to
B_{i}^{(-)}(t)=A_{i}(t)
and
B_{i}^{(+)}(t)=A_{i}^{\dagger}(t)
in Eq. (
2
).
Denoting the annihilation operators of the discrete bosonic modes in the star-to-chain transformation by
b_{i,1},b_{i,2}\dots b_{i,N_{m}}
, Eq. (
14
) is approximated instead by a Hamiltonian of the form

\displaystyle{H}_{\text{SE}}^{N_{m}}(t)=\sum_{i}\left[g_{i}(t)b_{{i},1}^{\dagger}(t)R_{i}(t)+g_{i}(t)b_{i,1}(t)R_{i}^{\dagger}(t)\right],

where
b_{i,1}(t)=e^{iH_{i,E}t}b_{i,1}e^{-iH_{i,E}t}
with

H_{i,E}=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{m}}\omega_{i,j}b_{i,j}^{\dagger}b_{i,j}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{m}-1}\big(t_{i,j}b_{i,j}^{\dagger}b_{i,j+1}+\text{h.c.}\big).

The constants
g_{i},\omega_{i,j},t_{i,j}
can be chosen to best approximate the memory kernels of
H_{\text{SE}}(t)
with those of
H^{N_{m}}_{\text{SE}}(t)
. We review the standard procedure to compute these coefficients from the kernels
K_{i}^{(\nu,\nu^{\prime})}(t)
(Eq.
8
) in the Supplement Sec.
XIII
.

Employing the Lieb-Robinson bounds from Proposition 1, we now estimate the number of modes
N_{m}
required to approximate local system observables using the star-to-chain transformation. The following proposition states our key result, and establishes that
N_{m}
can be chosen to be uniform in the system size and depend only on the evolution time and the precision demanded in the local observable.

Proposition 3
.

There exists an approximation of the non-Markovian environment with
N_{m}
discrete bosonic modes per bath such that every local system observable
O
with support that has an
O(1)
diameter and
\left\|{O}\right\|\leq 1
can be approximated to precision
\varepsilon
at time
t
with

\displaystyle N_{m}

\displaystyle=\Theta\big(\big(\varepsilon^{-1}{t^{2d+3}}\big)^{1+o(1)}\big)+\Theta\big(\big({\varepsilon}^{-1}\log\varepsilon^{-1}\big)^{1+o(1)}\big)+

\displaystyle\qquad\Theta\big(\big(t\kappa_{0}\big(\varepsilon^{-1}\Theta(t^{d+1})\Theta(t\log^{d}\varepsilon^{-1})\big)^{1+o(1)}\big),

where
\kappa_{0}:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
is a function that satisfies

\frac{1}{x}=\sum_{\tau\in\{0,t\}}\int_{\tau-\kappa_{0}(x)}^{\tau+\kappa_{0}(x)}\textnormal{K}_{c}(s)ds,

with
\textnormal{K}_{c}
the upper bound on
K_{i,c}^{(\nu,\nu^{\prime})}
for all
i
.

The proof of this proposition follows almost directly from Proposition 1 — the evolution of the local observable up to time
t
depends on a neighbourhood of diameter
O(t)
around it. Consequently, it is only affected by the error in the discrete-mode approximation of the baths interacting with this neighbourhood — we estimate this error by following previous analyses of the star-to-chain transformation
[
49
,
48
]
. The details of our analyses can be found in the supplementary material. While we have chosen to analyze the star-to-chain transformation, we expect a similar conclusion to hold for other methods for approximating the bath, such as the pseudo-mode approximation, which could outperform the star-to-chain transformation for specific systems
[
44
,
46
]
.

V
Conclusion

Our work provides a Lieb-Robinson bound for non-Markovian many-body systems, which fundamentally provides a velocity of propagation of information in these systems. Similar to the Markovian case, we expect this bound to be important in theoretical studies of both non-equilibrium as well equilibrium properties of non-Markovian many-body systems.
For example, our bound implies robustness of local observables against perturbations to the memory kernels by leveraging techniques in
[
74
]
, with applications in simulating such systems
[
75
]
.

Our work opens up several interesting questions pertaining to non-Markovian many-body physics, and provides techincal tools that could possibly be developed further to answering these questions. Since we are considering non-Markovian models, in comparison to the Markovian case, there is a possibility of creating interactions between the qubits, via the environment, that are correlated in time and not just in space. The first open question is to more sharply characterize propagation of information in non-Markovian models where the kernels decay slowly and algebraically and thus have an unbounded time-integrated one-norm (e.g., if the kernels
\sim t^{-\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
). These models are time-local in the sense that the memory kernels go to 0 at large time differences, albeit slowly. Our analysis does not conclusively support a linear light cone in these models, but instead suggests a polynomial one. In the same spirit, and motivated by results in many-body Hamiltonians and Lindbladians, building upon the tools developed in this paper we could investigate the light-cone structure of models with both algebraically decaying spatial interactions which are also non-Markovian. We expect these analyses to allow us to elucidate the many-body physics that can be induced by the rich spatio-temporal correlation that may exist in non-Markovian many-body models and reveal how they differ from the more well-understood Markovian case.

Supplementary Information

VI
Notation

Given a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, we will denote by
\text{L}(\mathcal{H})
the set of bounded linear operators from
\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
, define
\text{M}(\mathcal{H})
to be the set of the bounded Hermitian operators from
\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
and define
\text{D}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
as the set of valid density matrices on
\mathcal{H}
. Given a set of (not necessarily commuting) operators
O_{1},O_{2}\dots O_{n}
, we will use the product notation

\prod_{i=1}^{n}O_{i}=O_{1}O_{2}\dots O_{n}\text{ and }\prod_{i=n}^{1}O_{i}=O_{n}O_{n-1}\dots O_{1}.

We will typically use the
\dagger
superscript to indicate the adjoint, or Hermitian conjugate, of an operator or superoperator.
Specifically, the adjoint of a superoperator
\mathcal{L}
is defined as
\mathrm{Tr}(A\mathcal{L}(B))=\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}(A)B)
for
A,B\in\mathrm{L}(\mathcal{H})
.
However, in some cases, a more compact expression can be obtained by using the following notation — for some operator or superoperator
X
, we define
X^{(-)}:=X
and
X^{(+)}:=X^{\dagger}
. Furthermore we will use
\bar{+}=-
and
\bar{-}=+
. For example,
X^{(\bar{u})}:=X^{\dagger}
for
u=-
. While dealing with mixed states and their dynamics, it will often be convenient to adopt the vectorized notation, where we map operators on a (finite-dimensional) Hilbert space to state via
A=\sum_{i_{l},i_{r}}A_{i_{l},i_{r}}\ket{i_{l}}\!\bra{i_{r}}\to|{A}\rrangle=\sum_{i_{l},i_{r}}A_{i_{l},i_{r}}\ket{i_{l},i_{r}}
.
Superoperators, such as Lindbladians- or channels, will be mapped to ordinary operators in this picture. Given an operator
X\in\text{M}(\mathcal{H})
, we will define
X_{l},X_{r}\in\text{M}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H})
by
X_{l}|{\rho}\rrangle=(X\otimes I)|{\rho}\rrangle=|{X\rho}\rrangle
and
X_{r}|{\rho}\rrangle=(I\otimes X^{\text{T}})|{\rho}\rrangle=|{\rho X}\rrangle
.
X_{l}(X_{r})
can also be interpreted as a superoperator which left (right) multiplies its argument with
X
i.e.
X_{l}(Y)=XY
and
X_{r}(Y)=YX
. Furthermore, given an operator
X\in\text{L}(\mathcal{H})
, we will denote by
\mathcal{C}_{X}=X_{l}-X_{r}
as the commutator superoperator corresponding to
X
i.e.
\mathcal{C}_{X}|{\phi}\rrangle=|{[X,\phi]}\rrangle
.

Note also that inner products between vectorized operators are equivalent to Hilbert-Schmidth inner products between the unvectorized operators:
\llangle{A}|B\rrangle=\text{Tr}(A^{\dagger}B)
. Also note that
\text{Tr}(A)=\text{Tr}(I^{\dagger}A)=\llangle{I}|A\rrangle
. Similarly, if we consider the tensor product of two Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E}
and suppose
O\in\text{L}(\mathcal{H}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E})
, then we obtain the following notation for partial trace
\text{Tr}_{E}(O)=\llangle{I_{E}}|O\rrangle
. Finally, while we will use vectorized notation throughout this paper, all the norms, even when applied in the vectorized notation, will correspond to unvectorized norms. For e.g.
\left\|{|{\sigma}\rrangle}\right\|_{1}
will be correspond to
\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
i.e. the
1-
norm of
\sigma
as an operator and not to the
1-
norm of
\sigma
as a vector.
For finite-dimensional operators,
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{p}
denotes the Schatten
p
-norm when its argument is an operator; for superoperators,
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{\diamond}
denotes the diamond norm.

For a set
\mathcal{S}
, we will denote by
\Theta_{\mathcal{S}}
the indicator function on
\mathcal{S}
i.e.
\Theta_{\mathcal{S}}(s)=1
if
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
0
otherwise. We will denote by
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
the space of functions
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\left|{f}\right|^{p}
is integrable. For a function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
,
\left\|{f}\right\|_{p}
(when defined) will denote the
L^{p}
norm — in particular,

\left\|{f}\right\|_{p}=\bigg(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left|{f(x)}\right|^{p}dx\bigg)^{1/p},

and
\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}
is the smallest non-negative number such that
f(x)\leq\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}
almost everywhere. Given a function
f
and an interval
I
, we will define its norm restricted to an interval
I
via

\|{f_{I}}\|_{p}=\|{f\cdot\Theta_{I}}\|_{p}\quad\text{where}\quad\Theta_{I}(t)=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }t\in I,\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

For a vector of measurable functions
\bm{f}(t)=(f_{1}(t),f_{2}(t),\dots,f_{n}(t))
, we will define

\left\|{\bm{f}(t)}\right\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{\alpha}\left|{f_{\alpha}(t)}\right|,\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|{\bm{f}_{I}}\right\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{\alpha}\left\|{(f_{\alpha})_{I}}\right\|_{\infty},

where
f|_{I}
is the restriction of
f
to the interval
I
i.e.,
f|_{I}(t)=f(t)
if
t\in I
and
0
otherwise. We will also adopt the standard notation and denote by
\text{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
the space of continuously differentiable complex-valued functions on
\mathbb{R}
.

We will also define
\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
to be the space of tempered distributions
K
which are of the form

\displaystyle K(t)=K_{c}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{M}k[j]\delta(t-\tau_{j}),

(15)

where
K_{c}\in\text{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
is a continuous function and
\delta(\cdot)
is the Dirac-delta function. The function
K_{c}
will often be called the continuous part of
K
and
K-K_{c}=\sum_{j=1}^{M}k[j]\delta(t-\tau_{j})
will be called the atomic part of
K
. Identifying
K
with the complex measure
d\mu_{K}(t)=K_{c}(t)dt+\sum_{j=1}^{M}k[j]\delta_{\tau_{j}}(dt)
, we use the standard probability-theory definition
\mathrm{TV}(K;I):=|\mu_{K}|(I)
. For the representation in Eq. (
15
), this gives

\text{TV}(K;I)=\int_{I}\left|{K_{c}(t)}\right|dt+{\sum_{j:\,\tau_{j}\in I}\left|{k[j]}\right|.}

The total variation on the full line is

\text{TV}(K)=\text{TV}(K;\mathbb{R})=\left\|{K_{c}}\right\|_{1}+\sum_{j=1}^{M}\left|{k[j]}\right|.

Given two functions (or distributions),
f
and
g
, we will denote by
f\star g
the convolution of
f
and
g
formally defined via

(f\star g)(\tau)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(\tau^{\prime})g(\tau-\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}.

Summation convention
. We will often omit writing summations in more tedious calculations — in any equation or inequality, any index (superscript or subscript) on the right-hand side which does not appear on the left hand side will be assumed to be summed over. Any index (superscript or subscript) that appears on both left-hand and right-hand side of an equation will
not
be summed over. For e.g.

\displaystyle F=x_{i}y_{i}\text{ is shorthand for }F=\sum_{i}x_{i}y_{i},

\displaystyle F=x_{i}\text{ is shorthand for }F=\sum_{i}x_{i},

\displaystyle F_{k}=x_{i}^{k}y^{j}\text{ is shortand for }F_{k}=\bigg(\sum_{i}x_{i}^{k}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{j}y_{j}\bigg),\text{ and }

\displaystyle F_{k}=x_{i}^{k}y_{j}z^{j}_{k}\text{ is shortand for }F_{k}=\bigg(\sum_{i}x_{i}^{k}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{j}y_{j}z^{j}_{k}\bigg).

Note that the summation convention that we use is close to the Einstein’s summation convention with the difference being that unrepeated indices are also summed over as long as they do not appear on the left-hand side of an equation or inequality.

VII
Preliminaries

This section reviews the setup that we analyze (section
VII.1
), as well as some preliminaries that will be important in our calculations. Section
VII.2
reviews the Wick’s theorem and defines some notation to streamline its application — the notation introduced in this subsection will be used throughout the supplement. As introduced in the main text, in this supplement, we also consider system-environment models where the memory kernels can have
\delta
-function divergences — in section
VII.3
, we show that we can instead approximate the
\delta
function as a limit of smooth functions (i.e., mollifiers) while computing physically relevant quantities (such as the dynamics of system observables). This can be seen as effectively defining the dynamics associated with models whose kernels have
\delta
-function divergences, and is an important simplification for the following section. If the reader is willing to accept that this is possible, then they can skip section
VII.3
and directly go to next section for the calculation of the Lieb-Robinson bound.

VII.1
Setup

As introduced in the main text, we will assume that the system qudits are arranged on a
d-
dimensional lattice
\Lambda
. The qudits interact with each other either directly or through a bath. We will denote the system Hilbert space by
\mathcal{H}_{S}
and the environment Hilbert space by
\mathcal{H}_{E}
. The system-environment Hamiltonian introduced in the main text, expressed directly in the interaction picture with respect to the environment, is given by

\displaystyle H(t)=\sum_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(t)+\sum_{\alpha}B_{\alpha}(t)R_{\alpha}(t),

(16)

where
h_{\alpha}(t),R_{\alpha}(t)
are geometrically local operators supported on
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
and
B_{\alpha}(t)
are operators on the environment that are linear in its annihilation and creation operators. We also introduce the quantities,
\mathcal{Z}
and
a_{0}
, that are determined by the supports
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
via

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}=\sup_{S_{\alpha}}\left|{\{\mathcal{S}_{\alpha^{\prime}}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\neq\emptyset\}}\right|\text{ and }a_{0}=\sup_{S_{\alpha}}\text{diam}(\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}).

(17)

For geometrically local models both
a_{0},\mathcal{Z}
will be independent of the system size. The constant
a_{0}
is simply an upper bound on the diameters of the supports
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
. The constant
\mathcal{Z}
can be considered to be the coordination number of the interaction graph, i.e., it is an upper bound on the number of sets
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
that intersect with any one set
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
. We will also assume that
\left\|{h_{\alpha}(t)}\right\|,\left\|{R^{\nu}_{\alpha}(t)}\right\|\leq 1
and that
h_{\alpha}(t),R_{\alpha}(t)
are differentiable with bounded derivatives, i.e.,
\|{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t)}\|,\|{{R_{\alpha}}^{\prime}(t)}\|<\infty
.

We will often need to restrict this Hamiltonian to sub-regions of the lattice
\Lambda
. We will denote the full set of values of the index
\alpha
in Eq.
16
by
\mathcal{A}
. Given
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, we define

\displaystyle H_{\mathcal{B}}(t)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}\bigg(h_{\alpha}(t)+B_{\alpha}(t)R_{\alpha}(t)\bigg).

(18)

Note that
H_{\mathcal{A}}(t)=H(t)
. We will also define the unitary
U_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)
by

\displaystyle U_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)=\mathcal{T}\exp\bigg(-i\int_{s}^{t}H_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}\bigg).

(19)

We will also define
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)
to be the vectorized unitary corresponding to
U_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(t)
as the commutator corresponding to
H_{\mathcal{B}}(t)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)=U_{\mathcal{B},l}(t,s)U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{B},r}(t,s)\text{ and }\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(t)=H_{\mathcal{B},l}(t)-H_{\mathcal{B},r}(t)=\sum_{\sigma\in\{l,r\}}(-1)^{\sigma}H_{\mathcal{B},\sigma}(s),

(20a)

where, throughout this paper, we will use the convention

\displaystyle(-1)^{\sigma}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }\sigma=l,\\
-1&\text{ if }\sigma=r.\end{cases}

(20b)

Note also that

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)=\mathcal{T}\exp\bigg(-i\int_{s}^{t}\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}\bigg).

(20c)

Finally, for simplicity, we will use the notation
U(t,s)=U_{\mathcal{A}}(t,s),\mathcal{U}(t,s)=\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}}(t,s)
and
\mathcal{H}(t)=\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}(t)
. Given
X\subseteq\Lambda
, we will define
\mathcal{A}_{X}
by

\mathcal{A}_{X}=\{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X\neq\emptyset\}.

Note that
H_{\mathcal{A}_{X}}
will then be the sum of local terms whose support intersects with
X
.
Given a subregion
X\subseteq\Lambda
, we will also implicitly use

H_{X}(t)=H_{\mathcal{A}_{X}}(t),\mathcal{H}_{X}(t)=\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{X}}(t),U_{X}(t,s)=U_{\mathcal{A}_{X}}(t,s)\text{ and }\mathcal{U}_{X}(t,s)=\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{X}}(t,s).

We will consider the initial environment state,
\rho_{E}
, to be Gaussian. It can thus be described by its first and second moments of the annihilation and creation operators. In particular, we assume that

\displaystyle\text{Tr}(B_{\alpha}(t)B_{\alpha^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\rho_{E})=K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(t-t^{\prime})\text{ where }K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R}).

(21)

While in general a Gaussian state can also have a displacement, without loss of generality, we will assume that
\text{Tr}(B_{\alpha}(t)\rho_{E})=0
since a displacement can be equivalently incorporated as a Hamiltonian term acting only on the system using the Mollow transform
[
76
,
51
]
. It will be convenient to define the kernel
{K}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma}(t-t^{\prime})
, where
\sigma\in\{l,r\}
via

\displaystyle\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{\alpha,\sigma}(t)B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle={K}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(t-t^{\prime}),

(22)

Importantly, we emphasize that due to the cyclic property of the trace,
\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{\alpha,\sigma}(t)B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle
is independent of
\sigma\in\{l,r\}
. The kernels
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}
are related to the kernels
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
defined above via

K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma}(\tau)=\begin{cases}K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(\tau)&\text{ if }\sigma=l,\\
K_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}(-\tau)&\text{ if }\sigma=r.\end{cases}

Furthermore, from this relation, it also follows that
{K}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
for all
\sigma\in\{r,l\},\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
, i.e.,
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma}
has the representation

K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma}(\tau)=K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma;c}(\tau)+\sum_{j=1}^{M}k_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma}[j]\delta(\tau-T_{j}),

(23)

for some
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma;c}
and
k_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma}[j]
.

VII.2
Wick’s theorem

We begin by introducing two operator spaces,
\mathcal{S}(\rho_{E})
and
\mathcal{Q}(\rho_{E})
which depend on the initial environment state
\rho_{E}
, that we will frequently encounter in our analysis. The operator space
\mathcal{S}(\rho_{E})
, formally defined below, can be considered to be the set of operators generated by starting from an operator in which the environment is in
\rho_{E}
, applying a sequence of time-evolution under the restricted system-environment Hamiltonians
H_{\mathcal{B}}(t)
followed by modifying the state of the system (and not of the environment) by application of a linear super-operator.

Definition 1

(Operator space
\mathcal{S}(\rho_{E})
)
.

An operator
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\rho_{E})\subseteq\textnormal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E})
if
\exists\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\}_{i\in[1:n]},\{(s_{i},t_{i}):s_{i},t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\}_{[i:n]}
, system superoperators
\{\Omega_{i}:\left\|{\Omega_{i}}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq 1\}_{i\in[1:n]}
and system operator
\sigma_{S}
with
\left\|{\sigma_{S}}\right\|_{1}\leq 1
such that,

|{\phi}\rrangle=\prod_{i=n}^{1}\Omega_{i}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})|{\sigma_{S},\rho_{E}}\rrangle.

Furthermore,
(\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in[1:n]},\{(s_{i},t_{i})\}_{i\in[1:n]},\{\Omega_{i}\}_{i\in[1:n]},\sigma_{S})
will be called a
representation
of the operator
\phi
and
\{(s_{i},t_{i})\}_{i\in[1:n]}
will be called the
time edges
of the representation of
\phi
.

The operator space
\mathcal{Q}(\rho_{E})
can be considered to be the space dual to the operator space
\mathcal{S}(\rho_{E})
: Here, we start with an operator which is identity
I_{E}
on the environment and then apply a sequence of Heisenberg picture time-evolutions with respect to the restricted system-environment Hamiltonians
H_{\mathcal{B}}(t)
followed by a modification of the state of the system by application of a linear super-operator.

Definition 2

(Operator space
\mathcal{Q}(\rho_{E})
)
.

An operator
\theta\in\mathcal{Q}({\rho_{E}})
if
\exists\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\}_{i\in[1:n]},\{(s_{i},t_{i}):s_{i},t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\}_{i\in[1:n]}
, system superoperators
\{\Omega_{i}:\left\|{\Omega_{i}}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq 1\}_{i\in[1:n]}
and system operator
O_{S}
with
\left\|{O_{S}}\right\|\leq 1
such that

\llangle{\theta}|=\llangle{O_{S},I_{E}}|\prod_{i=n}^{1}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})\Omega_{i}.

Furthermore,
(\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in[1:n]},\{(s_{i},t_{i})\}_{i\in[1:n]},\{\Omega_{i}\}_{i\in[1:n]},O_{S})
will be called a
representation
of the state
\theta
and
\{(s_{i},t_{i})\}_{i\in[1:n]}
will be called the
time edges
of the representation of
\theta
.

In all the analysis in this as well as the following sections, we will repeatedly use Wick’s theorem which we briefly review below. We will directly formulate all the analysis in the vectorized picture. A particularly useful way of formulating the Wick’s theorem is to break it down into
Wick contractions
. For two super-operators
B_{1},B_{2}
linear in the environment annihilation and creation super-operators (henceforth referred to as “linear environment superoperators”), define the Wick contraction

and

are linear environment superoperators, then we define the wick contraction of the product

with

as

{}{}{

Note that the Wick contraction of the product
B_{1}B_{2}\dots B_{n}
with
B
is still an environment superoperator, but is “one degree” lower i.e. it is a sum of products of
n-1
linear environment superoperators.

We extend this definition by linearity: Suppose
C
is a super-operator such that

\displaystyle C=\sum_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}B_{1,\alpha}B_{2,\alpha}\dots B_{k_{\alpha},\alpha},

(24)

where
S_{\alpha}
is a system superoperator,
B_{i,\alpha}
are linear environment superoperators i.e.,
C
is a sum of products of linear environment superoperators. Then, if
B
is another linear ennvironment superoperator, we define

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.28647pt}{-2.66666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -8.7 -3.69)} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{{

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}

{}{}{

\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\vbox to13.00002pt{}{})B_{2}B_{4}|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle+\llangle{I_{E}}|(\hbox to12.57pt{\vbox to8.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.28647pt\lower-4.16666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.28647pt}{-2.66666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -8.7 -3.69)} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hbox to12.57pt{\vbox to8.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.28647pt\lower-4.16666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.28647pt}{-2.66666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -8.7 -3.69)} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 9.92 L 0 14.3 L 0 14.3 L 0 9.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\vbox to13.00002pt{}{})B_{2}B_{3}|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle,

\displaystyle=\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{1}B_{2}|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{3}B_{4}|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle+\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{1}B_{3}|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{2}B_{4}|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle+\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{1}B_{4}|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{2}B_{3}|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle.

In the proofs below, we will often need to analyze expressions of the form
\llangle{\theta}|B_{1}B_{2}\dots B_{n}|{\phi}\rrangle
, where
\theta\in\mathcal{Q}(\rho_{E}),\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\rho_{E})
and
B_{1},B_{2}\dots B_{n}
are linear environment superoperators. As a simple example of handling such expressions, consider the case where
|{\phi}\rrangle=\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle
,
\llangle{\phi}|=\llangle{I_{E}}|
and we are interested in analyzing
\llangle{\theta}|B|{\phi}\rrangle
. To apply the Wick’s theorem to this expression, we proceed by a first-order Trotterization of
\mathcal{U}_{B}(t,s)
followed by the application of the Wick’s theorem:

\displaystyle\llangle{\theta}|B|{\phi}\rrangle=\llangle{I_{E}}|B\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{=}}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\llangle{I_{E}}|B\prod_{j=T_{\varepsilon}-1}^{0}\mathcal{U}_{B}(s+(j+1)\varepsilon,s+j\varepsilon)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu2\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{=}}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\llangle{I_{E}}|B\prod_{j=T_{\varepsilon}-1}^{0}\left(\text{id}-\int_{s+j\varepsilon}^{s+(j+1)\varepsilon}i\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}\right)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu3\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{=}}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Theta_{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)(-1)^{\sigma}\sum_{j=0}^{T_{\varepsilon}-1}\prod_{j^{\prime}=j+1}^{T_{\varepsilon}-1}\llangle{I_{E}}|\left(\text{id}-\int_{s+j^{\prime}\varepsilon}^{s+(j^{\prime}+1)\varepsilon}i\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}\right)\bigg(-i\int_{s+j\varepsilon}^{s+(j+1)\varepsilon}R_{\alpha,\sigma}(s^{\prime})\llangle{I_{E}}|BB_{\alpha,\sigma}(s^{\prime})|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle ds^{\prime}\bigg)\times

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\prod_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-1}\left(\text{id}-\int_{s+j^{\prime}\varepsilon}^{s+(j^{\prime}+1)\varepsilon}i\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}\right)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu4\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{=}}-i\Theta_{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)(-1)^{\sigma}\int_{s}^{t}\llangle{I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,\tau)R_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(\tau,s)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle\llangle{I_{E}}|BB_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle d\tau.

(26)

where in (1), we defined
T_{\varepsilon}=(t-s)/\varepsilon
, in (2) we have made the approximation
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(\tau+\varepsilon,\tau)\approx\text{id}-i\int_{\tau}^{\tau+\varepsilon}\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}
, in (3) we have substituted
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime})=\Theta_{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)(-1)^{\sigma}R_{\alpha,\sigma}(s^{\prime})B_{\alpha,\sigma}(s^{\prime})
(note the use of the summation convention introduced in section
VII.1
) and then used the Wick’s theorem to contract
B
with every possible
B_{\alpha,\sigma}(s^{\prime})
in this expansion, and in (4), we have rewritten the product of infinitesimal evolution into a time-ordered one. It is convenient to rewrite the resulting expression as

\llangle{I_{E}}|B\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle=\llangle{I_{E}}|\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(B;\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle)

where we define the “right Wick contraction” of
B
that is linear in the environment annihilation and creation superoperators with the vectorized state
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle

\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(B;\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle))=-i\Theta_{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)(-1)^{\sigma}\int_{s}^{t}\llangle{I_{E}}|BB_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,\tau)R_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(\tau,s)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle.

The vectorized operator
\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(B;\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle)
can be interpreted as the operator obtained on wick contracting the superoperator
B
with every environment super-operator in an expansion of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)
. We now define the “right Wick contraction”
\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}
more generally: Suppose
|{\phi}\rrangle\in\mathcal{S}({\rho_{E}})
with representation
(\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in[1:n]},\{(s_{i},t_{i})\}_{[1:n]},\{\Omega_{i}\}_{[1:n]},\sigma_{S})
and
B
is a linear environment superoperator, then

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}(B;|{\phi}\rrangle)=-i(-1)^{\sigma}\Theta_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}(\alpha)\int_{s_{j}}^{t_{j}}\llangle{I_{E}}|BB_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle|{\phi_{j,\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)}\rrangle d\tau,

(27a)

where

|{\phi_{j,\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)}\rrangle\in\mathcal{S}(\rho_{E})
is given by

\displaystyle|{\phi_{j,\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)}\rrangle=\bigg(\prod_{i=n}^{j+1}\Omega_{i}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})\bigg)\Omega_{j}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}(t_{j},\tau)R_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}(\tau,s_{j})\bigg(\prod_{i=j-1}^{1}\Omega_{i}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})\bigg)|{\sigma_{S},\rho_{E}}\rrangle.

(27b)

Then, we define
\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}(\{B_{j}\}_{j\in[1:m]};|{\phi}\rrangle)
recursively via

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}(\{B_{k}\}_{k\in[1:m]};|{\phi}\rrangle)=-i(-1)^{\sigma}\Theta_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}(\alpha)\int_{s_{j}}^{t_{j}}\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{m}B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle{\overrightarrow{\text{W}}\big(\{B_{k}\}_{k\in[1:m-1]};|{\phi_{j,\alpha,\sigma}^{\nu}(\tau)}\rrangle\big)}d\tau.

(28)

Similarly, just as we defined the right Wick contraction in Eqs. (
27
) and (
28
), we can also define a “left Wick contraction”
\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}
that Wick contracts a linear environment super-operator with a vectorized operator in the dual space
\mathcal{Q}(\rho_{E})
. Suppose
\theta\in\mathcal{Q}({\rho_{E}})
with representation
(\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in[1:n]},\{(s_{i},t_{i})\}_{[1:n]},\{\Omega_{i}\}_{[1:n]},O_{S})
and
B
is a superoperator that is a linear combination of annihilation and creation operators, then we define

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\llangle{\theta}|;B)=-i(-1)^{\sigma}\Theta_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}(\alpha)\int_{s_{j}}^{t_{j}}\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)B|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle\llangle{\theta_{j,\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)}|d\tau,

(29)

where

\llangle{\theta_{j,\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)}|\in\mathcal{Q}(\rho_{E})
are given by

\llangle{\theta_{j,\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)}|=\llangle{O_{S},I_{E}}|\bigg(\prod_{i=n}^{j+1}\Omega_{i}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})\bigg)\Omega_{j}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}(t_{j},\tau)R_{j,\sigma}(\tau)\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}(\tau,s_{j})\bigg(\prod_{i=j-1}^{1}\Omega_{i}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})\bigg).

Then, we define
\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\llangle{\theta}|;\{B_{j}\}_{j\in[1:m]})
recursively via

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}\big(\llangle{\theta}|;\{B_{k}\}_{k\in[1:m]}\big)=-i\Theta_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}(\alpha)\int_{s_{j}}^{t_{j}}\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)B_{m}|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}\big(\llangle{\theta_{j,\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)}|;\{B_{k}\}_{k\in[1:m-1]}\big)d\tau.

(30)

With the right and left Wick contraction operations at hand, we can then state the Wick’s theorem as follows, which is a direct generalization of Eq. (
26
):

Lemma 1

(Wick’s theorem)
.

Suppose
|{\phi}\rrangle\in\mathcal{S}(\rho_{E})
,
\llangle{\theta}|\in\mathcal{Q}(\rho_{E})
and
B_{1},B_{2}\dots B_{n}
are superoperators that are linear combination of annihilation and creation operators, then

\llangle{\theta}|B_{1}B_{2}\dots B_{n}|{\phi}\rrangle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{I}_{l},\mathcal{I}_{r},\mathcal{I}\\
\mathcal{I}_{l}\cup\mathcal{I}_{r}\cup\mathcal{I}=[1:n]\\
\left|{\mathcal{I}}\right|\text{ is even}\end{subarray}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}\bigg(\prod_{(i,j)\in p}\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{i}B_{j}|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle\bigg)\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}\big(\llangle{\theta}|;\{B_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{I}_{l}}\big)\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}\big(\{B_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{I}_{r}};|{\phi}\rrangle\big),

(31)

where
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}
is the set of all pairings of the elements of
\mathcal{I}
, and each pairing
p\in\mathcal{P}_{I}
is expressed as a list of tuples
\{(i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2})\dots\}
where we assume
i_{n}<j_{n}
.

The following examples illustrate the application of lemma
1
and helps unpack the notation in the lemma:

\displaystyle\llangle{\theta}|B|{\phi}\rrangle=\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\llangle{\theta}|;B)|{\phi}\rrangle+\llangle{\theta}|\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(B;|{\phi}\rrangle),

\displaystyle\llangle{\theta}|B_{1}B_{2}|{\phi}\rrangle=\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}\big(\llangle{\theta}|;\{B_{1},B_{2}\}\big)|{\phi}\rrangle+\llangle{\theta}|\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}\big(\{B_{1},B_{2}\};|{\phi}\rrangle\big)+\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{1}B_{2}|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle\llangle{\theta}|\phi\rrangle+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\llangle{\theta}|;B_{1})\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(B_{2};|{\phi}\rrangle)+\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\llangle{\theta}|;B_{2})\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(B_{1};|{\phi}\rrangle).

VII.3
Analyzing the mollification

Throughout this section, we will assume that the kernels
{K}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
have locally bounded total variation i.e., within any finite interval, their total variation is finite. More, specifically, we make the following assumption.

Assumption 1

(Local integrability of the memory kernels)
.

\forall s,t\in\mathbb{R}
with
s\leq t
and
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma

\textnormal{TV}(K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma};[s,t])<\infty.

Remark
. We remark that we do not assume that
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma}
has a bounded total variation. Examples of kernels which are locally bounded but not bounded would include kernels of the form
1/(\left|{t}\right|+T_{0})^{\eta}
where
T_{0}>0
and
\eta<1
.

To define the regularized model, we will require a function
\eta:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
that is smooth, compactly supported in
[-1,1]
and is normalized such that
\left\|{\eta}\right\|_{1}=1
. An explicit example of such a function would be the standard mollifier

\displaystyle\eta(x)=\begin{cases}A_{0}e^{-1/(1-\left|{x}\right|^{2})}&\text{ if }|x|<1,\\
0&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

(32a)

where
A_{0}
is a constant chosen to satisfy
\left\|{\eta}\right\|_{1}=1
. We can then define a family of mollifiers
\eta_{\delta}
dependent on a (small) regularizing parameter
\delta>0
:

\displaystyle\eta_{\delta}(x)=\frac{1}{\delta}\eta\bigg(\frac{x}{\delta}\bigg),

(32b)

which, like
\eta(x)
, is again a smooth, non-negative function compactly supported in
[-\delta,\delta]
and with
\left\|{\eta_{\delta}}\right\|_{1}=1
. Given
\delta>0
, we first define a “mollified” system-environment Hamiltonian corresponding to Eq.
2
,
{H}^{\delta}(t)
, via

\displaystyle{H}^{\delta}(t)=\sum_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(t)+\sum_{\alpha}B_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t)R_{\alpha}(t),

(33a)

where

\displaystyle B_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\eta_{\delta}(t-s)B_{\alpha}(s)ds.

(33b)

Furthermore, this definition of
B_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t)
defines a set of kernels
K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}
, paralleling Eq.
22
. More specifically, we define
K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}
via

\displaystyle K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(t-t^{\prime})=\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\delta}(t)B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle.

(34a)

For notational convenience, we will often use
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta}:=K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta,\delta}
. Using Eq. (
33b
), we obtain that

\displaystyle K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(t-t^{\prime})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\eta_{\delta}(t-s)K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(s-s^{\prime})\eta_{\delta^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}-s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}ds=\big(\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}\star K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\big)(t-t^{\prime}),

(34b)

with
