\displaystyle\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(\tau)=(\eta_{\delta}\star\eta_{\delta^{\prime}})(\tau)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\eta_{\delta}(\tau+s^{\prime})\eta_{\delta^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}.

(34c)

It can be noted that
\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}
is a smooth, non-negative function that is compactly supported in the interval
[-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),\delta+\delta^{\prime}]
and
\left\|{\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}}\right\|_{1}=1
. The kernel
K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}
can therefore be considered to be the mollification of the kernel
K_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}
with mollifier
\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}
. The following lemma provides a useful property of the mollified kernel
K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 2
.

The kernel
K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}
is a smooth and locally bounded function, and satisfies

(a)

\forall\delta,\delta^{\prime}>0
,

|K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau)|\leq\min\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta},\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}\bigg)\left\|{\eta}\right\|_{\infty}\textnormal{TV}(\text{K}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}};[\tau-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),\tau+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})]),

(35)

(b)

\forall\delta,\delta^{\prime}>0
,

\textnormal{TV}\big(K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime};\sigma^{\prime}};[s,t]\big)\leq\textnormal{TV}(\text{K}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}};[s-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),t+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})]).

(36)

Proof.

If
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}
has a representation given by

\displaystyle K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime};\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau)=K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};c}(\tau)+\sum_{j}k_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};j}\delta(\tau-T_{j}),

then

\displaystyle K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};c}(\tau^{\prime})\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}+\sum_{j}k_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};j}\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(\tau-T_{j}),

\displaystyle=\int_{\tau-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})}^{\tau+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})}K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};c}(\tau^{\prime})\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}+\sum_{j:T_{j}\in[\tau-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),\tau+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})]}|{k_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};j}}|\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(\tau-T_{j}),

where we have used the fact that
\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}
is supported in
[-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),\delta+\delta^{\prime}]
.
It is immediately clear from this expression and the smoothness of
\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}
that
K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}
is a smooth function. Next, to upper bound
|{K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau)}|
, note that

\displaystyle|{K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau)}|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\tau-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})}^{\tau+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})}|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},c}(\tau^{\prime})}|{\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})}d\tau^{\prime}+\sum_{j}|{k_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};j}}|{\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(\tau-T_{j})},

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}}\right\|_{\infty}\bigg(\int_{\tau-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})}^{\tau+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})}|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},c}(\tau^{\prime})}|d\tau^{\prime}+\sum_{j:T_{j}\in[\tau-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),\tau+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})]}|{k_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};j}}|\bigg),

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}}\right\|_{\infty}\text{TV}(\text{K}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}};[\tau-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),\tau+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})]),

(37)

where, the reader can note that
u
is the function from Assumption
1
.
Next, we note that since
\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}=\eta_{\delta}\star\eta_{\delta^{\prime}}
,
\left\|{\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|{\eta_{\delta}}\right\|_{1}\left\|{\eta_{\delta^{\prime}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|{\eta}\right\|_{\infty}/\delta^{\prime}
. A similar bound holds on switching
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
and consequently, we obtain
\left\|{\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|{\eta}\right\|_{\infty}\min(1/\delta,1/\delta^{\prime})
. From this bound together with Eq.
37
, we obtain the bound on
\|{K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}}\|_{\infty}
in the lemma statement.

Proceeding similarly, we note that

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}|{K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau)}|d\tau=\int_{\tau=s}^{t}\int_{\tau^{\prime}=-\infty}^{\infty}|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};c}(\tau^{\prime})}|\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}d\tau+\sum_{j}|{k_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};j}}|\int_{\tau=s}^{t}\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(\tau-T_{j})d\tau,

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\tau=s}^{t}\int_{\tau^{\prime}=s-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})}^{t+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})}|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};c}(\tau^{\prime})}|\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}d\tau+\sum_{j:T_{j}\in[s-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),t+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})]}|{k_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};j}}|\int_{\tau=s}^{t}\eta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(\tau-T_{j})d\tau,

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{\tau^{\prime}=s-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})}^{t+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})}|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};c}(\tau^{\prime})}|d\tau^{\prime}+\sum_{j:T_{j}\in[s-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),t+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})]}|{k_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime};j}}|,

\displaystyle\qquad=\textnormal{TV}\big(K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}};[s-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),t+(\delta+\delta^{\prime})]\big),

(38)

which establishes the bound on
\text{TV}(K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}};[s,t])
in the lemma statement.
∎

Next, we expect the mollified kernel
K^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}
to approximate the unmollified kernel
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}
as a distribution. To make this expectation precise and to define a quantitative distance between two distributions, we would need to introduce a space of ‘test’ functions. For our purposes, as will become clear in our analysis below, the appropriate function space is the space of piecewise continous and differentiable functions, which we define below.

Definition 3

(Function space
\text{PC}^{1}_{t^{*}}(\mathbb{R})
)
.

Given
t^{*}=\{t_{1}^{*},t_{2}^{*}\dots t_{n}^{*}\}
, where
t_{1}^{*}<t_{2}^{*}\dots<t_{n}^{*}
, any
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}\in\text{PC}_{t^{*}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
has a representation

f(t)=\begin{cases}0&\text{for }t\in(-\infty,t_{1}^{*}),\\
f_{1}(t)&\text{for }t\in(t_{1}^{*},t_{2}^{*}),\\
f_{2}(t)&\text{for }t\in(t_{2}^{*},t_{3}^{*}),\\
\vdots\\
f_{n-1}(t)&\text{for }t\in(t_{n-1}^{*},t_{n}^{*}),\\
0&\text{for }t\in(t_{n}^{*},\infty),\end{cases}

for some
f_{1},f_{2}\dots f_{n-1}\in\textnormal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and at the points of discontinuity, the function
f
can assume any arbitrary value.

Consider a kernel
K\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
and a test function
f\in\text{PC}_{t^{*}}(\mathbb{R})
—due to the discontinuities in
f
and delta functions in
K
, there is some ambiguity in how to precisely apply
K
to
f
. More specifically, we need to choose a convention on how to integrate a delta function across a discontinuity. We make this convention concrete in the following definition.

Definition 4

(Applying
K\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
to
f\in\textnormal{PC}^{1}_{t^{*}}(\mathbb{R})
)
.

Suppose
K
has the decomposition
K=K_{c}+\sum_{j=1}^{M}k_{j}\delta(t-T_{j})
, then the action of
K
on
f
, which we will denote by
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K(\tau)f(\tau)d\tau
, is defined as

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K(\tau)f(\tau)d\tau=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K_{c}(\tau)f(\tau)d\tau+\sum_{j=1}^{M}\frac{k_{j}}{2}\big(f(T_{j}^{-})+f(T_{j}^{+})\big)

Importantly, we assume that, at a point of discontinuity of a test function, the action of a
\delta
-function is equivalent to averaging its left limit and right limit. While this choice is arbitrary, it is consistent with our goal of considering
\delta-
functions in a kernel as the limit of
symmetric
mollifiers and is also the convention that is commonly used to deal with such kernels in practice in the open quantum systems literature.

We next introduce a notion of how close two kernels
K,K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
are, or alternatively, a notion of how small the kernel
K-K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
is.

Definition 5
.

Given
t^{*}=\{t_{1}^{*},t_{2}^{*}\dots t_{M}^{*}\}
, a kernel
K\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
is
(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})-
small on the test function space
\textnormal{PC}_{t^{*}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
if
\forall f\in\textnormal{PC}_{t^{*}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
with
\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty},\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}<\infty
,

\left|{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K(t)f(t)dt}\right|\leq\lambda_{0}\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}+\lambda_{1}\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}.

Two kernels
K,K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
are
(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
-close on
\textnormal{PC}_{t^{*}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
if the kernel
K-K^{\prime}
is
(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})-
small on
\textnormal{PC}_{t^{*}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

Next, we establish that the kernels
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}
and
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}
are close in the sense of definition
5
.

Lemma 3
.

Suppose
K\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
is a kernel with continuous part
K_{c}
and
\delta
-functions at
\{T_{1},T_{2}\dots\}
and suppose
r_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth, non-negative even function that is compactly supported on
[-\delta,\delta]
and satisfies
\left\|{r_{\delta}}\right\|_{1}=1
. Consider the test function space
\textnormal{PC}_{t^{*}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and suppose that
\delta<\Delta\tau_{\textnormal{min}}/2
, where

\Delta\tau_{\textnormal{min}}=\min_{t,s\in\mathbb{T},t\neq s}\left|{t-s}\right|\text{ where }\mathbb{T}=\{T_{1},T_{2}\dots\}\cup\{t_{1}^{*},t_{2}^{*}\dots\},

i.e.,
\Delta\tau_{\textnormal{min}}
is the minimum non-zero spacing in the set
\mathbb{T}
formed by the locations of the
\delta-
functions in
K
and the points of discontinuity of
\textnormal{PC}_{t^{*}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
K
and
K\star r_{\delta}
are
(\lambda_{0}(\delta),\lambda_{1}(\delta))-
close, where

\lambda_{0}(\delta)=2\sum_{t\in t^{*}}\textnormal{TV}\big(K_{c};[t-\delta,t+\delta]\big)\text{ and }\lambda_{1}(\delta)=\delta\ \textnormal{TV}(K;[t_{1}^{*}-\delta,t_{n}^{*}+\delta]).

Proof.

For notational convenience, given
\xi>0
, we will define the set
\mathbb{T}_{\xi}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
via

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\xi}(t^{*})=[t_{1}^{*}-\xi,t_{1}^{*}+\xi]\cup[t_{2}^{*}-\xi,t_{2}^{*}+\xi]\cup\dots\cup[t_{n}^{*}-\xi,t_{n}^{*}+\xi].

(39)

Suppose
K
has a representation

K(\tau)=K_{c}(\tau)+\sum_{j=1}^{M}k_{j}\delta(\tau-T_{j}),

where
K_{c}
is a continuous function. We will also use the notation
K^{\delta}=K\star r_{\delta}
, which can then be expressed as

K^{\delta}(\tau)=K_{c}^{\delta}(\tau)+\sum_{j=1}^{M}k_{j}r_{\delta}(\tau-T_{j}).

For
f\in\textnormal{PC}_{t^{*}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left|{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K(\tau)f(\tau)d\tau-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K^{\delta}(\tau)f(\tau)d\tau}\right|,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\underbrace{\left|{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K_{c}(\tau)f(\tau)d\tau-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K^{\delta}_{c}(\tau)f(\tau)d\tau}\right|}_{\Delta_{c}[f]:\text{Error due to the continuous part}}+\underbrace{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left|{k_{j}}\right|\left|{\frac{1}{2}(f(T_{j}^{+})+f(T_{j}^{-}))-(f\star r_{\delta})(T_{j})}\right|}_{\Delta_{d}[f]:\text{Error due to the atomic part}},

where
K_{c}^{\delta}=K_{c}\star r_{\delta}
. We can separately analyze the errors due to the continuous part contribution,
\Delta_{c}[f]
, and the
\delta-
function contribution,
\Delta_{d}[f]
.

Error from the continuous part:
We begin by rewriting the expression for
\Delta_{c}[f]
as

\displaystyle\Delta_{c}[f]=\left|{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K_{c}(\tau){f}(\tau)d\tau-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K_{c}^{\delta}(\tau)f(\tau)d\tau}\right|\leq\int_{t_{1}^{*}-\delta}^{t_{n}^{*}+\delta}\left|{K_{c}(\tau)}\right|\left|{{f}(\tau)-(f\star r_{\delta})(\tau)}\right|d\tau,

(41)

where we have used that
f\in\text{PC}_{t^{*}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
is, by definition, supported in
[t_{1}^{*},t_{n}^{*}]
and hence
f\star r_{\delta}
is supported in
[t_{1}^{*}-\delta,t_{n}^{*}+\delta]
. This upper bound on
\Delta_{c}[f]
can be further upper bounded by analyzing the point-wise error
f(\tau)-(f\star r_{\delta})(\tau)
between
f
and its mollification
f\star r_{\delta}
. Consider first analyzing this error at
\tau
which are
\delta
-away from all points of discontinuity of
f
i.e. suppose
\tau\notin\mathbb{T}_{\delta}(t^{*})
where
\mathbb{T}_{\delta}(t^{*})=[t^{*}_{1}-\delta,t^{*}_{1}+\delta]\cup[t^{*}_{2}-\delta,t^{*}_{2}+\delta]\dots\cup[t^{*}_{n}-\delta,t^{*}_{n}+\delta]
, then

\displaystyle\left|{f(\tau)-(f\star r_{\delta})(\tau)}\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{=}}\left|{\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\big(f(\tau)-f(\tau+\tau^{\prime})\big)r_{\delta}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}}\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu2\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\left|{\tau^{\prime}}\right|r_{\delta}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}\leq\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\delta,

(42)

where, in (1), we have used the fact that
\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}r_{\delta}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}=1
to rewrite
f(\tau)=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}f(\tau)r_{\delta}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}
. In (2), we have used the fact that for
\tau\in\mathbb{T}_{\delta}(t^{*})
and
\tau^{\prime}\in[-\delta,\delta]
,
f(\tau+\tau^{\prime})
has no points of non-differentiability and thus
\left|{f(\tau)-f(\tau+\tau^{\prime})}\right|\leq\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\tau^{\prime}
. Then, starting from Eq.
41
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\Delta_{c}[f]

\displaystyle\leq\delta\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\int_{[t_{1}^{*}-\delta,t_{n}^{*}+\delta]\setminus\mathbb{T}_{\delta}(t^{*})}\left|{K_{c}(\tau)}\right|d\tau+\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\delta}(t^{*})}\left|{K_{c}(\tau)}\right|\left|{f(\tau)-(f\star r_{\delta})(\tau)}\right|d\tau,

\displaystyle\leq\delta\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\text{TV}(K_{c};[t_{1}^{*}-\delta,t_{n}^{*}+\delta])+\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\delta}(t^{*})}\left|{K_{c}(\tau)}\right|\big(\left|{f(\tau)}\right|+\left|{(f\star r_{\delta})(\tau)}\right|\big)d\tau,

\displaystyle\leq\delta\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\text{TV}(K_{c};[t_{1}^{*}-\delta,t_{n}^{*}+\delta])+2\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}\text{TV}(K_{c};\mathbb{T}_{\delta}(t^{*})).

(43)

Error from the atomic part:
Next, we consider the error
\Delta_{d}[f]=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left|{k_{j}}\right|\Delta_{d,j}[f]
where

\Delta_{d,j}[f]=\left|{\frac{1}{2}\big(f(T_{j}^{+})+f(T_{j}^{-})\big)-(f\star r_{\delta})(T_{j})}\right|.

We note that since
f
is supported in the interval
[t_{1}^{*},t_{n}^{*}]
, and thus
f\star r_{\delta}
is supported on
[t_{1}^{*}-\delta,t_{n}^{*}+\delta]
, it follows that

\Delta_{d,j}[f]=0\text{ if }T_{j}\notin[t_{1}^{*}-\delta,t_{n}^{*}+\delta].

Furthermore, since we assume that
\forall t\in\{T_{1},T_{2}\dots\},s\in\{t_{1}^{*},t_{2}^{*}\dots\}
with
t\neq s:\left|{t-s}\right|>2\delta
, there are only two possibilities—either
T_{j}\in t^{*}
, or
T_{j}\notin\mathbb{T}_{2\delta}(t^{*})
. Consider first the case where
T_{j}\notin\mathbb{T}_{2\delta}
—in this case,
f
is continuous and differentiable in
[T_{j}-\delta,T_{j}+\delta]
and thus

\Delta_{d,j}[f]=\left|{f(T_{j})-(f\star r_{\delta})(T_{j})}\right|\leq\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\delta,

where we have used Eq.
42
. Next, consider the case if
T_{j}\in t^{*}
, then

\displaystyle\Delta_{d,j}[f]

\displaystyle=\left|{\frac{1}{2}(f(T_{j}^{+})+f(T_{j}^{-}))-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}r_{\delta}(\tau)f(T_{j}-\tau)d\tau}\right|,

\displaystyle\leq\left|{\frac{1}{2}f(T_{j}^{+})-\int_{-\delta}^{0}r_{\delta}(\tau)f(T_{j}-\tau)d\tau}\right|+\left|{\frac{1}{2}f(T_{j}^{-})-\int_{0}^{\delta}r_{\delta}(\tau)f(T_{j}-\tau)d\tau}\right|,

\displaystyle\leq\left|{\int_{-\delta}^{0}r_{\delta}(\tau)(f(T_{j}^{+})-f(T_{j}-\tau))d\tau}\right|+\left|{\int_{0}^{\delta}r_{\delta}(\tau)(f(T_{j}^{-})-f(T_{j}-\tau))d\tau}\right|,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{s\in\{-,+\}}\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\left|{\tau}\right|\left|{r_{\delta}(\tau)}\right|d\tau\leq\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\delta,

where, in (1), we have used the fact that
f
is separately differentiable in both the intervals
(T_{j},T_{j}+\delta)
and
(T_{j}-\delta,T_{j})
. Thus, in both the cases,
T_{j}\in t^{*}
or
T_{j}\notin\mathbb{T}_{2\delta}(t^{*})
, we obtain that
\Delta_{d,j}[f]\leq\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\delta
. Therefore, we obtain that the error from the atomic part,
\Delta_{d}[f]
, can be upper bounded by

\displaystyle\Delta_{d}[f]\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left|{k_{j}}\right|\Delta_{d,j}[f]\leq\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\delta\sum_{j:T_{j}\in[t_{1}^{*}-\delta,t_{n}^{*}+\delta]}\left|{k_{j}}\right|.

(44)

Finally, combining Eqs.
VII.3
,
43
and
44
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left|{\int_{a}^{b}K(\tau)f(\tau)d\tau-\int_{a}^{b}K^{\delta}(\tau)f(\tau)d\tau}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\delta\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\bigg(\text{TV}(K_{c};[t_{1}^{*}-\delta,t_{n}^{*}+\delta])+\sum_{j:T_{j}\in[t_{1}^{*}-\delta,t_{n}^{*}+\delta]}\left|{k_{j}}\right|\bigg)+2\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}\text{TV}(K_{c},\mathbb{T}_{\delta}(t^{*})),

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\delta\left\|{f^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\text{TV}(K;[t_{1}^{*}-\delta,t_{n}^{*}+\delta])+2\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}\sum_{t\in t^{*}}\text{TV}(K_{c};[t-\delta,t+\delta]).

which proves the lemma statement.
∎

The previous lemma establishes that a kernel and its mollification are close to each other in a meaningful sense. Next, we use this fact to argue that the quantum dynamics of the un-mollified and mollified models are also close. As with the un-mollified model, given
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, we will define

{H}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(t)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}h_{\alpha}(t)+\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}B_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t)R_{\alpha}(t)\text{ and }{U}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)=\mathcal{T}\exp\bigg(-i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{H}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(\tau)d\tau\bigg).

The main result that we will establish is given by the following lemma.

Proposition 4
.

For any system operators
O_{S},\sigma_{S}
, system superoperators
\{\Omega_{i}:\left\|{\Omega_{i}}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq 1\}_{i\in[1:n]}
, lattice subregions
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in[1:n]}
as well as times
\{(s_{i},t_{i})\}_{i\in[1:n]}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\ \llangle{I_{E},O_{S}}|\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{U}}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})\Omega_{i}\bigg)|{\rho_{E},\sigma_{S}}\rrangle,

(45)

exists.

Proof sketch.

The proof of this proposition is a straightforward but tedious analysis: Here, for clarity and readability, we will simply sketch out the basic ideas in the proof in the simple case of proving that

\lim_{\delta\to 0}g(\delta)\text{ where }g(\delta)=\llangle{I_{E},O_{S}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(t,s)|{\rho_{E},\sigma_{S}}\rrangle,

exists and provide the full analysis in appendix
A
. To prove the existence of this limit, we will show that
g(\delta)
for any set of decreasing
\delta
forms a Cauchy sequence. In other words, we have to show that
\left|{g(\delta_{1})-g(\delta_{2})}\right|\to 0
as
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\to 0
. We begin by writing

\displaystyle\left|{g(\delta_{1})-g(\delta_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\int_{s}^{t}\llangle{I_{E},O_{S}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta_{1}}(t,\tau)\Delta B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)R_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta_{2}}(\tau,s)|{\rho_{E},\sigma_{S}}\rrangle}\right|\text{ where }\Delta B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)=B^{\delta_{1}}_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)-B^{\delta_{2}}_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau).

(46)

Next, we use the Wick’s theorem to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{g(\delta_{1})-g(\delta_{2})}\right|\leq\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}(\alpha)\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}(\alpha^{\prime})\bigg(

\displaystyle\qquad\left|{\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\tau}^{t}\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta_{1}}(\tau^{\prime})\Delta B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle\llangle{I_{E},O_{S}}|\mathcal{U}^{\delta_{1}}_{\mathcal{A}}(t,\tau^{\prime})R_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})\mathcal{U}^{\delta_{1}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tau^{\prime},\tau)R_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta_{2}}(\tau,s)|{\rho_{E},\sigma_{S}}\rrangle d\tau^{\prime}d\tau}\right|+

\displaystyle\qquad\left|{\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{\tau}\llangle{I_{E}}|\Delta B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta_{2}}(\tau^{\prime})|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle\llangle{I_{E},O_{S}}|\mathcal{U}^{\delta_{1}}_{\mathcal{A}}(t,\tau)R_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)\mathcal{U}^{\delta_{1}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})R_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta_{2}}(\tau^{\prime},s)|{\rho_{E},\sigma_{S}}\rrangle d\tau^{\prime}d\tau}\right|\bigg).

(47)

Consider now the kernels
\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta_{1}}(\tau^{\prime})\Delta B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle
and
\llangle{I_{E}}|\Delta B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)B^{\delta_{2}}_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle
— we can argue using lemma
3
that these are “small”. For instance,

\displaystyle\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta_{1}}(\tau^{\prime})\Delta B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle

\displaystyle=\llangle{I_{E}}|B^{\delta_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})B^{\delta_{1}}_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle-\llangle{I_{E}}|B^{\delta_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})B^{\delta_{2}}_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle,

\displaystyle=K^{\delta_{1},\delta_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)-K^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}(\tau^{\prime}-\tau),

(48)

and similarly

\displaystyle\llangle{I_{E}}|\Delta B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta_{2}}(\tau^{\prime})|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle=K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})-K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta_{2},\delta_{2}}(\tau-\tau^{\prime}).

(49)

Now, for a given a test-function space
\text{PC}_{t^{*}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, it follows from lemma
3
, it then follows that for sufficiently small
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
, both
K_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{1}}
and
K_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}
are close to
K_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}
when applied to a test function. Using the triangle inequality together with Eqs. (
48
) and (
49
), it can then be deduced that
\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta_{1}}(\tau^{\prime})\Delta B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle
and
\llangle{I_{E}}|\Delta B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta_{2}}(\tau^{\prime})|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle
are close to 0 when applied on this test function space. Since the terms in Eq. (
47
) can be reformulated as applying the kernels
\llangle{I_{E}}|B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta_{1}}(\tau^{\prime})\Delta B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle
and
\llangle{I_{E}}|\Delta B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta_{2}}(\tau^{\prime})|{\rho_{E}}\rrangle
on a piecewise continuous function (see appendix
A
for more details), we then obtain that
\left|{g(\delta_{1})-g(\delta_{2})}\right|
is small and in particular goes to
0
as
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\to 0
.

∎

Formally, this lemma allows us to “define” expressions of the form of Eq.
45
but for the original unregularized model i.e. as a matter of definition, we will set

\displaystyle\llangle{I_{E},O_{S}}|\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})\bigg)|{\rho_{E},\sigma_{S}}\rrangle:=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\ \llangle{I_{E},O_{S}}|\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{U}}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})\bigg)|{\rho_{E},\sigma_{S}}\rrangle.

(50)

We point out that, from a more mathematical standpoint, we might be interested in defining the unitary group
U(t,s)=\mathcal{T}\exp(-i\int_{s}^{t}H(\tau)d\tau)
instead of defining only expressions in Eq.
50
. This is a subtle issue due to the
\delta-
function contribution in the kernels, especially when the system itself is infinite-dimensional, but such a unitary group can in-fact be defined using the mathematical machinery of quantum stochastic calculus and its generalizations
[
48
,
77
]
. In this paper, since our focus is to derive a Lieb-Robinson bound, we will not concern ourselves with this mathematical issue but use the definition in Eq.
50
instead of attempting to define the unitary group.

VIII
Recap: Lieb Robinson bound for bounded lattice Hamiltonians

In this section, we first briefly recap the derivation of the Lieb-Robinson bound for bounded lattice Hamiltonian following Ref.
[
4
]
— we will explicitly point out where this derivation fails for the models considered in this paper. This will also serve to guide the remainder of the proof of proposition 1.

Here, we consider a
d
-dimensional lattice
\Lambda
of qudits that locally interact with each other only through a bounded Hamiltonian — this is equivalent to setting
R_{\alpha}(s)=0
in the model in Eq.
16
to obtain

H(s)=\sum_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(s),

where, similar to Eq.
16
,
h_{\alpha}(s)
is supported on a geometrically local set
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
. Furthermore, we will assume that
\left\|{h_{\alpha}(s)}\right\|\leq 1
and
h_{\alpha}(s)
are differentiable with
\left\|{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(s)}\right\|\leq\infty
. We will denote by
\mathcal{M}_{H}
the set of all such Hamiltonians —
\mathcal{M}_{H}
will depend only on the supports
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
and as will be clear below, the Lieb-Robinson bounds that we derive will also depend only on the supports
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
and thus work for any Hamiltonian from
\mathcal{M}_{H}
. We consider
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)
defined in the main-text: Given an operator
O_{X}
supported on the region
X
,

\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)=\left|{\text{Tr}(U^{\dagger}(t,t^{\prime})O_{X}U(t,t^{\prime})-U_{X[l]}^{\dagger}(t,t^{\prime})O_{X}U_{X[l]}(t,t^{\prime}))\rho(t^{\prime}))}\right|,

where
X[l]=\{x\in\Lambda:d(x,X)\leq l\}
,
U_{X[l]}(t,t^{\prime})
is as defined in the main text and
\rho(t^{\prime})=U(t^{\prime},0)\sigma_{S}U^{\dagger}(t^{\prime},0)
is the state on evolving an initial state of the qudits
\sigma_{S}
for time
t^{\prime}
. We will break up the analysis of
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t;l)
into several steps.

Step 1: Defining a commutator bound.
For the purpose of obtaining a bound on
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)
, it is convenient to first obtain a related commutator bound — given
X,Y\subseteq\Lambda
and
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, we define
\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,Y}(t,t^{\prime})
by

\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})=\sup_{O_{X},\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\frac{\left\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\dagger}(U_{\mathcal{B}}^{\dagger}(t,t^{\prime})O_{X}U_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime}))}\right\|}{\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|\left\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\right\|_{\diamond}}.

Here, the supremum is taken over all operators
O_{X}
supported on
X
and superoperators
\mathcal{L}_{Y}
supported on
Y
which also satisfy
\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\dagger}(I)=0
. Now,
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)
can be upper-bounded in by
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
— to see this, first note that

\displaystyle\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)

\displaystyle\leq\|{U^{\dagger}(t,t^{\prime})O_{X}U(t,t^{\prime})-U_{{X[l]}}^{\dagger}(t,t^{\prime})O_{X}U_{{X[l]}}(t,t^{\prime})}\|,

\displaystyle=\|{O_{X}-U(t,t^{\prime})U_{{X[l]}}(t^{\prime},t)O_{X}U_{{X[l]}}(t,t^{\prime})U(t^{\prime},t)}\|,

\displaystyle=\left\|{\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}U(t,s)[H(s)-H_{{X[l]}}(s),U_{{X[l]}}(s,t)O_{X}U_{{X[l]}}(t,s)]U(s,t)ds}\right\|.

Suppose
X_{c}[l]
to be the set of sites which are contained in sub-regions
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
overlapping with
X[l]
:
X_{c}[l]=\bigcup_{\alpha:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X[l]\neq\emptyset}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
. Then, since
U_{{X[l]}}(t^{\prime},s)O_{X}U_{{X[l]}}(s,t^{\prime})
is supported only on
X_{c}[l]
,
[h_{\alpha}(s),U_{{X[l]}}(t^{\prime},s)O_{X}U_{{X[l]}}(s,t^{\prime})]=0
for any
\alpha:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X_{c}[l]=\emptyset
. Therefore, we obtain that

[H(s)-H_{{X[l]}}(s),U_{{X[l]}}(s,t)O_{X}U_{{X[l]}}(t,s)]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}\\
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X_{c}[l]\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}[h_{\alpha}(s),U_{{X[l]}}(s,t)O_{X}U_{{X[l]}}(t,s)].

Finally, we note that

\displaystyle\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}\\
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X_{c}[l]\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}\left\|{[h_{\alpha}(s),U_{{X[l]}}(s,t)O_{X}U_{{X[l]}}(t,s)]}\right\|ds\leq 2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}\\
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X_{c}[l]\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}\gamma^{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}_{\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}(t,s)ds.

(51)

Thus, obtaining a bound on
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
also furnishes a bound on
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)
. We remark that this bound (Eq.
51
) implicitly assumes that
\gamma^{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}_{\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}(t,t^{\prime})
is integrable with respect to
t^{\prime}
— while this might seem to be a reasonable assumption, we will provide a rigorous proof in Step 3.

To understand the complications that arise when considering the full unbounded system-environment model, it will be more insightful to re-express
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
as

\displaystyle\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})=\sup_{\phi,O_{X},\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\frac{\left|{\text{Tr}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\dagger}(U_{\mathcal{B}}^{\dagger}(t,t^{\prime})O_{X}U_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime}))\phi)}\right|}{\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|\left\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\right\|_{\diamond}\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}}=\sup_{\phi,O_{X},\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\frac{|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\phi}\rrangle}|}{\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|\left\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\right\|_{\diamond}\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}},

(52)

where we have used the fact that, for any operator
A
,
\left\|{A}\right\|=\sup_{\phi:\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}\leq 1}\left|{\text{Tr}(A\phi)}\right|
. In this expression for
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
, we can interpret
\phi
to be the
state
(even though it is not constrained to be a positive operator) over which the expected value of the operator
\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\dagger}(U_{\mathcal{B}}^{\dagger}(t,t^{\prime})O_{X}U_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime}))
is being maximized over. For bounded Hamiltonians, we can maximize this expected value over all
\phi
with the only constraint being its normalization (i.e.
\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}\leq 1
) — while dealing with the unbounded system-environment model, a key challenge would be to identify “good” spaces of operators
\phi
to define a quantity similar to
\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,Y}(t,t^{\prime})
.

Step 2: Deriving a first-order expansion for the commutator bound.
The general strategy to derive an upper bound on
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
is to consider it for a slightly larger time interval
[t^{\prime}-\varepsilon,t]
,
\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,Y}(t,t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)
, and relate it to
\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,Y}(t,t^{\prime})
by performing a first order expansion of the propagator
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
. Throughout the following analysis, without loss of generality, will assume the normalization
\left\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\right\|_{\diamond}=1,\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|=1,\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}=1
. We begin by noting that

\displaystyle\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\phi}\rrangle}\right|=\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t^{\prime}-\varepsilon,t^{\prime})\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)|{\phi}\rrangle}\right|.

(53)

Now, to
O(\varepsilon^{2})
, we expect
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t^{\prime}-\varepsilon,t^{\prime})
to be equal to
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime}-\varepsilon,t^{\prime})
i.e., by only considering the terms in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s)
that intersect with
Y
. This can be made precise, via a
1^{\text{st}}
order Dyson series expansion:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t^{\prime}-\varepsilon,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathcal{L}_{Y}-i\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime}),\mathcal{L}_{Y}]ds^{\prime}+i^{2}\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t^{\prime}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})[\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime\prime}),[\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime}),\mathcal{L}_{Y}]]\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime})ds^{\prime\prime}ds^{\prime},

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}_{Y}-i\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(s^{\prime}),\mathcal{L}_{Y}]ds^{\prime}+i^{2}\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t^{\prime}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})[\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime\prime}),[\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime}),\mathcal{L}_{Y}]]\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime})ds^{\prime\prime}ds^{\prime},

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu2\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{=}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime}-\varepsilon,t^{\prime})+i^{2}\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t^{\prime}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})[\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime\prime}),[\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime}),\mathcal{L}_{Y}]]\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime})ds^{\prime\prime}ds^{\prime}-

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad i^{2}\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t^{\prime}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})[\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(s^{\prime\prime}),[\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(s^{\prime}),\mathcal{L}_{Y}]]\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(s^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime})ds^{\prime\prime}ds^{\prime}.

(54)

In (1), we have used that if
\alpha\in\mathcal{B}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{Y}
then
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap Y=\emptyset
and consequently
[\mathcal{C}_{h_{\alpha}(s^{\prime})},\mathcal{L}_{Y}]=0
. Since
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime})=\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(s^{\prime})+\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(s^{\prime})
and consequently
[\mathcal{L}_{Y},\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime})]=[\mathcal{L}_{Y},\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(s^{\prime})]
. In (2), we have used a first order Dyson series expansion of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime}-\varepsilon,t^{\prime})
. Therefore, from Eq.
54
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\phi}\rrangle-\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle}\right|\leq 8(\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|^{2}+\left|{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}\right|^{2})\varepsilon^{2},

(55)

where
|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle=\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime}-\varepsilon,t^{\prime})\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)|{\phi}\rrangle
(note that
\|{\tilde{\phi}}\|_{1}=\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}=1
). Next, we again perform a
1^{\text{st}}
order Dyson expansion of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)
in
\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle
to obtain

\displaystyle\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle=\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle-i\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(s^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle ds^{\prime}+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad(-i)^{2}\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t^{\prime}}\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(s^{\prime\prime})\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(s^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle ds^{\prime\prime}ds^{\prime},

(56)

from which we obtain that

\displaystyle\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle-\bigg(\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle-i\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(s^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle ds^{\prime}\bigg)}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad 2\left|{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}\right|^{2}\varepsilon^{2}.

(57)

From Eqs.
55
and
57
and the triangle inequality, we obtain that (here,
C_{0}=8\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|^{2}+10\left|{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}\right|^{2}
):

\displaystyle\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\phi}\rrangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle}\right|+\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}(s^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle}\right|ds^{\prime}+C_{0}\varepsilon^{2},

\displaystyle\leq\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle}\right|+\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}}\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{C}_{h_{\alpha}(s^{\prime})}\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle}\right|ds^{\prime}+C_{0}\varepsilon^{2},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})+2\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{Y}}\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(t,t^{\prime})\varepsilon+C_{0}\varepsilon^{2},

(58)

where in (1) we have used the definition of
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
and used the upper bounds:

\displaystyle\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\phi}\rrangle}\right|\leq\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,Y}(t,t^{\prime})\left\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\right\|_{\diamond}\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}\leq\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,Y}(t,t^{\prime})\text{ and }

\displaystyle\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{C}_{h_{\alpha}(s^{\prime})}\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\tilde{\phi}}\rrangle}\right|\leq\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(t,t^{\prime})\|{\mathcal{C}_{h_{\alpha}(s^{\prime})}}\|_{\diamond}\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\tilde{\phi})}\|_{1}\leq 2\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(t,t^{\prime}).

In (1), we have also simultaneously upper bounded the sum over
\alpha\in\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}
with a (larger) sum over
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{Y}
. Since this bound holds for any
O_{X},\mathcal{L}_{Y}
and
|{\phi}\rrangle
, again from the definition of
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
, we then obtain that

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,Y}(t,t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\leq\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,Y}(t,t^{\prime})+2\varepsilon\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{Y}}\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(t,t^{\prime})+C_{0}\varepsilon^{2}.

(59)

Step 3: Lieb-Robinson integral inequation.
For a given
t>0
and
\mathcal{B}
, it is convenient to reformulate Eq.
59
in terms of
\Gamma_{X,Y}(\tau)=\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t-\tau)
:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{X,Y}(\tau+\varepsilon)\leq\Gamma_{X,Y}(\tau)+2\varepsilon\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{Y}}\Gamma_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(\tau)+C_{0}\varepsilon^{2}.

(60)

We will next convert it into an integral equation — for this, we choose
\varepsilon=\tau/N
and define
\tau_{n}=n\varepsilon
for
n\in[0:N]
. Then, from Eq.
60
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{X,Y}(\tau_{n})\leq\Gamma_{X,Y}(\tau_{n-1})+2\varepsilon\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{Y}}\Gamma_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(\tau_{n-1})+C_{0}\bigg(\frac{\tau}{N}\bigg)^{2},

(61)

and consequently, we have that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{X,Y}(\tau)\leq\Gamma_{X,Y}(0)+2\varepsilon\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{Y}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\Gamma_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(\tau_{n-1})+C_{0}\frac{\tau^{2}}{N},

(62)

or, in the limit of
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{X,Y}(\tau)\leq\Gamma_{X,Y}(0)+2\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{Y}}\lim_{N\to\infty}\bigg(\varepsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\Gamma_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(\tau_{n-1})\bigg).

(63)

We would like to identify
\lim_{N\to\infty}\varepsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\Gamma_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(\tau_{n-1})
with
\int_{0}^{\tau}\Gamma_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}
. However, for this to hold, we must ensure that
\Gamma_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(\tau)
is integrable with respect to
\tau
. For the finite-dimensional case that we are currently considering, this is relatively straightforward to show since we can establish that
\Gamma_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(\tau)
is in fact a continuous function of
\tau
. To see this, note that assuming
\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|=\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}=\left\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\right\|_{\diamond}=1
,

\displaystyle\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})|{\phi}\rrangle-\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)|{\phi}\rrangle}\right|=\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})(I-\mathcal{U}_{B}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\varepsilon))|{\phi}\rrangle}\right|,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\bigg(\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s)\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)ds\bigg)|{\phi}\rrangle}\right|,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\int_{t^{\prime}-\varepsilon}^{t^{\prime}}\left\|{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s)}\right\|_{\diamond}ds\leq 2\varepsilon\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|.

Therefore, we obtain that

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,Y}(t,t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)=\sup_{\phi,O_{X},\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\frac{|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\phi}\rrangle}|}{\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|\left\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\right\|_{\diamond}\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}}\leq 2\varepsilon\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|+\sup_{\phi,O_{X},\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\frac{|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})|{\phi}\rrangle}|}{\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}\left\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\right\|_{\diamond}}\leq\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})+2\varepsilon\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|\text{ and }

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}^{X,Y}(t,t^{\prime})=\sup_{\phi,O_{X},\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\frac{\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{Y}|{\phi}\rrangle}\right|}{\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|\left\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\right\|_{\diamond}\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}}\leq 2\varepsilon\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|+\sup_{\phi,O_{X},\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\frac{\left|{\llangle{O_{X}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)}\right|}{\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|\left\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\right\|_{\diamond}\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}}\leq\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)+2\varepsilon\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|,

which, taken together, imply that

\left|{\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})-\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime}-\varepsilon)}\right|\leq 2\varepsilon\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|.

Since
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
is a continuous function of
t^{\prime}
,
\Gamma_{X,Y}(\tau)
is also a continuous function of
\tau
. Thus,
\Gamma_{X,Y}(\tau)
is an integrable function of
\tau
(and so is
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
integrable with respect to
t^{\prime}
— note that this also justifies Eq.
51
). From Eq.
63
, we then obtain

\displaystyle\Gamma_{X,Y}(\tau)\leq\Gamma_{X,Y}(0)+2\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{Y}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\Gamma_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}\leq\Gamma_{X,Y}(0)+2L\sum_{S_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}_{Y}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\Gamma_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}.

(64)

Furthermore, we can note that if
X\cap Y=\emptyset
,
\Gamma_{X,Y}(0)=0
and for any
X,Y
,
\Gamma_{X,Y}(t)\leq 1
.

Step 4: Solving the Lieb-Robinson integral inequation.
Finally, we can solve Eq.
64
to obtain the Lieb-Robinson bound. We explicitly provide the solution of this integral inequality as a lemma, since we will reuse it in the non-Markovian case.

Lemma 4

(Lieb Robinson recursion, Ref.
[
4
]
)
.

Suppose, for any
X,Y\subseteq\Lambda
,
\nu_{X,Y}(t)\in[0,1]
satisfies the integral inequality

\nu_{X,Y}(t)\leq\nu_{X,Y}(0)+\omega_{0}\sum_{{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap Y\neq\emptyset}\int_{0}^{t}\nu_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(s)ds,

together with the initial condition
\nu_{X,Y}(0)=0
for
X\cap Y=\emptyset
, then

\nu_{X,Y}(t)\leq\left|{Y}\right|(\exp(e\omega_{0}\mathcal{Z}t)-1)\exp(-d_{X,Y}/a_{0}),

where
\mathcal{Z},a_{0}
are defined in Eq.
17
.

Proof.

This lemma underlies the proof of standard Lieb Robinson bounds, and is well known in the literature on Lieb Robinson bounds. We only provide a proof of this statement for completeness and for the convenience of the reader.

We begin by considering
X,Y\subseteq\Lambda
with
X\cap Y=\emptyset
, then
\Gamma_{X,Y}(0)=0
and we obtain that

\displaystyle\nu_{X,Y}(t)\leq\omega_{0}\sum_{{\alpha}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap Y\neq\emptyset}\int_{0}^{t}\nu_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}(s)ds\leq\omega_{0}\mathcal{Z}\left|{Y}\right|\sup_{{\alpha_{0}}:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{0}}\cap Y\neq\emptyset}\int_{0}^{t}\nu_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{0}}}(s)ds.

(65)

Now, suppose
k
is the smallest integer such that
\exists\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{0}},\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{1}}\dots\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{k}}
such that
Y\cap\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{0}}\neq\emptyset,\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{1}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{2}}\neq\emptyset\dots\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{k-1}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{k}}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{k}}\cap X\neq\emptyset
. Clearly, since
k
is the smallest such integer, for any
\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{0}},\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{1}}\dots\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{k-1}}
such that
Y\cap\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{0}}\neq\emptyset,\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{0}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{1}}\neq\emptyset,\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{1}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{2}}\neq\emptyset\dots\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{k-2}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{k-1}}\neq\emptyset
then
\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{i}}\cap X=\emptyset
for all
i\in[0:(k-1)]
. Consequently, for all such
\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{0}},\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{1}}\dots\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{k-1}}
,
\Gamma_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{i}}}(0)=0
. Therefore, we obtain that for
j\in[0:(k-1)]

\nu_{X,\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{j}}}(t)\leq\omega_{0}\sum_{\beta_{j+1}:S_{\beta_{j}}\cap S_{\beta_{j+1}}\neq\emptyset}\int_{0}^{t}\nu_{X,S_{\beta_{j+1}}}(s)ds,

from which we obtain that

\displaystyle\nu_{X,S_{\beta_{0}}}(s)

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{0}^{k}\sum_{{\beta_{1}}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{1}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{0}}\neq\emptyset}\bigg(\sum_{{\beta_{2}}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{2}}\cap{\beta_{1}}\neq\emptyset}\dots\bigg(\sum_{\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{k}}:\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{k}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{k-1}}\neq\emptyset}\int_{0}^{s}\int_{0}^{s_{1}}\dots\int_{0}^{s_{k-1}}\nu_{X,S_{\beta_{k}}}(s_{k})ds_{k}ds_{k-1}\dots ds_{1}\bigg)\bigg)\bigg),

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{0}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{k}\int_{0}^{s}\int_{0}^{s_{1}}\dots\int_{0}^{s_{k-1}}ds_{k}ds_{k-1}\dots ds_{1}=\frac{(\omega_{0}\mathcal{Z}s)^{k}}{k!}.

Then, using Eq.
65
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\nu_{X,Y}(t)\leq\left|{Y}\right|\frac{(\omega_{0}\mathcal{Z}t)^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}\leq\left|{Y}\right|e^{-(k+1)}\frac{(e\omega_{0}\mathcal{Z}t)^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}\leq\left|{Y}\right|e^{-(k+1)}\sum_{k^{\prime}\geq 1}^{\infty}\frac{(e\omega_{0}\mathcal{Z}t)^{k^{\prime}}}{k^{\prime}!}\leq\left|{Y}\right|e^{-(k+1)}(\exp(e\omega_{0}\mathcal{Z}t)-1).

(66)

Furthermore, we also note that the integer
k
is lower bounded by a function of the distance
d_{X,Y}
. In particular, suppose
z_{0}\in Y\cap\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{0}},z_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{0}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{1}}\dots z_{k+1}\in\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{k}}\cap X
. Then, since
z_{j},z_{j+1}\in\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{j}}
for
j\in[0:k]
,
d(z_{j},z_{j+1})\leq\text{diam}(\mathcal{S}_{\beta_{j}})\leq a_{0}
.

d_{X,Y}\leq d(z_{0},z_{k+1})\leq d(z_{0},z_{1})+d(z_{1},z_{2})\dots d(z_{k},z_{k+1})\leq(k+1)a_{0}.

Finally, observing that the upper bound on the right hand side of Eq.
66
is a decreasing function of
k
, we obtain that

\nu_{X,Y}(t)\leq\left|{Y}\right|(\exp(e\omega_{0}\mathcal{Z}t)-1)\exp(-d_{X,Y}/a_{0}),

which establishes the lemma statement.
∎

Applying this lemma, we then obtain that

\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})=\Gamma_{X,Y}(t-t^{\prime})\leq\left|{Y}\right|\big(\exp(2Le\mathcal{Z}\left|{t-t^{\prime}}\right|)-1\big)\exp(-d_{X,Y}/a_{0}),

and which, together with Eq.
51
yields an upper bound on
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)
.

IX
Common framework for proving non-Markovian Lieb-Robinson bounds

We first establish the basic ingredients of the non-Markovian Lieb–Robinson proof, which are common to both the independent-bath and coupled-bath settings. The specific applications to these two settings will be discussed in the next section.

We will work with the mollified model introduced in the previous section (Eq. (
33
)).
To simplify notation, we drop the explicit dependence on
\delta
throughout the following sections; for example,
B_{\alpha}(t)
denotes
B_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t)
and
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(t)
denotes
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta}(t)
.
Since we will work only with the mollified model, the kernels
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(t)
will be smooth and locally bounded functions of
t
. For notational convenience, given an interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, we will define

\left\|{K_{I}}\right\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{\tau\in I}\sup_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\left|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau)}\right|.

It will also be convenient to define
\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
via

\displaystyle\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}(a,b)=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\int_{a}^{b}|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau)}|d\tau,

(67)

and
\mu^{\textnormal{L}}_{\alpha,\sigma}(a,b):\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
via

\displaystyle\mu^{\textnormal{L}}_{\alpha,\sigma}(a,b)=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{a}^{b}|K_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})|d\tau^{\prime}.

(68)

Note that since
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(t)
already corresponds to the mollified model, it is a smooth function and consequently there is no ambiguity in the integral in above equation. We next observe the following simple properties of
\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}
:

•

For any
a,b
,
\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}(a,b)=-\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}(b,a)
.

•

If
[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]\subseteq[a,b]
,
\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\leq\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}(a,b)
.

•

For any
a\leq b\leq c
,
\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}(a,c)=\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}(a,b)+\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}(b,c)
,

and the same property holds for
\mu^{\textnormal{L}}_{\alpha,\sigma}
.
Given
a,b\subseteq\mathbb{R}
with
a<b
, it will also be convenient to define
{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[a,b]}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
{\mu}^{\textnormal{L}}_{\alpha,\sigma,[a,b]}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
via

\displaystyle{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[a,b]}(t)=\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}(t-b,t-a)\text{ and }{\mu}^{\textnormal{L}}_{\alpha,\sigma,[a,b]}(t)=\mu^{\textnormal{L}}_{\alpha,\sigma}(a-t,b-t),

(69)

and by
\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}_{[a,b]}=\{\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{1,[a,b]},\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{2,[a,b]}\dots\}
as well as the same definition for the vector
\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{L}}_{[a,b]}
. Furthermore, we note that from the properties of
\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}(a,b)
, it follows that
\forall a\leq b\leq c

\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{[a,c]}={\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}_{[a,b]}+{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}_{[b,c]},

and the same relation holds for
\mu^{\textnormal{L}}
.
Furthermore, it also follows from these definitions that
\forall a<b
and
\tau
:

\sum_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\int_{a}^{b}|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})}|d\tau^{\prime}\leq{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[a,b]}(\tau)\text{ and }\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{a}^{b}|{K_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)}|d\tau^{\prime}\leq{\mu}^{\textnormal{L}}_{\alpha,\sigma,[a,b]}(\tau).

It will also be convenient to collect
\mu^{\text{R}}_{\alpha}(a,b),\mu^{\text{R}}_{\alpha,[a,b]},\mu^{\text{L}}_{\alpha,\sigma}(a,b)
and
\mu^{\text{L}}_{\alpha,\sigma,[a,b]}
into vectors over the indices
\alpha
and
\sigma
:

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}(a,b)=\{\mu_{\alpha}^{\text{R}}(a,b)\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}},\quad\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}_{[a,b]}=\{\mu_{\alpha,[a,b]}^{\text{R}}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}},

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{L}}(a,b)=\{\mu_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\text{L}}(a,b)\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},\sigma\in\{l,r\}},\quad\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{L}}_{[a,b]}=\{\mu_{\alpha,\sigma,[a,b]}^{\text{L}}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},\sigma\in\{l,r\}}.

We also remind the reader that
\|{\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}(a,b)}\|_{\infty}=\max_{\alpha}\{\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}(a,b)\}
,
\|{\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{L}}(a,b)}\|_{\infty}=\max_{\alpha,\sigma}\{\mu^{\textnormal{L}}_{\alpha,\sigma}(a,b)\}
, and
\|{{\bm{\mu}}^{\textnormal{R}}_{[a,b]}(t)}\|_{\infty}=\left\|{\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}(t-b,t-a)}\right\|_{\infty}\text{ and }\|{\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{L}}_{[a,b]}(t)}\|_{\infty}=\|{\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{L}}(a-t,b-t)}\|_{\infty},

In the remaining subsections of the common framework, we develop the estimates that will be used in both bath models. We will build upon the derivation of the Lieb-Robinson bound for bounded lattice Hamiltonians reviewed in the previous section, but we will have to modify several different parts of the proof. We briefly sketch the major changes below:

Step 1
:

The counterpart of
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
for the non-Markovian model has to be defined differently from Eq.
52
— in particular, since the environment is an infinite-dimensional quantum field, it cannot be defined as a supremum over all
\phi
(as in Eq.
52
) and still be expected to follow a Lieb-Robinson bound. We will devote subsection
IX.1
to identifying certain spaces of operators over which this supremum can be taken and still admit a Lieb Robinson bound.

Step 2
:

Since the full system-environment Hamiltonian is unbounded, bounding the error in first order Dyson series expansion of
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
with respect to
t^{\prime}
requires more careful analysis.

Step 3
:

The key point in step 3, although simple, is that
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
can be shown to be continuous with respect to
t^{\prime}
. For the full non-Markovian model, we will find that this isn’t necessarily true—however, using a more sophisticated argument, we will establish that (the counter-part of)
\gamma^{X,Y}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})
will be integrable with respect to
t^{\prime}
, even if it is not continuous. This will turn out to be sufficient for our proof of Lieb-Robinson bounds to go through.

Step 4
:

This step will go through without any major modifications.

IX.1
Identifying operator spaces

We briefly recap the definition of
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)
for the non-Markovian model from the main text — we will be given an operator
O_{X}
supported on
X\subseteq\Lambda
. We will then consider, for given
t^{\prime}>0
, a state
\rho(t^{\prime})=U(t^{\prime},0)(\sigma_{S}\otimes\rho_{E})U(0,t^{\prime})
generated by evolving
\sigma_{S}\otimes\rho_{E}
for time
t^{\prime}
, where
\sigma_{S}
is an arbitrary system state and
\rho_{E}
is the initial Gaussian environment state. From time
t^{\prime}
to
t
, we will consider evolution under two Hamiltonians — the full Hamiltonian
H(t)
[Eq.
16
], and the Hamiltonian
H_{\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}(t)
obtained by restricting
H(t)
, as per Eq.
18
, to

X[l]=\{x\in\Lambda:d(x,X)\leq l\}

i.e. to a distance
l
around the support
X
of the observable
O_{X}
. The quantity that we will upper bound will be the error between the local observable
O_{X}
under the states obtained from these two evolutions i.e.

\displaystyle\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)=\left|{\text{Tr}\big((U(t^{\prime},t)O_{X}U(t,t^{\prime})-U_{{X[l]}}(t^{\prime},t)O_{X}U_{{X[l]}}(t,t^{\prime}))\rho(t^{\prime})\big)}\right|,

(70)

where
U_{X[l]}(t,t^{\prime})
will be the propagator of the Hamiltonian
H_{X[l]}(t)
. We begin with the following straightforward lemma which will motivate our definition of a commutator bound.

Lemma 5

(Truncation error decomposition)
.

The error
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)
can be bounded as

\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)\leq\Delta_{O_{X}}^{\textnormal{R}}(t,t^{\prime};l)+\Delta^{\textnormal{L}}_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l),

where the right-contraction contribution is

\displaystyle\Delta^{\textnormal{R}}_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}\\
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap{X}_{c}[l]\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\bigg(\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}\left|{\llangle{O_{X},I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(t,s)\mathcal{C}_{h_{\alpha}(s)}|{\rho(s)}\rrangle}\right|ds+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\sum_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\int_{s=t^{\prime}}^{t}\int_{s^{\prime}=0}^{s}|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(s-s^{\prime})}||{\llangle{O_{X},I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(t,s)\mathcal{C}_{R_{\alpha}(s)}|{\rho_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(s,s^{\prime})}\rrangle}|ds^{\prime}ds\bigg),

(71)

and the left-contraction contribution is bounded by

\displaystyle\Delta_{O_{X}}^{\textnormal{L}}(t,t^{\prime};l)=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}\\
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{X[l]},\sigma\end{subarray}}\int_{s=t^{\prime}}^{t}\int_{s^{\prime}=s}^{t}\left|{K_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}(s^{\prime}-s)}\right|\times

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left|{\llangle{O_{X},I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(t,s^{\prime})\mathcal{C}_{R_{\alpha^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})}|{\tilde{\rho}_{\alpha,\sigma}(s,s^{\prime})}\rrangle}\right|ds^{\prime}ds.

(72)

where
X_{c}[l]=\bigcup_{\alpha:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X[l]\neq\emptyset}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
,
|{\rho(s)}\rrangle=\mathcal{U}(s,0)|{\rho(0)}\rrangle
,
|{\rho_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(s,s^{\prime})}\rrangle=\mathcal{U}(s,s^{\prime})R_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})\mathcal{U}(s^{\prime},0)|{\rho(0)}\rrangle
and
|{\tilde{\rho}_{\alpha,\sigma}(s,s^{\prime})}\rrangle=\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(s^{\prime},s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)\mathcal{U}(s,0)|{\rho(0)}\rrangle
.

Proof.

In vectorized notation, the error
\Delta_{O_{X}}(l)
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)

\displaystyle=\left|{\llangle{O_{X},I_{E}}|\big(\mathcal{U}(t,t^{\prime})-\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(t,t^{\prime})\big)|{\rho(t^{\prime})}\rrangle}\right|,

\displaystyle=\left|{\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}\llangle{O_{X},I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(t,t^{\prime})\bigg(\frac{d}{ds}\big(\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(t^{\prime},s)\mathcal{U}(s,t^{\prime})\big)\bigg)|{\rho(t^{\prime})}\rrangle ds}\right|,

\displaystyle=\left|{\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}\llangle{O_{X},I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(t,s)\big(\mathcal{H}_{X[l]}(s)-\mathcal{H}(s)\big)\mathcal{U}(s,0)|{\rho(0)}\rrangle ds}\right|.

(73)

Next, we decompose
\mathcal{H}(s)-\mathcal{H}_{X[l]}(s)
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(s)-\mathcal{H}_{X[l]}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}\bigg(\mathcal{C}_{h_{\alpha}(s)}+\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}B_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)\bigg),

where we have used
X_{c}[l]=\bigcup_{\alpha:\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X[l]\neq\emptyset}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
(or, said in words,
X_{c}[l]
is the union of all sub-regions
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
which overlap with
X[l]
).
For notational convenience, define

\llangle{\Theta(s)}|:=\llangle{O_{X},I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(t,s),\qquad|{\rho(s)}\rrangle:=\mathcal{U}(s,0)|{\rho(0)}\rrangle.

If
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X_{c}[l]=\emptyset
, then

\displaystyle\llangle{\Theta(s)}|\mathcal{C}_{h_{\alpha}(s)}=\llangle{\Theta(s)}|\mathcal{C}_{R_{\alpha}(s)}=0.

Lemma
1
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}\llangle{\Theta(s)}|B_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)|{\rho(s)}\rrangle=\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\llangle{\Theta(s)}|;B_{\alpha,\sigma}(s))R_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)|{\rho(s)}\rrangle+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}\llangle{\Theta(s)}|R_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(B_{\alpha,\sigma}(s);|{\rho(s)}\rrangle).

(74)

The second term in the last expression satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}\llangle{\Theta(s)}|R_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(B_{\alpha,\sigma}(s);|{\rho(s)}\rrangle)\right|\leq\sum_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{s}\left|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(s-s^{\prime})}\right|\left|{\llangle{\Theta(s)}|\mathcal{C}_{R_{\alpha}(s)}|{\rho_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(s,s^{\prime})}\rrangle}\right|ds^{\prime},

(75)

and hence contributes only when
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X_{c}[l]\neq\emptyset
. A similar calculation can be applied to
\mathcal{C}_{h_{\alpha}(s)}
. For the first term, using the definition of
\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\cdot;\cdot)
,

\displaystyle\left|\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\llangle{\Theta(s)}|;B_{\alpha,\sigma}(s))R_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)|{\rho(s)}\rrangle\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}\\
\end{subarray}}\int_{s}^{t}\left|K_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}(s^{\prime}-s)\right|\left|\llangle{O_{X},I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(t,s^{\prime})\mathcal{C}_{R_{\alpha^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})}\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(s^{\prime},s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)|{\rho(s)}\rrangle\right|ds^{\prime}.

(76)

We emphasize that in the last line, the contribution of
\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}
cannot be restricted to
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X_{c}[l]\neq\emptyset
in general. Combining the above estimates with Eq. (
73
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}\\
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X_{c}[l]\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}\left|\llangle{\Theta(s)}|\mathcal{C}_{h_{\alpha}(s)}|{\rho(s)}\rrangle\right|ds

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}\\
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\cap X_{c}[l]\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\left|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(s-s^{\prime})}\right|\left|{\llangle{\Theta(s)}|\mathcal{C}_{R_{\alpha}(s)}|{\rho_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(s,s^{\prime})}\rrangle}\right|ds^{\prime}ds

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}\\
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}\\
\sigma\end{subarray}}\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}\int_{s}^{t}\left|{K_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}(s^{\prime}-s)}\right|\left|{\llangle{O_{X},I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(t,s^{\prime})\mathcal{C}_{R_{\alpha^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})}\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(s^{\prime},s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)|{\rho(s)}\rrangle}\right|ds^{\prime}ds

\displaystyle=\Delta_{O_{X}}^{\textnormal{R}}(t,t^{\prime};l)+\Delta^{\textnormal{L}}_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l).

∎

Closed operator spaces
. Lemma
5
reduces the first truncation contribution
\Delta_{1,O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)
to commutators, e.g. terms of the form

\displaystyle\left|{\llangle{O_{X},I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(t,s)\mathcal{C}_{h_{\alpha}(s)}|{\rho(s)}\rrangle}\right|=\left|{\text{Tr}([U_{X[l]}(s,t)O_{X}U_{X[l]}(t,s),h_{\alpha}(s)]\rho(s))}\right|\text{ and }

\displaystyle\left|{\llangle{O_{X},I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{X[l]}(t,s)\mathcal{C}_{R_{\alpha}(s)}|{\rho_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(s,s^{\prime})}\rrangle}\right|=\left|{\text{Tr}([U_{X[l]}(s,t)O_{X}U_{X[l]}(t,s),R_{\alpha}(s)]\rho_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(s,s^{\prime}))}\right|.

An approach to bounding these terms would be to attempt to place a bound on the norms of the commutators i.e.
\left\|{[U_{X[l]}(s,t)O_{X}U_{X[l]}(t,s),h_{\alpha}(s)]}\right\|
and
\left\|{[U_{X[l]}(s,t)O_{X}U_{X[l]}(t,s),R_{\alpha}(s)]}\right\|
. In the typical setting of a bounded lattice Hamiltonian, these norms can be upper-bounded by a quantity that exponentially decreases with the distance between
X
and
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
. However, for the system-environment models considered in this paper, we do not expect such a bound to exist due to the infinite-dimensionalty of the environment and consequently, we need to specifically exploit the properties of the states (i.e.
\rho(s)
and
\rho_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(s,s^{\prime})
) with respect to which the commutator expectations are being taken. This motivates us to define a space of operators that are generated from the initial environment gaussian (definition
6
) which also has mathematically useful closure properties under time-evolution (lemma
6
) that enable a Lieb-Robinson bound.

Definition 6

(Operator space
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
)
.

Suppose
\bm{\zeta}=\{\zeta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
with
\zeta_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
, then
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
is the set of operators
\phi
such that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\rho_{E})
[Def.
1
] and has a representation with time edges
\{(s_{i},t_{i})\}_{i\in[1:n]}
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{\sum_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\int_{s_{i}}^{t_{i}}|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})}|d\tau^{\prime}}\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|{\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[s_{i},t_{i}]}(\tau)}|\leq\zeta_{\alpha}(\tau)\ \forall\ \tau\in\mathbb{R}.

Lemma 6

(Closure of
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
)
.

The set
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
satisifies the following properties:

(a)

For any
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and
0<s<t
,

{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})U_{\mathcal{B}}(s,t)\text{ and }{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,t)\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E}){U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}+\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}_{[s,t]}}(\rho_{E}),

where
{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}_{[s,t]}
is defined in Eq. (
69
).

(b)

Suppose
\Omega
is a system super-operator, then

\Omega\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})\subseteq\left\|{\Omega}\right\|_{\diamond}\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E}).

(c)

Suppose
f
is either of the two functions:
f(\phi)=\left\|{\phi}\right\|_{1}
or
f(\phi)=\left|{\llangle{\theta}|\phi\rrangle}\right|
for some
\theta\in\mathcal{Q}(\rho_{E})
, then

f\big(\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\{B_{\alpha_{j},\sigma_{j}}(\tau_{j})\}_{j\in\{1:m\}};\phi)\big)\leq\zeta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{\alpha_{2}}(\tau_{2})\dots\zeta_{\alpha_{m}}(\tau_{m})\bigg(\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})}f(\phi)\bigg).

Proof.

(a) Assume that
s<t
and note that if
\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
, then there is a representation of
\phi
with time-edges
\{(s_{i},t_{i})\}_{i\in[1:n]}
, then
\mathcal{U}_{X}(t,s)|{\phi}\rrangle
has a representation with time-edges
\{(s_{i}^{\prime},t_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i\in[1:(n+1)]}=\{(s_{i},t_{i})\}_{i\in[1:n]}\cup\{(s,t)\}
. We then obtain that
\forall\tau,\alpha

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}|{\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[s_{i}^{\prime},t_{i}^{\prime}]}(\tau)}|=\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|{\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[s_{i},t_{i}]}(\tau)}|\bigg)+\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[s,t]}(\tau)\leq\zeta_{\alpha}(\tau)+\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[s,t]}(\tau),

which implies that
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)|{\phi}\rrangle\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}+\bm{\mu}_{[s,t]}^{R}}(\rho_{E})
. A similar argument holds for
U_{\mathcal{B}}(s,t)\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}(\rho_{E})U_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)
.

(b) follows directly from definition and sub-multiplicity of the diamond norm.

(c) For notational convenience, let us define

\Lambda_{m}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\dots\tau_{m})=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})\\
\{\alpha_{i},\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in[1:m]}\end{subarray}}f\big(\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\{B_{\alpha_{j},\sigma_{j}}(\tau_{j})\}_{j\in[1:m]};|{\phi}\rrangle)\big).

with
\Lambda_{0}=\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})}f(\phi)
. Consider first
m=1
. Suppose
|{\phi}\rrangle\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
has a representation

|{\phi}\rrangle=\prod_{i=n}^{1}\Omega_{i}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})|{\sigma_{S},\rho_{E}}\rrangle,

then using Eqs. (
27
a) and (
27
b), we obtain that

\overrightarrow{\text{W}}_{\rho_{E}}\big(B_{\alpha_{1},\sigma_{1}}(\tau_{1});|{\phi}\rrangle)\big)=-i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,\sigma^{\prime}\\
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\sigma^{\prime}}\int_{s_{j}}^{t_{j}}K_{\alpha_{1},\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau_{1}-\tau^{\prime})|{\phi_{j,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})}\rrangle d\tau^{\prime},

where

|{\phi_{j,\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)}\rrangle=\bigg(\prod_{i=n}^{j+1}\Omega_{i}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})\bigg)\Omega_{j}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}(t_{j},\tau)R_{\alpha,\sigma}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}(\tau,s_{j})\bigg(\prod_{i=j-1}^{1}\Omega_{i}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})\bigg)|{\sigma_{S},\rho_{E}}\rrangle.

We first show that in
|{\phi_{j,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau)}\rrangle\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
: Observe that
|{\phi_{j,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau)}\rrangle\in\mathcal{S}(\rho_{E})
with time-edges
\{(s_{i}^{\prime},t_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i\in[1:(n+1)]}=\{(s_{1},t_{1})\dots(s_{j-1},t_{j-1}),(s_{j},\tau),(\tau,t_{j}),(s_{j+1},t_{j+1})\dots(s_{n},t_{n})\}
. Furthermore, note that since
\tau
is in the interval between
s_{j}
and
t_{j}
, either
s_{j}\leq\tau\leq t_{j}
if
s_{j}\leq t_{j}
or
t_{j}\leq\tau\leq s_{j}
if
t_{j}\leq s_{j}
. Therefore, both
\mu_{\alpha,[s_{j},\tau]}(t)
and
\mu_{\alpha,[\tau,t_{j}]}(t)
have the same sign and consequently,

|{\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[s_{j},\tau]}(t)}|+|{\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[\tau,t_{j}]}(t)}|=|{\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[s_{j},\tau]}(t)+\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[\tau,t_{j}]}(t)}|=|{\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha,[s_{j},t_{j}]}(t)}|.

Using this, we then obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}|{\mu_{\alpha,[s_{i}^{\prime},t_{i}^{\prime}]}^{\textnormal{R}}(t)}|=

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}|{\mu_{\alpha,[s_{i},t_{i}]}^{\textnormal{R}}(t)}|+\sum_{i=j+1}^{n}|{\mu_{\alpha,[s_{i},t_{i}]}^{\textnormal{R}}(t)}|\bigg)+|{\mu_{\alpha,[\tau,t_{j}]}^{\textnormal{R}}(t)}|+|{\mu_{\alpha,[s_{j},\tau]}^{\textnormal{R}}(t)}|,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\mu_{\alpha,[s_{i},t_{i}]}^{\textnormal{R}}(t)+\sum_{i=j+1}^{n}\mu_{\alpha,[s_{i},t_{i}]}^{\textnormal{R}}(t)\bigg)+|{\mu_{\alpha,[s_{j},t_{j}]}^{\textnormal{R}}(t)}|,

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\zeta_{\alpha}(t),

This establishes that
|{\phi_{j,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau)}\rrangle\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
. Therefore, we have from the triangle inequality (which is satisfied for
f
)

\displaystyle f\big(\overrightarrow{\textnormal{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(B_{\alpha_{1},\sigma_{1}}(\tau_{1});|{\phi}\rrangle)\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,\sigma^{\prime}\\
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}\end{subarray}}{\int_{s_{j}}^{t_{j}}|{K_{\alpha_{1},\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau_{1}-\tau^{\prime})}|f({\phi_{j,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})})d\tau^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})}f(\phi)\bigg)\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j},\sigma^{\prime}}{\int_{s_{j}}^{t_{j}}|{K_{\alpha_{1},\alpha^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau_{1}-\tau^{\prime})}|d\tau^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|{\mu_{\alpha_{1},[s_{j},t_{j}]}(\tau_{1})}|\leq\Lambda_{0}\zeta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau_{1}).

Next, we proceed similarly using Eq.
28
: Recall that

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\text{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\{B_{\alpha_{i},\sigma_{i}}(\tau_{i})\}_{i\in[1:m]};{\phi})=-i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,\sigma^{\prime}\\
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\sigma^{\prime}}\int_{s_{j}}^{t_{j}}K_{\alpha_{m},\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau_{m}-\tau)\overrightarrow{\text{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\{B_{\alpha_{i},\sigma_{i}}(\tau_{i})\}_{i\in[1:m-1]};{\phi_{j,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau)})d\tau,

where
|{\phi_{j,\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)}\rrangle
is as defined above. Using the fact that
f
satisfies a triangle-inequality,

\displaystyle f\big(\overrightarrow{\text{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\{B_{\alpha_{i},\sigma_{i}}(\tau_{i})\}_{i\in[1:m]};{\phi})\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,\sigma^{\prime}\\
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}\end{subarray}}\int_{s_{j}}^{t_{j}}|{K_{\alpha_{m},\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau_{m}-\tau)}|f\big(\overrightarrow{\text{W}}_{\rho_{E}}(\{B_{\alpha_{i},\sigma_{i}}(\tau_{i})\}_{i\in[1:m-1]};\phi_{j,\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau))\big)d\tau,

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{m-1}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\dots\tau_{m-1})\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j},\sigma^{\prime}}\int_{s_{j}}^{t_{j}}|{K_{\alpha_{m},\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau_{m}-\tau)}|d\tau,

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{m-1}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\dots\tau_{m-1})\sum_{j=1}^{n}|{\mu_{\alpha_{m},[s_{j},t_{j}]}(\tau_{m})}|\leq\Lambda_{m-1}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\dots\tau_{m-1})\zeta_{\alpha_{m}}(\tau_{m}),

and therefore
\Lambda_{m}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\dots\tau_{m})\leq\zeta_{\alpha_{m}}(\tau_{m})\Lambda_{m-1}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\dots\tau_{m-1})
. Recursing this bound, it follows that
\Lambda_{m}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\dots\tau_{m})\leq\zeta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{\alpha_{2}}(\tau_{2})\dots\zeta_{\alpha_{m}}(\tau_{m})\Lambda_{0}
, which proves the lemma statement.
∎

Remark
. We note that lemma
6
c can be physically interpreted as effectively bounding the action of a bath operator
B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)
on a state in the space
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
. Since we will always use Wick’s theorem to handle the Bath super-operators
B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)
on a given state, the right Wick contraction can be understood to be the relevant way of applying the super-operator
B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)
on a given state (as opposed to multiplying with the super-operator
B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)
). Then, lemma
6
(c) essentially says that on the space
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
, the bath super-operator
B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)
, despite being unbounded as an operator on the entire Hilbert space, is effectively bounded in the sense of Wick contractions.

It is also convenient to define an operator space
\mathcal{Q}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
that acts like the dual of
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
.

Definition 7
.

Given
\bm{\zeta}=\{\zeta_{\alpha,\sigma}\}
with
\zeta_{\alpha,\sigma}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
, an operator
\llangle{\theta}|\in\mathcal{Q}_{\bm{\zeta}}({\rho_{E}})
if
\llangle{\theta}|\in\mathcal{Q}(\rho_{E})
[Def.
2
] and has a representation with time edges
\{(s_{i},t_{i})\}_{i\in[1:n]}
such that

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}\left|{\int_{s_{j}}^{t_{j}}|K_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)|d\tau^{\prime}}\right|=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu^{\textnormal{L}}_{\alpha,\sigma,[s_{j},t_{j}]}(\tau)\leq\zeta_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)\ \forall\ \tau\in\mathbb{R}.

We can also obtain a lemma similar to lemma
6
c that will be useful in the calculations in the following subsections.

Lemma 7
.

Suppose
f
is either of the two functions:
f(\theta)=\left\|{\theta}\right\|_{1}
or
f(\theta)=\left|{\llangle{\theta}|\phi\rrangle}\right|
for some
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\rho_{E})
, then

f\big(\overleftarrow{\textnormal{W}}(\llangle{\theta}|;\{B_{\alpha_{j},\sigma_{j}}(\tau_{j})\}_{j\in\{1:m\}})\big)\leq 2^{m}\zeta_{\alpha_{1},\sigma_{1}}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{\alpha_{2},\sigma_{2}}(\tau_{2})\dots\zeta_{\alpha_{m},\sigma_{m}}(\tau_{m})\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(\rho_{E})}f(\theta),

for all
\theta\in\mathcal{Q}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})
,
\{\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{A}\}_{i\in[1:m]},\{\sigma_{i}\in\{l,r\}\}_{i\in[1:m]},\{\tau_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\}_{i\in[1:m]}
.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is the same as the proof of lemma
6
c, which we refer the reader to. The factor
2
comes from the summation of
\sigma^{\prime}
in the contraction
\mathrm{Tr}(B_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})B_{\alpha,\sigma}(\tau)\rho_{E})
.
∎

IX.2
Defining the commutator bound

Having defined an appropriate operator space and motivated by lemma
5
, we will use
\gamma_{\bm{\zeta},\mathcal{B}}^{X,Y}(t,t^{\prime})
defined below.

Definition 8
.

Given
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and
X,Y\subseteq\Lambda
and
\bm{\zeta}=\{\zeta_{\alpha}\}
with
\zeta_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
, define

\displaystyle\gamma_{\bm{\zeta},\mathcal{B}}^{X,Y}(t,t^{\prime})=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})\\
\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|,\left\|{\mathcal{L}_{Y}}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left|{\textnormal{Tr}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\dagger}\big(U_{\mathcal{B}}(t^{\prime},t)O_{X}U_{\mathcal{B}}(t,t^{\prime})\big)\phi)}\right|=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\rho_{E})\\
