\mu^{\textnormal{R}}_{\alpha}(0,t)\leq 2(C_{E}(t+2\delta)^{d}+B_{E})
.
Futhermore, Eq. (
140
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{s=t^{\prime}}^{t}\gamma_{\bm{\mu}_{[0,s]}^{\text{R}},\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}^{X,S_{\alpha}}(t,s)ds

\displaystyle\leq\left|{\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}\right|\left|{X}\right|\Gamma_{0,c}(t)\int_{s=t^{\prime}}^{t}\exp\left(\frac{\kappa_{E}}{p_{+}}({v_{\textnormal{LR,c}}(t)(t-s)-d(X,S_{\alpha})})\right)ds

\displaystyle\leq(a_{0}+1)^{d}\left|{X}\right|\frac{p_{+}\Gamma_{0,c}(t)}{\kappa_{E}v_{\text{LR},c}(t)}\exp\left(\frac{\kappa_{E}}{p_{+}}({v_{\textnormal{LR,c}}(t)(t-t^{\prime})-d(X,S_{\alpha})})\right)

\displaystyle\leq(a_{0}+1)^{d}\left|{X}\right|\frac{\Gamma_{0,c}(t)}{\lambda_{E}}\exp\left(\frac{\kappa_{E}}{p_{+}}({v_{\textnormal{LR,c}}(t)(t-t^{\prime})-d(X,S_{\alpha})})\right),

(142)

where we have used
\left|{S_{\alpha}}\right|\leq(a_{0}+1)^{d}
and that
v_{\text{LR},c}(t)\geq v_{0}\geq p_{+}\lambda_{E}/\kappa_{E}
.
Thus, we obtain that

\displaystyle\Delta_{O_{X}}^{\text{R}}(t,t^{\prime};l)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2(a_{0}+1)^{d}}{\lambda_{E}}\left|{X}\right|\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|(1+2B_{E}+2C_{E}(t+2\delta)^{d})\Gamma_{0,c}(t)

(143)

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(\frac{\kappa_{E}v_{\textnormal{LR,c}}(t)(t-t^{\prime})}{p_{+}}\right)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}\\
S_{\alpha}\cap X_{c}[l]\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\exp\left(-\frac{\kappa_{E}d(X,S_{\alpha})}{p_{+}}\right).

For these supports,
d(X,S_{\alpha})>l
and
S_{\alpha}
intersects
X[l+a_{0}]\setminus X[l]
. Counting this boundary by a union of lattice shells centered at sites of
X
gives, for a constant
C_{d}>0
depending only on
d
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}\\
S_{\alpha}\cap X_{c}[l]\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\exp\left(-\frac{\kappa_{E}d(X,S_{\alpha})}{p_{+}}\right)\leq C_{d}\mathcal{Z}\left|{X}\right|(1+a_{0})(1+l+a_{0})^{d-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\kappa_{E}l}{p_{+}}\bigg).

(144)

Therefore, we obtain that

\displaystyle\Delta_{O_{X}}^{\text{R}}(t,t^{\prime};l)\leq\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|f^{\text{R}}(l)g^{\text{R}}(t)\exp\left(\frac{\kappa_{E}}{p_{+}}(v_{\text{LR},c}(t)(t-t^{\prime})-l)\right),

(145)

where
f^{\text{R}}(l)\leq O(l^{d-1})
as
l\to\infty
and
g^{\text{R}}(t)\leq O(t^{3d})
as
t\to\infty
.

Upper bounding
\Delta_{O_{X}}^{\text{L}}(t,t^{\prime};l)
.
Returning to lemma
8
:

\displaystyle\Delta^{\textnormal{L}}_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)\leq 2\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}\sum_{\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]},\sigma}\int_{s^{\prime}=t^{\prime}}^{t}\int_{s=t^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}|{K^{\delta}_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}(s^{\prime}-s)}|\gamma^{X,S_{\alpha^{\prime}}}_{\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}_{[0,s^{\prime}]},\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}(t,s^{\prime})ds\,ds^{\prime}.

(146)

First integrate over
s
at fixed
s^{\prime}
. The total-variation bound gives

\int_{t^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}|{K^{\delta}_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma}(s^{\prime}-s)}|\,ds\leq\frac{K_{0}\exp(2\lambda_{E}\delta)}{\min(1,\lambda_{0})^{\eta}}F_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}-t^{\prime}),

where
F_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
is defined in lemma
18
. Since
\lambda_{E}\leq\kappa_{E}v_{\textnormal{LR},c}(t)/p_{+}
, the remaining time integral satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{s^{\prime}=t^{\prime}}^{t}\exp(\lambda_{E}(s^{\prime}-t^{\prime}))\exp\bigg(\frac{\kappa_{E}v_{\text{LR},c}(t)}{p_{+}}(t-s^{\prime})\bigg)ds^{\prime}\leq t\exp\bigg(\frac{\kappa_{E}v_{\text{LR},c}(t)}{p_{+}}(t-t^{\prime})\bigg).

(147)

Therefore, we obtain that

\displaystyle\Delta^{\textnormal{L}}_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4K_{0}\exp(2\lambda_{E}\delta)(a_{0}+1)^{d}t\Gamma_{0,c}(t)}{\min(1,\lambda_{0})^{\eta}}\left|{X}\right|\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|\exp\bigg(\frac{\kappa_{E}v_{\text{LR},c}(t)}{p_{+}}(t-t^{\prime})\bigg)

(148)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}\sum_{\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}\frac{\exp(-\kappa_{E}(p_{+}d(\text{x}_{\alpha},\text{x}_{\alpha^{\prime}})+d({S}_{\alpha^{\prime}},X))/p_{+})}{(1+d(\text{x}_{\alpha},\text{x}_{\alpha^{\prime}}))^{\eta}}.

Next, using Eq. (
100
) together with the lattice triangle inequality and
\mathrm{diam}(S_{\alpha^{\prime}})\leq a_{0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle p_{+}d(\text{x}_{\alpha},\text{x}_{\alpha^{\prime}})+d(S_{\alpha^{\prime}},X)\geq d({S}_{\alpha},S_{\alpha^{\prime}})+d(S_{\alpha^{\prime}},X)-q_{+}\geq d(S_{\alpha},X)-(q_{+}+a_{0}),

(149)

Enforcing
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{X[l]},\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}
, we then obtain
p_{+}d(\text{x}_{\alpha},\text{x}_{\alpha^{\prime}})+d(S_{\alpha^{\prime}},X)\geq l-(q_{+}+a_{0})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}\sum_{\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}\frac{\exp(-\kappa_{E}(p_{+}d(\text{x}_{\alpha},\text{x}_{\alpha^{\prime}})+d({S}_{\alpha^{\prime}},X))/p_{+})}{(1+d(\text{x}_{\alpha},\text{x}_{\alpha^{\prime}}))^{\eta}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\exp(-\kappa_{E}l/p_{+})\exp(\kappa_{E}(q_{+}+a_{0})/p_{+})\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}\sum_{\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}\frac{1}{(1+d(\text{x}_{\alpha},\text{x}_{\alpha^{\prime}}))^{\eta}}

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}\frac{B_{\eta}}{2^{\eta}}\exp(-\kappa_{E}l/p_{+})\exp(\kappa_{E}(q_{+}+a_{0})/p_{+})\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{X[l]}}1

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{B_{\eta}}{2^{\eta}}\exp(-\kappa_{E}l/p_{+})\exp(\kappa_{E}(q_{+}+a_{0})/p_{+})\mathcal{Z}\left|{X}\right|(2l+1)^{d},

(150)

where in (1) we have used lemma
15
. Combining the above estimates, we then obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{O_{X}}^{\text{L}}(t,t^{\prime};l)\leq\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|f^{\text{L}}(l)g^{\text{L}}(t)\exp\left(\frac{\kappa_{E}}{p_{+}}(v_{\text{LR},c}(t)(t-t^{\prime})-l)\right),

(151)

where
f^{\text{L}}(l)\leq O(l^{d})
as
l\to\infty
and
g^{\text{L}}(t)\leq O(t^{2d+1})
.

Combining the upper bounds in Eqs. (
145
) and (
151
), we obtain that

\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)\leq\left\|{O_{X}}\right\|f(l)g(t)\exp\left(\frac{\kappa_{E}}{p_{+}}(v_{\text{LR},c}(t)(t-t^{\prime})-l)\right),

where
f(l)=f^{\text{R}}(l)+f^{\text{L}}(l)\leq O(l^{d})
as
l\to\infty
and
g(t)=\max(g^{\text{R}}(t),g^{\text{L}}(t))\leq O(t^{3d})
as
t\to\infty
. Finally, recall that this bound holds for the
\delta-
mollified kernel. However, from proposition
4
, it follows that the limit of
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,t^{\prime};l)
as
\delta\to 0
exists. It can be checked that the limit of
\delta\to 0
of the right hand side in the above inequality also exists: Taking this limit is equivalent to setting
\delta=0
in the expressions for
f(l),g(t)
and
v_{\text{LR},c}(t)
. This proves the proposition.
∎

XII
Saturating the Lieb-Robinson bounds: Violation of the linear light-cone

This section gives independent-bath and coupled-bath examples that saturate the velocity scalings in Propositions 1 and 2, respectively. In the independent-bath example, the memory measure
m(t)
defined in Eq. (
9
) grows linearly with
t
, and the transport velocity has the same scaling. The coupled-bath example establishes the tightness of the exponential-tail scaling in one dimension.

XII.1
Independent bath model

Here we analyze the explicit example with independent baths, when the memory kernels
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
have
windowed total variation that grows linearly in time, which induces a supersonic transportation and will violate a linear Lieb-Robinson light cone.
We first provide the system-environment Hamiltonian that was sketched out in the main text. The system will be a 1D chain of
n
qubits, and the environment will have
n-1
baths, each of which will have only one bosonic mode. Specifically, we label the bath operators by
\alpha=(i,\nu)
and set
B_{(i,\nu)}(t)=a_{i}^{(\nu)}
for all
t
, where
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
and
\nu\in\{-,+\}
, with
a_{i}^{(-)}=a_{i}
and
a_{i}^{(+)}=a_{i}^{\dagger}
. The
i^{\text{th}}
bath will interact with the
i^{\text{th}}
and
(i+1)^{\text{th}}
qubit. We use
\sigma_{i}:=\ket{0_{i}}\!\bra{1_{i}}
,
\sigma_{i}^{\dagger}:=\ket{1_{i}}\!\bra{0_{i}}
,
\sigma_{i}^{x}:=\sigma_{i}+\sigma_{i}^{\dagger}
, and
\sigma_{i}^{z}:=\ket{0_{i}}\!\bra{0_{i}}-\ket{1_{i}}\!\bra{1_{i}}
. All bath modes start in the vacuum. Define
K_{i}(\tau):=K_{(i,-),(i,+)}(\tau)=1
; the other vacuum two-point functions vanish. The one-sided memory budget and the two-sided memory measure are therefore

\mu^{\mathrm{R}}_{(i,-)}(0,t)=\mathrm{TV}(K_{i};[0,t])=t,\qquad m(t)=\mathrm{TV}(K_{i};[-t,t])=2t.

(152)

In particular, at the observation time
t=2T+1
, these quantities equal
2T+1
and
4T+2
, respectively. Proposition
1
consequently gives
v_{\mathrm{LR}}(2T+1)=O(T)
. We will construct a protocol with transport velocity
v_{\mathrm{S}}(T)=\Omega(T)
, showing that this scaling is tight up to constants. Here
f(T)=\Omega(g(T))
means that
f(T)\geq cg(T)
for some
c>0
and all sufficiently large
T
.

Fix a positive integer
T
and the observation time
2T+1
. The Hamiltonian
H(t)
is defined in three stages:

H(t)=\begin{cases}H_{\mathrm{pump}}(t),&0\leq t<T;\\
H_{\mathrm{ex}}(t),&T\leq t<T+1;\\
H_{\mathrm{prop}}(t),&T+1\leq t<2T+1.\end{cases}

(153)

where, as the names suggest,
H_{\text{pump}}(t)
pumps the environment oscillators with photons,
H_{\text{ex}}(t)
puts an excitation in the system and
H_{\text{prop}}(t)
propagates the excitation. In order to ensure that the time-dependent system Hamiltonian and the system coupling operators are smooth, we will use a pulse function
\xi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
in their construction —
\xi
can be chosen to be any smooth function which satisfies

\xi(t)=0\text{ for all }t\notin[0,1]\text{ and }\int_{0}^{1}\xi(s)ds=\frac{\pi}{2}.

(154)

Description of
H_{\mathrm{pump}}(t)
. Physically, the Hamiltonian
H_{\mathrm{pump}}(t)
serves as a constant driving field for each environmental mode, creating a coherent state with high average photon numbers. More specifically,

H_{\mathrm{pump}}(t)=i\xi\left(\frac{t}{T}\right)\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\sigma_{i}^{z}\left(a_{i}^{\dagger}-a_{i}\right),

(155)

where
\xi(s)
is defined in Eq. (
154
).
If the system is initially in
\ket{0}^{\otimes n}
and the environment is initially in the vacuum state, we directly identify that this pumping Hamiltonian will drive the environmental mode to the coherent state
\ket{\alpha=T}
as

\displaystyle\ket{\psi(T)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}\exp\!\left(-i\int_{0}^{T}H_{\mathrm{pump}}(s)\,ds\right)\left(\ket{0}_{S}^{\otimes n}\otimes\ket{0}_{E}^{\otimes(n-1)}\right)

(156)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\exp\!\left(T\sigma_{i}^{z}(a_{i}^{\dagger}-a_{i})\right)\left(\ket{0}_{S}^{\otimes n}\otimes\ket{0}_{E}^{\otimes(n-1)}\right)

\displaystyle=\ket{0}_{S}^{\otimes n}\otimes\ket{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{T}}_{E},\qquad\ket{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{T}}_{E}:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n-1}\ket{\alpha=T}_{E_{i}}.

Here
\ket{\alpha=T}_{E_{i}}
is the coherent state of mode
i
with real displacement
T
; the scalar coherent amplitude
\alpha
is distinct from the bath-index label
(i,\nu)
.

Description of
H_{\mathrm{ex}}(t)
.
H_{\mathrm{ex}}(t)
acts only on the first qubit and applies a
\sigma_{1}^{x}
rotation on it.

H_{\mathrm{ex}}(t)=\xi(t-T)\sigma_{1}^{x}.

(157)

This pulse is supported on the singleton
\{1\}
and creates an excitation on the first qubit. Up to a global phase, the resulting state is
\ket{1_{1}0_{2}\cdots 0_{n}}\otimes\ket{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{T}}_{E}
.

Description of
H_{\mathrm{prop}}(t)
.This Hamiltonian leverages the excitations in the environment to rapidly propagate the excitations in the system from site
i=1
to site
i=T^{2}+1
. We construct
H_{\text{prop}}(t)
by first switching on an interaction between qubits 1, 2 and the first bath, then between qubits 2, 3 and the second bath and so on:

H_{\mathrm{prop}}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}g_{i}(t)\ket{0_{i}1_{i+1}}{\bra{1_{i}0_{i+1}}}\otimes a{{}_{i}}+\text{h.c.}=:\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}g_{i}(t)h_{i,i+1},

(158)

with
g_{i}(t)=\xi\bigl(T(t-T-1)-(i-1)\bigr)
. Intuitively, each environmental mode has an average photon number
T^{2}
. For a Fock state with exactly the same photon number, the propagation of excitation by
1
site using Jaynes–Cummings coupling Eq. (
158
) requires
1/T
time. When
T
is very large, the photon number fluctuation in the coherent state will be suppressed, and the coherent state should have a similar effect as the Fock state with the same average photon number. Therefore, we can propagate the excitation by
T^{2}
sites in
T
time. More concretely, we have

Lemma 21
.

Let
n\geq T^{2}+1
. If the system is initially in
\ket{0}^{\otimes n}
and the environment is initially in the vacuum state, the occupation probability at site
T^{2}+1
after evolution under
H(t)
remains bounded below by a nonzero constant. In other words, defining
\ket{\psi(t)}=U(t,0)(\ket{0}^{\otimes n}\otimes\ket{0}^{\otimes(n-1)})
, we have, for large enough
T
,

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\sigma_{T^{2}+1}^{{\dagger}}\sigma_{T^{2}+1}\ket{\psi(2T+1)}\bra{\psi(2T+1)}\right)\geq C,

with
C
a constant which does not depend on
T
and
n
.

Before proving this lemma, we establish a one-step Jaynes–Cummings estimate.

Lemma 22
.

Consider the Jaynes–Cummings Hamiltonian

H=\sigma^{\dagger}a+\sigma a^{\dagger},

(159)

where
\sigma=\ket{0}\!\bra{1}
acts on the qubit and
a
acts on a single bosonic mode. Tensor factors below are ordered as qubit then mode. For an interaction time
\Delta t>0
, define the transition-amplitude magnitude

A_{\mathrm{tr}}:=\left|\left(\bra{1}\otimes\bra{\alpha}\right)e^{-iH\Delta t}\left(\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\alpha}\right)\right|.

(160)

If
\alpha\Delta t=\pi/2
and
\alpha>0
, then

A_{\mathrm{tr}}\geq 1-\frac{C_{\mathrm{JC}}}{\alpha^{2}},\qquad C_{\mathrm{JC}}:=\frac{1}{2}.

(161)

Proof.

Let
D(\alpha)
be the displacement operator acting on the bosonic mode, so that
D(\alpha)\ket{0}=\ket{\alpha}
and
D(\alpha)^{\dagger}aD(\alpha)=a+\alpha
. We suppress its identity action on the qubit. In the displaced frame,

H^{\prime}:=D(\alpha)^{\dagger}HD(\alpha)=H_{0}+V,\qquad H_{0}:=\alpha\sigma^{x},\qquad V:=\sigma^{\dagger}a+\sigma a^{\dagger}.

(162)

Writing
\ket{\Phi_{0}}:=\ket{0}\otimes\ket{0}
, the choice
\Delta t=\pi/(2\alpha)
gives

e^{-iH_{0}\Delta t}\ket{\Phi_{0}}=-i\ket{1}\otimes\ket{0}=:\ket{\Phi_{\mathrm{id}}}.

(163)

Notice that we can now rewrite
A_{\mathrm{tr}}
as

A_{\mathrm{tr}}=\left|\bra{\Phi_{\mathrm{id}}}e^{-iH^{\prime}\Delta t}\ket{\Phi_{0}}\right|.

Applying Duhamel’s identity we obtain

\left(e^{-iH^{\prime}\Delta t}-e^{-iH_{0}\Delta t}\right)\ket{\Phi_{0}}=-i\int_{0}^{\Delta t}e^{-iH^{\prime}(\Delta t-s)}Ve^{-iH_{0}s}\ket{\Phi_{0}}\,ds.

(164)

Moreover,

e^{-iH_{0}s}\ket{\Phi_{0}}=\cos(\alpha s)\ket{0,0}-i\sin(\alpha s)\ket{1,0},\qquad Ve^{-iH_{0}s}\ket{\Phi_{0}}=-i\sin(\alpha s)\ket{0}\otimes\ket{1},

(165)

and hence

\left\|e^{-iH^{\prime}\Delta t}\ket{\Phi_{0}}-\ket{\Phi_{\mathrm{id}}}\right\|\leq\int_{0}^{\Delta t}|\sin(\alpha s)|\,ds=\frac{1}{\alpha}.

(166)

Both vectors are normalized, so

A_{\mathrm{tr}}=\left|\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{id}}|e^{-iH^{\prime}\Delta t}|\Phi_{0}\rangle\right|\geq\operatorname{Re}\!\left\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{id}}\middle|e^{-iH^{\prime}\Delta t}\middle|\Phi_{0}\right\rangle=1-\frac{1}{2}\left\|e^{-iH^{\prime}\Delta t}\ket{\Phi_{0}}-\ket{\Phi_{\mathrm{id}}}\right\|^{2}\geq 1-\frac{1}{2\alpha^{2}},

(167)

which proves the claim.
∎

We now prove Lemma
21
.

Proof.

After the pumping and excitation stages, the joint state is, up to a global phase,

\ket{\psi(T+1)}=\ket{q_{0}}\otimes\ket{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{T}}_{E},\qquad\ket{q_{0}}:=\ket{1_{1}0_{2}\cdots 0_{n}}.

(168)

For
j=1,\ldots,T^{2}
, define

U_{j}:=\exp\!\left(-i\frac{\pi}{2T}h_{j,j+1}\right),\qquad\ket{q_{j}}:=\ket{0_{1}\cdots 0_{j}1_{j+1}0_{j+2}\cdots 0_{n}}.

(169)

The pulse supports are ordered, so

U_{\mathrm{prop}}(2T+1,T+1)=U_{T^{2}}\cdots U_{2}U_{1}.

(170)

It is easy to verify that

\displaystyle\left|{\left(\bra{q_{T^{2}}}\otimes{}_{E}\bra{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{T}}\right)U_{T^{2}}\cdots U_{1}\left(\ket{q_{0}}\otimes\ket{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{T}}_{E}\right)}\right|

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{=}}\left|{{}_{E}\bra{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{T}}\left(\prod_{j=T^{2}}^{1}\bra{q_{j}}U_{j}\ket{q_{j-1}}\right)\ket{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{T}}_{E}}\right|

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu2\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{=}}\prod_{j=1}^{T^{2}}\left|{\left(\bra{0_{j}1_{j+1}}\otimes{}_{E_{j}}\bra{\alpha=T}\right)U_{j}\left(\ket{1_{j}0_{j+1}}\otimes\ket{\alpha=T}_{E_{j}}\right)}\right|,

(171)

where (1) follows from the fact that a branch that does not transfer the excitation from
j
to
j+1
is annihilated by the projection onto
\ket{q_{T^{2}}}
and (2) follows from the fact that
U_{j}
only acts on the sites
j,j+1
and the
j^{\text{th}}
bath.
On the two-dimensional subspace spanned by
\ket{1_{j}0_{j+1}}
and
\ket{0_{j}1_{j+1}}
,
h_{j,j+1}
is the Jaynes–Cummings Hamiltonian of Lemma
161
, and thus each factor in Eq. (
171
) is bounded below by Eq. (
161
), with
\alpha=T
and effective interaction time
\Delta t=\int g_{j}(t)\,dt=\pi/(2T)
. The physical duration of each pulse is
1/T
.

Let
P_{T^{2}+1}
denote the final occupation probability of site
T^{2}+1
. Projecting the environment onto
\ket{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{T}}_{E}
can only decrease that probability. Hence Lemma
161
, with
\alpha=T
, yields

\displaystyle P_{T^{2}+1}

\displaystyle\geq\left|\left(\bra{q_{T^{2}}}\otimes{}_{E}\bra{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{T}}\right)U_{\mathrm{prop}}(2T+1,T+1)\ket{\psi(T+1)}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{C_{\mathrm{JC}}}{T^{2}}\right)^{2T^{2}}.

(172)

For
T^{2}\geq 2C_{\mathrm{JC}}
, the inequality
\log(1-y)\geq-3y/2
with
y\leq 1/2
gives

P_{T^{2}+1}\geq e^{-3C_{\mathrm{JC}}}=:C>0,

(173)

where
C
is independent of
T
and
n
. This proves the lemma.
∎

According to Lemma
21
, we transport an excitation by distance
T^{2}
within time
2T+1
. Therefore, the particle transport velocity is

v_{\mathrm{S}}(T)=\Omega(T).

(174)

Compared with Eq. (
152
), we see that the derived Lieb-Robinson velocity is tight up to some constant. This example with windowed environmental memory growing linearly in time also violates the conventional linear light-cone structure, as we formally show below:

Lemma 23
.

Let
X=\{T^{2}+1\}
and
O_{X}=\sigma_{T^{2}+1}^{\dagger}\sigma_{T^{2}+1}
. Starting from the all-zero system state and the bath vacuum, evolution under
H_{X[l]}(t)
with
0\leq l<T^{2}
leaves the reduced system state equal to
(\ket{0}\!\bra{0})^{\otimes n}
at time
2T+1
.

Proof.

Since
l<T^{2}
, the singleton support
\{1\}
of
H_{\mathrm{ex}}(t)
does not intersect
X[l]
, so this pulse is absent from
H_{X[l]}(t)
. The retained pumping terms leave the all-zero system state unchanged. Thus, at time
T+1
, all system qubits remain in
\ket{0}
. Every propagation term annihilates the all-zero system state, so this remains true through time
2T+1
.
∎

Violation of the conventional linear light-cone structure
: We now consider the conventional linear light-cone structure, which can be obtained by considering a similar formula in Proposition 1 but with a constant
v_{\text{LR}}
. Using lemmas
21
and
23
, we obtain that for an initial system state
\rho_{S}=(\ket{0}\!\bra{0})^{\otimes n}
, environment state
\rho_{E}=(\ket{0}\!\bra{0})^{\otimes{n-1}}
, and observable
O_{X}=\sigma_{T^{2}+1}^{\dagger}\sigma_{T^{2}+1}

\text{Tr}(O_{X}U(2T+1,0)(\rho_{S}\otimes\rho_{E})U(0,2T+1))\geq C,\text{ and }\text{Tr}(O_{X}U_{X[l]}(2T+1,0)(\rho_{S}\otimes\rho_{E})U_{X[l]}(0,2T+1))=0,

where we have assumed
l<T^{2}
. This immediately implies that
\Delta_{O_{X}}(2T+1,0;l)\geq C
for any such
l
, thus violating the linear light-cone in Proposition 1 if
v_{\text{LR}}(t)
is uniformly bounded for all
t
.

XII.2
Coupled baths

Proposition 2 gives
v_{\mathrm{LR,c}}(t)=O(t)
in one dimension. We now construct a coupled-bath example that saturates this scaling in the exponential tail of the truncation error. We consider a one-dimensional chain of
n
qubits, indexed by
i=1,\ldots,n
, coupled to a bosonic field initially in the vacuum. Fix a positive integer
T
. In the Schrödinger picture the total Hamiltonian is

H_{\mathrm{tot}}(t)=H_{\mathrm{SE}}^{\mathrm{Sch}}(t)+H_{E}+H_{S}(t),

(175)

where

H_{E}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega a_{\omega}^{\dagger}a_{\omega}\,d\omega,\qquad H_{\mathrm{SE}}^{\mathrm{Sch}}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\bigl(R_{i}(t)A_{i}^{\dagger}+R_{i}^{\dagger}(t)A_{i}\bigr).

The spatial field operators are

A_{x}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{d\omega}{\sqrt{2\pi}}a_{\omega}e^{-i\omega x},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},

(176)

with
[a_{\omega},a_{\omega^{\prime}}^{\dagger}]=\delta(\omega-\omega^{\prime})
and hence
[A_{x},A_{y}^{\dagger}]=\delta(x-y)
. Each
R_{i}(t)
acts on qubits
i,i+1
. In the interaction picture with respect to
H_{E}
, we write
H(t)=H_{\mathrm{SE}}(t)+H_{S}(t)
, where

H_{\mathrm{SE}}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left(R_{i}(t)A_{i+t}^{\dagger}+{R}_{i}^{\dagger}(t)A_{i+t}\right).

(177)

As in the preceding subsection, use the bath label
\alpha=(i,\nu)
, with
\nu\in\{-,+\}
, and set
B_{(i,\nu)}(t)=A_{i+t}^{(\nu)}
with
A_{x}^{(-)}=A_{x}
and
A_{x}^{(+)}=A_{x}^{\dagger}
. The vacuum kernel has a strict light cone:

K_{(i,-),(i^{\prime},+)}(\tau)=\delta\bigl(\tau-(i^{\prime}-i)\bigr).

Proposition 2 implies a Lieb-Robinson velocity bounded by

v_{\mathrm{LR},c}(t)=O(t).

(178)

In the remainder of this subsection, we will construct an example that saturates the Lieb-Robinson bound in proposition 2. In particular, we will show that if a Lieb-Robinson bound, with exponential tails outside the light-cone, exists for this model then it must be
v_{\mathrm{LR},c}(t)=\Omega(t)
.

We use the same smooth pulse
\xi
as in the preceding subsection. Fix an integer
T
, take
n\geq T^{2}+2T+2
, and start from
\ket{0}_{S}^{\otimes n}\otimes\ket{0}_{E}
.
The protocol again has three stages:

H(t)=\begin{cases}H_{\mathrm{pump}}(t),&0\leq t<T,\\
H_{\mathrm{ex}}(t),&T\leq t<T+1,\\
H_{\mathrm{prop}}(t),&T+1\leq t<2T+1.\end{cases}

(179)

For the pumping stage, choose

H_{\mathrm{pump}}(t)=i\frac{2}{\pi}\xi\!\left(\frac{t}{T}\right)\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\sigma_{j}^{z}\left(a_{j+t}^{\dagger}-a_{j+t}\right).

(180)

Since
\sigma_{j}^{z}\ket{0_{j}}=\ket{0_{j}}
, this leaves the system unchanged and displaces the field to a coherent state
\ket{f}
satisfying

A_{x}\ket{f}=f(x)\ket{f},\qquad f(x)=\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\xi\!\left(\frac{x-j}{T}\right).

(181)

In the following, the field coordinates used during the propagation stage satisfy

T+2\leq x\leq T^{2}+2T+1.

(182)

Thus, the choice
n\geq T^{2}+2T+2
removes boundary truncation from the sum in Eq. (
181
). A shifted Riemann-sum estimate gives

\left|\frac{f(x)}{T}-1\right|\leq\frac{2\|\xi^{\prime}\|_{1}}{\pi T},

(183)

uniformly on this interval, where we used
\int_{0}^{1}\xi(u)\,du=\pi/2
.

The excitation pulse is

H_{\mathrm{ex}}(t)=\xi(t-T)\sigma_{1}^{x},

(184)

and it is supported on the singleton
\{1\}
. It maps the first qubit to
\ket{1}
, up to a global phase. For the propagation stage, choose

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{prop}}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{T^{2}}g_{i}(t)\left(J_{i}a_{i+t}+J_{i}^{\dagger}a_{i+t}^{\dagger}\right),

(185)

\displaystyle g_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=\xi\!\left(T(t-T-1)-(i-1)\right),

\displaystyle J_{i}

\displaystyle=\ket{0_{i}1_{i+1}}\!\bra{1_{i}0_{i+1}}.

Thus, on an active bond during propagation, the coefficient in the general coupling Hamiltonian is
R_{i}(t)=g_{i}(t)J_{i}^{\dagger}
.
The
i^{\text{th}}
pulse is supported on

t_{i}\leq t\leq t_{i}+\frac{1}{T},\qquad t_{i}:=T+1+\frac{i-1}{T}.

(186)

Conditioned on the excitation having reached site
i
, its state at the beginning of the
i^{\text{th}}
pulse is
\ket{1_{i}0_{i+1}}
. In the effective basis

\ket{0_{\mathrm{eff}}}:=\ket{1_{i}0_{i+1}},\qquad\ket{1_{\mathrm{eff}}}:=\ket{0_{i}1_{i+1}},

(187)

write
\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}:=\ket{0_{\mathrm{eff}}}\!\bra{1_{\mathrm{eff}}}
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{x}:=\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}+\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\dagger}
. On this subspace
J_{i}=\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\dagger}
; the pulse index on the effective operators is suppressed.

As in the preceding subsection, we can work in a displaced frame. Define the field displacement operator

D[f]:=\exp\!\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\left[f(x)a_{x}^{\dagger}-f(x)^{*}a_{x}\right]dx\right),

which acts only on the environment and satisfies

D[f]\ket{0}_{E}=\ket{f},\qquad D[f]^{\dagger}a_{x}D[f]=a_{x}+f(x).

Recall that
f
is defined in Eq. (
181
) and is real because
\xi
is real.
Immediately before the propagation stage, the joint state is, up to a global phase,

\ket{\psi(T+1)}=\ket{1_{1}0_{2}\cdots 0_{n}}\otimes\ket{f}
. In the displaced frame this becomes

D[f]^{\dagger}\ket{\psi(T+1)}=\ket{1_{1}0_{2}\cdots 0_{n}}\otimes\ket{0}_{E}.

During the
i^{\text{th}}
pulse, the Hamiltonian on the effective qubit and its input field is

H_{i}(t)=g_{i}(t)(\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\dagger}a_{i+t}+\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}a_{i+t}^{\dagger})
. Its displaced-frame Hamiltonian is

\widetilde{H}_{i}(t):=D[f]^{\dagger}H_{i}(t)D[f]=g_{i}(t)f(i+t)\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{x}+g_{i}(t)\left(\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\dagger}a_{i+t}+\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}a_{i+t}^{\dagger}\right).

(188)

The displaced field is in the vacuum and satisfies

[a_{i+t},a_{i+s}^{\dagger}]=\delta(t-s)
. Let
\rho_{i}(t)
denote the normalized reduced state of the effective qubit during pulse
i
, conditioned on arrival at site
i
at time
t_{i}
. The displacement acts only on the environment and therefore leaves this reduced state unchanged. Tracing out the displaced vacuum field gives
[
51
]

\displaystyle\frac{d\rho_{i}}{dt}

\displaystyle=-ig_{i}(t)f(i+t)[\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{x},\rho_{i}]+g_{i}(t)^{2}\mathcal{D}[\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}](\rho_{i}),

(189)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}](\rho)

\displaystyle:=\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}\rho\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\dagger}\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}},\rho\}.

For the
i^{\text{th}}
pulse, introduce the dimensionless local time
u:=T(t-t_{i})
. Then
0\leq u\leq 1
,
t=t_{i}+u/T
, and
g_{i}(t_{i}+u/T)=\xi(u)
. Define the rescaled state by
\widetilde{\rho}_{i}(u):=\rho_{i}(t_{i}+u/T)
. Thus, Eq. (
189
) becomes

\frac{d\widetilde{\rho}_{i}}{du}=-i\xi(u)\frac{f(i+t_{i}+u/T)}{T}[\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{x},\widetilde{\rho}_{i}]+\frac{\xi(u)^{2}}{T}\mathcal{D}[\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}](\widetilde{\rho}_{i}).

(190)

Let
\rho_{\mathrm{id}}(u)
solve the ideal equation

\frac{d\rho_{\mathrm{id}}}{du}=-i\xi(u)[\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{x},\rho_{\mathrm{id}}],\qquad\widetilde{\rho}_{i}(0)=\rho_{\mathrm{id}}(0)=\ket{0_{\mathrm{eff}}}\!\bra{0_{\mathrm{eff}}}.

(191)

The normalization of
\xi
gives

\rho_{\mathrm{id}}(1)=\ket{1_{\mathrm{eff}}}\!\bra{1_{\mathrm{eff}}}.

(192)

Let
\mathcal{E}_{i}(u,s)
denote the propagator of the actual rescaled master equation. Duhamel’s identity gives

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{i}(1)-\rho_{\mathrm{id}}(1)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{E}_{i}(1,u)\Bigg[

\displaystyle-i\xi(u)\left(\frac{f(i+t_{i}+u/T)}{T}-1\right)[\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{x},\rho_{\mathrm{id}}(u)]

\displaystyle+\frac{\xi(u)^{2}}{T}\mathcal{D}[\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}](\rho_{\mathrm{id}}(u))\Bigg]du.

(193)

The propagator
\mathcal{E}_{i}(1,u)
is trace-norm contractive on Hermitian operators. Using

\|[\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}^{x},\rho]\|_{1}\leq 2\|\rho\|_{1},\qquad\|\mathcal{D}[\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}](\rho)\|_{1}\leq 2\|\rho\|_{1},

(194)

together with Eq. (
183
) and
\|\rho_{\mathrm{id}}(u)\|_{1}=1
, we obtain directly from Eq. (
193
)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\rho}_{i}(1)-\rho_{\mathrm{id}}(1)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\left[2|\xi(u)|\left|\frac{f(i+t_{i}+u/T)}{T}-1\right|+\frac{2\xi(u)^{2}}{T}\right]du

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\left(\frac{4\|\xi^{\prime}\|_{1}\|\xi\|_{1}}{\pi}+2\|\xi\|_{2}^{2}\right)=:\frac{C_{\xi}}{T}.

(195)

For the target projector, Eq. (
195
) means that, conditioned on arrival at site
i
, the transfer succeeds with probability at least
1-C_{\xi}/T
. A failed transfer cannot reach the final site through any later pulse, and different pulses couple to disjoint vacuum field increments. The same one-pulse bound therefore applies successively to all
T^{2}
transfers. Let
\rho_{S}(t)
denote the reduced state of the system at time
t
. The final occupation therefore obeys

\operatorname{Tr}\!\left[\rho_{S}(2T+1)\sigma_{T^{2}+1}^{\dagger}\sigma_{T^{2}+1}\right]\geq\left(1-\frac{C_{\xi}}{T}\right)^{T^{2}}\geq e^{-2C_{\xi}T}

(196)

for
T\geq 2C_{\xi}
. This estimate supplies an
e^{-O(T)}
lower bound on the final occupation probability; it does not establish a
T
-independent lower bound.

Finally, choose

X=\{T^{2}+1\},\qquad O_{X}=\sigma_{T^{2}+1}^{\dagger}\sigma_{T^{2}+1},\qquad l=T^{2}-1.

(197)

The truncated Hamiltonian does not contain
H_{\mathrm{ex}}
, so its expectation of
O_{X}
is zero. Hence

\Delta_{O_{X}}(2T+1,0;T^{2}-1)\geq e^{-2C_{\xi}T}.

(198)

Fix
a_{E}^{\prime}>a_{E}
as in Proposition 2. Its bound gives

e^{-2C_{\xi}T}\leq f_{c}(2T+1,T^{2}-1)\exp\!\left[\frac{(2T+1)v_{\mathrm{LR,c}}(2T+1)-(T^{2}-1)}{p_{+}a_{E}^{\prime}}\right].

(199)

For
d=1
,
\log f_{c}(2T+1,T^{2}-1)=O(\log T)
. Taking logarithms therefore yields

(2T+1)v_{\mathrm{LR,c}}(2T+1)\geq T^{2}-1-2p_{+}a_{E}^{\prime}C_{\xi}T-O(\log T),

(200)

and consequently

v_{\mathrm{LR,c}}(2T+1)=\Omega(T).

(201)

Together with Eq. (
178
), this proves that the scaling of the exponential tail is tight up to constants in one dimension.

XIII
Markovian Dilations for local observables with independent memory

As an application of the Lieb-Robinson bound, we analyze the star-to-chain transformation which approximates the independent environment by a finite number of discrete modes with the quality of the approximation governed by the number of modes. Typically, for a system with
n
qubits, in order to approximate the system state to an
O(1)
precision, we need a number of modes per independent bath of the environment that scale as
\text{poly}(n)
. However, for geometrically local many-body open quantum systems, we would expect that to approximate local observables to an
O(1)
precision, the number of bath modes needed should be independent of
n
. In this section, we show that this qualitative expectation can be made precise using the Lieb Robinson bounds derived in this paper. We first describe the star-to-chain transformation, and review the analysis of its accuracy without using geometrical locality of the system
[
48
]
. Then, we combine this analysis with the Lieb-Robinson bounds to provide tighter results for local observables.

XIII.1
Preliminaries

Throughout this section, we will assume that the environment is initially in vacuum state with
L=2
, and
B_{\alpha}(t)=A_{\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}^{(\nu)}(t)
. For notational convenience, throughout this section we will write
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
as
\alpha
and
A_{\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}^{(\nu)}(t)
as
A_{\alpha}^{(\nu)}(t)
, respectively. Consequently, it follows that

K_{A_{\alpha}^{(\nu)},A^{(\nu^{\prime})}_{{\alpha^{\prime}}}}(t)=\delta_{\alpha,{\alpha^{\prime}}}\delta_{\nu,-}\delta_{\nu^{\prime},+}V_{\alpha}(t),

and
\text{V}_{\alpha}(t-t^{\prime})=[A_{\alpha}(t),A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})]
.
We will also define by
\text{V}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
as the kernel that upper bounds
\text{V}_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha
and assume that
\text{TV}(\text{V})<\infty
.
The system-environment coupling can be correspondingly written as

H_{\text{SE}}(t)=\sum_{\alpha}\left(A_{\alpha}(t)R_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(t)+A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(t)R_{\alpha}(t)\right).

We introduce the
spectral density function
,
\hat{\text{V}}_{\alpha}
, for the
\alpha^{\text{th}}
bath as the fourier transform of the Kernel
\hat{\text{V}}_{\alpha}

\hat{\text{V}}_{\alpha}(\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\text{V}}_{\alpha}(t)e^{i\omega t}dt.

We note that
\hat{\text{V}}_{\alpha}
is a bounded and non-negative function — the fact that it is bounded follows straightforwardly:

\left|{\hat{\text{V}}_{\alpha}(\omega)}\right|\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}(t)}\right|dt=\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha}).

To see that
\hat{\text{V}}_{\alpha}(\omega)
is non-negative, note that for any smooth function
h(t)
that
\to 0
faster than any polynomial in
1/t
as
\left|{t}\right|\to\infty
,

\displaystyle 0\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h(t)[A_{\alpha}(t),A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})]h^{*}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}dt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h(t)\text{V}_{\alpha}(t-t^{\prime})h^{*}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}dt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left|{\hat{h}(\omega)}\right|^{2}\hat{\text{V}}_{\alpha}(\omega)\frac{d\omega}{2\pi},

where
\hat{h}(\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h(t)e^{-i\omega t}dt
. Now, since this is true for any
\hat{h}(\omega)
, we conclude that
\hat{\text{V}}_{\alpha}(\omega)\geq 0
for all
\omega
. It will also be convenient to work with an explicit second quantization of the Hilbert space of the baths — for describing the
\alpha^{\text{th}}
bath’s Hilbert space, it is always possible to introduce annihilation operators
a_{\alpha,\omega}
, where
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
, which satisfy the canonical commutation relations
[a_{\alpha,\omega},a^{\dagger}_{\alpha^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}}]=\delta_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\delta(\omega-\omega^{\prime})
such that

A_{\alpha}(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{v}_{\alpha}(\omega)a_{\alpha,\omega}e^{-i\omega t}\frac{d\omega}{\sqrt{2\pi}},

where
\hat{v}_{\alpha}(\omega)=\sqrt{\hat{\text{V}}_{\alpha}(\omega)}
— this can be verified by noting that this representation of
A_{\alpha}(t)
reproduces the original commutation relation
[A_{\alpha}(t),A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})]=\text{V}_{\alpha}(t-t^{\prime})
. We will refer to
a_{\alpha,\omega}
as the frequency-domain annihilation operator, at frequency
\omega
, for the
\alpha^{\text{th}}
bath consistent with their physical interpretation as annihilating an excitation at frequency
\omega
in the bath. In terms of
a_{\alpha,\omega}
, following section
VII.3
, the regularized annihilation operator is given by

A_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\eta_{\delta}(t-s)A_{\alpha}(s)ds=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)a_{\alpha,\omega}e^{-i\omega t}\frac{d\omega}{\sqrt{2\pi}},

where
\hat{v}^{\delta}_{\alpha}(\omega)=\hat{v}_{\alpha}(\omega)\hat{\eta}(\omega\delta)
with
\hat{\eta}(\omega)=\int_{-1}^{1}\eta(t)e^{i\omega t}dt
being the Fourier transform of the mollifier
\eta
introduced in section
VII.3
. The mollified kernel,
{\text{V}}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t-t^{\prime})=[A_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t),A_{\alpha}^{\delta\dagger}(t^{\prime})]
, is related to
\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)
via

\text{V}^{\delta}_{\alpha}(\tau)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}e^{-i\omega\tau}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}.

We will also define
\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(t-t^{\prime})=[A_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t),A_{\alpha}^{\delta^{\prime}\dagger}(t^{\prime})]
which can be expressed as

\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(\tau)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{v}^{\delta}_{\alpha}(\omega)\hat{v}^{\delta^{\prime}*}_{\alpha}(\omega)e^{-i\omega\tau}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}.

XIII.2
Analyzing error in star-to-chain transformation

To perform the star-to-chain transformation, there will be three steps — first, we will regularize the model as already described in section
VII.3
. Next, we will introduce a hard frequency cut-off on the regularization. Finally, we will apply the star-to-chain transformation as laid out in Ref.
[
45
]
. We will analyze the error incurred in each of these steps separately.

Regularization error
. We begin by using the result of section
VII.3
to provide an explicit bound for the regularization error.

Lemma 24
.

For
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, observable
O
with
\left\|{O}\right\|\leq 1
and sufficiently small
\delta
,

\displaystyle|{\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta}(t,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle-\lim_{\delta\to 0}\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta}(t,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle}|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq 4t\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|\lambda_{0}(2\delta)+4t\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|\big[1+\big(2^{2}t+2^{4}t\textnormal{TV}(\textnormal{V})\big)\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|\big]\lambda_{1}(2\delta),

where

\lambda_{0}(\delta)=4\sum_{t^{\prime}\in\{0,t\}}\textnormal{TV}(\textnormal{V}_{c};[t^{\prime}-\delta,t^{\prime}+\delta]\big)\text{ and }\lambda_{1}(\delta)=2\delta\textnormal{TV}(\textnormal{V}),

where
\textnormal{V}_{c}
is the continuous part of
V
.

Proof.

We begin by considering
\delta,\delta^{\prime}>0
and consider the error
\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left|{\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta}(t,0)-\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t,0)|{\rho_{S},0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|,

\displaystyle\leq\Theta_{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)\bigg(\left|{\int_{0}^{t}\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t,s)(A_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\delta}(s)-A_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\delta^{\prime}}(s))R_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta}(s,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle ds}\right|+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left|{\int_{0}^{t}\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t,s)(A_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\delta\dagger}(s)-A_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\delta^{\prime}\dagger}(s))R_{\alpha,\sigma}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta}(s,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle ds}\right|\bigg),

\displaystyle\leq\Theta_{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)\big(\left|{\Gamma^{(1)}_{\alpha,\sigma}}\right|+\left|{\Gamma^{(2)}_{\alpha,\sigma}}\right|+\left|{\Gamma^{(3)}_{\alpha,\sigma}}\right|+\left|{\Gamma^{(4)}_{\alpha,\sigma}}\right|\big).

(202)

where, from Wick’s theorem,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(1)}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\big(\text{V}^{\delta,\delta}_{\alpha}(s-s^{\prime})-\text{V}^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha}(s-s^{\prime})\big)f_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(1)}(s,s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}ds\text{ with }

\displaystyle f_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(1)}(s,s^{\prime})=\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t,s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta}(s,s^{\prime})R_{\alpha,l}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta}(s^{\prime},0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(2)}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{s}^{t}\big(\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(s-s^{\prime})-\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}(s-s^{\prime})\big)f_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(2)}(s,s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}ds\text{ with }

\displaystyle f_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(2)}(s,s^{\prime})=\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t,s^{\prime})R_{\alpha,\sigma}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(s^{\prime},s)R_{\alpha,l}^{\dagger}\mathcal{U}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime},0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(3)}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\big(\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\delta}(s^{\prime}-s)-\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}-s)\big)f_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(3)}(s,s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}ds\text{ where }

\displaystyle f_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(3)}(s,s^{\prime})=\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t,s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}\mathcal{U}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,s^{\prime})R_{\alpha,r}^{\dagger}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta}(s^{\prime},0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(4)}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{s}^{t}(\text{V}^{\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha}(s^{\prime}-s)-\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta^{\prime},\delta}(s^{\prime}-s))f_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(4)}(s,s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}ds\text{ where }

\displaystyle f_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(4)}(s,s^{\prime})=\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t,s^{\prime})R_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}\mathcal{U}^{\delta^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime},s)R_{\alpha,l}\mathcal{U}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle.

(203)

We can now bound
\Gamma_{\alpha,\sigma}^{(j)}
using lemma
30
— the bound for each
j
is identical, so we perform the calculation, to illustrate the procedure, only for
j=1
. We begin by noting from lemma
3
, on the test-function space
\text{PC}_{\{0,t\}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and for sufficiently small
\delta,\delta^{\prime}
,
\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\delta}-\text{V}^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha}
is
\lambda_{0}(\Delta),\lambda_{1}(\Delta)
-small where
\Delta=2\max(\delta,\delta^{\prime})
and

\lambda_{0}(\Delta)=4\sum_{t^{\prime}\in\{0,t\}}\text{TV}(\text{V}_{c};[t^{\prime}-\Delta,t^{\prime}+\Delta])\text{ and }\lambda_{1}(\Delta)=2\Delta\textnormal{TV}(\text{V}).

To see this, note that for any
f\in\text{PC}_{\{0,t\}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\left|{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big(\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\delta}(s)-\text{V}^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha}(s)\big)f(s)ds}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big(\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\delta}(s)-\text{V}_{\alpha}(s)\big)f(s)ds}\right|+\left|{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big(\text{V}^{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}_{\alpha}(s)-\text{V}_{\alpha}(s)\big)f(s)ds}\right|,

and then use lemma
3
. Next, we show that
f^{(1)}_{\alpha,\sigma}
is both bounded and differentiable with respect to its arguments with bounded bounded partial derivatives. The fact that it is bounded follows immediately from Eq.
203
— in particular,

\displaystyle|{f^{(1)}_{\alpha,\sigma}(s,s^{\prime})}|=|{\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t,s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta}(s,s^{\prime})R_{\alpha,l}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta}(s^{\prime},0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle}|\leq\left\|{R_{\alpha}}\right\|^{2}\leq 1.

(204)

Next, we compute its derivatives —

\displaystyle\left|{\partial_{1}f^{(1)}_{\alpha,\sigma}(s,s^{\prime})}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Theta_{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha^{\prime})\bigg(\left|{\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t,s)[\mathcal{C}_{h_{\alpha^{\prime}}},R_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}]\mathcal{U}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,s^{\prime})R_{\alpha,l}\mathcal{U}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime},0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|+

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\left|{\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t,s)(A_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(s)R_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\dagger}+A_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta^{\prime}\dagger}(s)R_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}})R_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}\mathcal{U}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,s^{\prime})R_{\alpha,l}\mathcal{U}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime},0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|+

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\left|{\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t,s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}(A_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta}(s)R_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\dagger}+A_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\delta\dagger}(s)R_{\alpha^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}})\mathcal{U}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,s^{\prime})R_{\alpha,l}\mathcal{U}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime},0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|\bigg),

\displaystyle\leq 4\Theta_{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha^{\prime})\left\|{h_{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right\|\left\|{R_{\alpha}}\right\|^{2}+2^{4}\Theta_{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha^{\prime})\left\|{R_{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right\|^{2}\left\|{R_{\alpha}}\right\|^{2}\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha}),

\displaystyle\leq\big(2^{2}+2^{4}\text{TV}(\text{V})\big)\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|.

(205)

Similarly,

\displaystyle\left|{\partial_{2}f^{(1)}_{\alpha,\sigma}(s,s^{\prime})}\right|\leq\big(2^{2}+2^{4}\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})\big)\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|.

(206)

Using lemma
30
together with the estimates from Eqs.
204
,
205
and
206
, we then obtain that

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(1)}_{\alpha,\sigma}\leq t\lambda_{0}(\Delta)+\lambda_{1}(\Delta)\big[1+\big(2^{2}t+2^{4}t\text{TV}(\text{V})\big)\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|\big].

(207)

A similar bound can be calculated for
\Gamma^{(2)}_{\alpha,\sigma},\Gamma^{(3)}_{\alpha,\sigma},\Gamma^{(4)}_{\alpha,\sigma}
. We then obtain from Eq.
202
that

\left|{\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta}(t,0)-\mathcal{U}_{X}^{\delta^{\prime}}(t,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|\leq 4t\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|\lambda_{0}(\Delta)+4t\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|\big[1+\big(2^{2}t+2^{4}t\text{TV}(\text{V})\big)\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|\big]\lambda_{1}(\Delta).

Taking the limit of
\delta^{\prime}\to 0
, which amounts to setting
\Delta=2\max(\delta,\delta^{\prime})
to
2\delta
, we obtain the lemma statement.
∎

Analyzing the frequency cutoff
. Next, we will introduce a hard frequency cut-off on the regularized model. Given a frequency-cutoff
\omega_{c}
, we will introduce the bath annihilation operator

A_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(t)=\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)a_{\alpha,\omega}e^{-i\omega t}\frac{d\omega}{\sqrt{2\pi}},

and define the Hamiltonian
H_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}
by

\displaystyle H^{\delta,\omega_{c}}_{\mathcal{B}}(t)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}h_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}\big(R_{\alpha}^{\dagger}A_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(t)+\text{h.c.}\big).

(208)

We will now consider the error between the regularized model
H^{\delta}_{\mathcal{B}}(t)
and the regularized model with frequency cut-off
H^{\delta,\omega_{c}}_{\mathcal{B}}(t)
. Physically, due to the regularization,
\hat{v}^{\delta}_{\alpha}(\omega)
decreases to
0
as
\omega\to\infty
and consequently if
\omega_{c}
is sufficiently large, we expect to incur a small error on introducing the frequency cut-off. For this purpose of analyzing this error, it is convenient to first introduce a bound on
\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|
.

Lemma 25
.

For any
\omega_{c}>0
,

\int_{\left|{\omega}\right|\geq\omega_{c}}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\leq\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\delta^{2}\omega_{c}}\textnormal{TV}(\textnormal{V}_{\alpha})\exp\bigg[-\bigg(\frac{{\omega_{c}}\delta}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg],

where
\gamma_{0}
is a constant.

Proof.

To prove this lemma, we note that
\hat{\eta}(\omega)
, the fourier transform of the standard mollifier
\eta
defined in Eq.
32
, is bounded above by any polynomial in
\omega^{-1}
— more precisely, for any
\omega

\left|{\hat{\eta}(\omega)}\right|=\left|{\int_{-1}^{1}\eta(t)e^{i\omega t}dt}\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{=}}\left|{\frac{1}{\omega^{k}}\int_{-1}^{1}\eta^{(k)}(t)e^{i\omega t}dt}\right|\leq\frac{2\left\|{\eta^{(k)}}\right\|_{\infty}}{\left|{\omega}\right|^{k}},

where, in (1), we have used integration by parts
k
times together with the fact that since
\eta(t)
is a smooth and compact function on
[-1,1]
,
\eta^{(k)}(\pm 1)=0
. Now, for the standard mollifier,
\eta^{(k)}
can be explicitly evaluated and upper bounded to obtain
[
78
]
(here,
A_{0}
is the constant, defined in Eq.
32
, chosen such that
\int_{[-1,1]}\eta(x)dx=1
)

\left\|{\eta^{(k)}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq A_{0}16^{k}k^{2k}\text{ and therefore }\left|{\hat{\eta}(\omega)}\right|\leq\left|{\hat{\eta}(\omega)}\right|\leq 2A_{0}\bigg(\frac{16k^{2}}{\left|{\omega}\right|}\bigg)^{k}.

Since this is true for any
k
, we can choose
k
to be the smallest integer such that
k\leq(\left|{\omega}\right|/16e)^{1/2}
(and since
k
is an integer, it is also true that
(\left|{\omega}\right|/16e)^{1/2}-1\leq k
) and therefore

\displaystyle\left|{\hat{\eta}(\omega)}\right|\leq\frac{2A_{0}}{e}\exp\bigg[-\bigg(\frac{\left|{\omega}\right|}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg].

(209)

With this, we can upper bound
\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}
by noting that

\displaystyle\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left|{\hat{\textnormal{V}}_{\alpha}(\omega)}\right|\left|{\hat{\eta}(\omega\delta)}\right|^{2}=\left|{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\text{V}}_{\alpha}(t)e^{i\omega t}dt}\right|\left|{\hat{\eta}(\omega\delta)}\right|^{2},

\displaystyle\leq\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})\left|{\hat{\eta}(\omega\delta)}\right|^{2}\leq\frac{4A_{0}^{2}}{e^{2}}\textnormal{TV}(\textnormal{V}_{\alpha})\exp\bigg[-2\bigg(\frac{\left|{\omega}\right|}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg].

(210)

We can now use this bound to obtain the bound in the lemma statement:

\displaystyle\left|{\int_{\left|{\omega}\right|\geq\omega_{c}}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2A_{0}^{2}}{e^{2}\pi}\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha}){\int_{\left|{\omega}\right|\geq\omega_{c}}\exp\bigg[-2\bigg(\frac{\delta\left|{\omega}\right|}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg]d\omega},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}\frac{2^{9}A_{0}^{2}M_{0}}{e^{2}\delta^{2}\pi}\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})\int_{\left|{\omega}\right|\geq\omega_{c}}\exp\bigg[-\bigg(\frac{\delta\left|{\omega}\right|}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg]\frac{d\omega}{\omega^{2}},

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{9}A_{0}^{2}M_{0}}{e^{2}\delta^{2}\pi}\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})\exp\bigg[-\bigg(\frac{\delta{\omega_{c}}}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg]\int_{\left|{\omega}\right|\geq\omega_{c}}\frac{d\omega}{\omega^{2}},

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{10}A_{0}^{2}M_{0}}{e^{2}\delta^{2}\omega_{c}\pi}\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})\exp\bigg[-\bigg(\frac{\delta{\omega_{c}}}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg]

(211)

where in (1) we have introduced the constant
M_{0}=\sup_{\omega>0}\omega^{2}e^{-\sqrt{\omega}}
and used that
e^{-\sqrt{\delta\left|{\omega}\right|/16e}}\leq 2^{8}e^{2}M_{0}/\left|{\omega}\right|^{2}\delta^{2}
. Noting that
\gamma_{0}=2^{10}A_{0}^{2}M_{0}/e^{2}\pi
is a constant, we obtain the lemma statement.
∎

To analyze this error, it is convenient to introduce the following lemma.

Lemma 26
.

Given
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, suppose

H_{\mathcal{B}}(t)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}h_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}(A_{\alpha}(t)R_{\alpha}^{\dagger}+\textnormal{h.c.})\text{ and }\tilde{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(t)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}h_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{A}_{\alpha}(t)R_{\alpha}^{\dagger}+\textnormal{h.c.})

such that
\textnormal{V}_{\alpha}(t):=[A_{\alpha}(t),A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(0)],\tilde{\textnormal{V}}_{\alpha}(t):=[\tilde{A}_{\alpha}(t),\tilde{A}_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(0)]
are both continuous functions, then for any system observable
O
and initial system state
\rho(0)
,

\left|{\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,0)-\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|\leq{2}t^{2}\left\|{O}\right\|\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}\bigg(\big(\sqrt{\textnormal{V}_{\alpha}(0)}+\sqrt{\tilde{\textnormal{V}}_{\alpha}(0)}\big)\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\sqrt{[\delta A_{\alpha}(s),\delta A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(s)]}\bigg),

where
\delta A_{\alpha}(s)=A_{\alpha}(s)-\tilde{A}_{\alpha}(s)
.

Proof.

We begin by the standard first-order perturbation theory expression:

\displaystyle\left|{\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,0)-\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad=\left|{\int_{0}^{t}\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)\big(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(s)-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}(s)\big)\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,0)|{\rho(0)}\rrangle ds}\right|,

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\Theta_{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)\bigg(\left|{\int_{0}^{t}\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)\delta\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle ds}\right|+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left|{\int_{0}^{t}\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)\delta A_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}(s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle ds}\right|\bigg),

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\Theta_{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)\bigg(\left|{\int_{0}^{t}\int_{s}^{t}\llangle{I_{E}}|A_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}^{\dagger}(s^{\prime})\delta A_{\alpha,r}(s)|{0_{E}}\rrangle\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s^{\prime})R_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime},s)R^{\dagger}_{\alpha,\sigma}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle ds^{\prime}ds}\right|+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\ \left|{\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\llangle{I_{E}}|\delta A_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)\tilde{A}_{\alpha,l}^{\dagger}(s^{\prime})|{0_{E}}\rrangle\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,s^{\prime})R_{\alpha,l}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime},0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle ds^{\prime}ds}\right|+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\left|{\int_{0}^{t}\int_{s}^{t}\llangle{I_{E}}|A_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})\delta A_{\alpha,l}^{\dagger}(s)|{0_{E}}\rrangle\llangle{O_{X},I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s^{\prime})R^{\dagger}_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime},s)R_{\alpha,l}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle ds^{\prime}ds}\right|+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\left|{\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\llangle{I_{E}}|\delta A_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}(s)\tilde{A}_{\alpha,r}(s^{\prime})|{0_{E}}\rrangle\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(t,s)R_{\alpha,\sigma}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{B}}(s,s^{\prime})R_{\alpha,r}^{\dagger}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}(s^{\prime},0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle ds^{\prime}ds}\right|\bigg),

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\Theta_{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)\bigg(\sup_{s,s^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\left|{\llangle{I_{E}}|A_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}^{\dagger}(s^{\prime})\delta A_{\alpha,r}(s)|{0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|+\sup_{s,s^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\left|{\llangle{I_{E}}|\delta A_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)\tilde{A}_{\alpha,l}^{\dagger}(s^{\prime})|{0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\sup_{s,s^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\left|{\llangle{I_{E}}|A_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})\delta A_{\alpha,l}^{\dagger}(s)|{0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|+\sup_{s,s^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\left|{\llangle{I_{E}}|\delta A_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}(s)\tilde{A}_{\alpha,r}(s^{\prime})|{0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|\bigg)t^{2}\left\|{R_{\alpha}}\right\|^{2}.

(212)

Now, we note that

\displaystyle\sup_{s,s^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\left|{\llangle{I_{E}}|A_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}^{\dagger}(s^{\prime})\delta A_{\alpha,r}(s)|{0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{s,s^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\left|{\bra{0_{E}}\delta A_{\alpha}(s)A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(s^{\prime})\ket{0_{E}}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{s,s^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\sqrt{\llangle{0_{E}}|\delta A_{\alpha}(s)\delta A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(s)\ket{0_{E}}}\sqrt{\llangle{0_{E}}|A_{\alpha}(s)A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(s)\ket{0_{E}}},

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\text{V}_{\alpha}(0)}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\sqrt{[\delta A_{\alpha}(s),\delta A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(s)]}.

(213)

Proceeding similarly, we can also obtain that

\displaystyle\sup_{s,s^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\left|{\llangle{I_{E}}|A_{\alpha,\sigma^{\prime}}^{\dagger}(s^{\prime})\delta A_{\alpha,r}(s)|{0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|\leq\sqrt{\text{V}_{\alpha}(0)}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\sqrt{[\delta A_{\alpha}(s),\delta A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(s)]},

\displaystyle\sup_{s,s^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\left|{\llangle{I_{E}}|\delta A_{\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}(s)\tilde{A}_{\alpha,r}(s^{\prime})|{0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|,\sup_{s,s^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\left|{\llangle{I_{E}}|\delta A_{\alpha,\sigma}(s)\tilde{A}_{\alpha,l}^{\dagger}(s^{\prime})|{0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|\leq\sqrt{\tilde{\text{V}}_{\alpha}(0)}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\sqrt{[\delta A_{\alpha}(s),\delta A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(s)]}.

(214)

Substituting Eqs.
213
and
214
into Eq.
212
, we obtain the lemma statement.
∎

We emphasize that a similar result also appears in Ref.
[
74
]
.
We can now use lemma
25
and
26
to obtain the following lemma quantifying the error made on introducing frequency cut-off.

Lemma 27
.

For any
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and
\omega_{c}>0
:

\displaystyle\left|{\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta}(t,0)-\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(t,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|\leq\frac{4\sqrt{\gamma_{0}}}{\sqrt{\delta^{3}\omega_{c}}}t^{2}\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|\left\|{O}\right\|\textnormal{TV}(\textnormal{V})\exp\bigg[-\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\frac{{\omega_{c}}\delta}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg],

(215)

where
\gamma_{0}
is the constant introduced in lemma
25
.

Proof.

We will use lemma
26
— we recall that
\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t-t^{\prime})=[A_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t),A_{\alpha}^{\delta\dagger}(t^{\prime})]=\big(\text{V}_{\alpha}\star\eta_{\delta}\star\eta_{\delta}\big)(t-t^{\prime})
and hence we obtain that

\left|{\text{V}^{\delta}_{\alpha}(t)}\right|\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}(t-\tau)}\right|\left|{(\rho_{\delta}\star\rho_{\delta})(\tau)}\right|d\tau\leq\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})\left\|{\rho_{\delta}\star\rho_{\delta}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})\frac{\left\|{\rho}\right\|_{1}}{\delta}=\frac{\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})}{\delta},

and

\displaystyle\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(t)}\right|\leq\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}=\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(0)}\right|\leq\frac{\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})}{\delta}.

(216)

Next, we obtain from lemma
25
that

\displaystyle\left|{[\delta A_{\alpha}(s),\delta A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(s)]}\right|=\int_{\left|{\omega}\right|\geq\omega_{c}}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\leq\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\delta^{2}\omega_{c}}\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})\exp\bigg[-\bigg(\frac{\omega_{c}\delta}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg],

(217)

where
\gamma_{0}
is the constant introduced in lemma
25
. Using lemma
26
, we then obtain that

\left|{\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}^{\delta}(t,0)-\mathcal{U}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(t,0)|{\sigma_{0},0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|\leq\frac{4\sqrt{\gamma_{0}}}{\sqrt{\delta^{3}\omega_{c}}}t^{2}\left\|{O}\right\|\bigg(\sum_{\alpha}\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})\bigg)\exp\bigg[-\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\frac{{\omega_{c}}\delta}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg].

Upper bounding
\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})\leq\text{TV}(\text{V})
, we obtain the lemma.
∎

Analysis of the star-to-chain transformation
. Having introduced this frequency cut-off, we now generate a set of
N_{m}
modes in the bath by performing a Lanczos iteration
[
45
]
with annihilation operators
b_{\alpha,1},b_{\alpha,2}\dots b_{\alpha,N_{m}}
where

b_{\alpha,k}=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{N}_{\alpha,k}}\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}p_{\alpha,k}(\omega)\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)a_{\alpha,\omega}d\omega\text{ with }\mathfrak{N}_{\alpha,k}=\bigg[\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\left|{p_{\alpha,k}(\omega)}\right|^{2}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}d\omega\bigg]^{1/2},

and
p_{\alpha,k}(\omega)
is a
(k-1)^{\text{th}}
degree polynomial generated recursively via the rule:
p_{\alpha,1}(\omega)=1
,
B_{\alpha,0}=0
,

\displaystyle i)\ A_{\alpha,j}=\frac{\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\omega p_{\alpha,j}^{2}(\omega)\left|{\hat{v}^{\delta}_{\alpha}(\omega)}\right|^{2}d\omega}{\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}p_{\alpha,j}^{2}(\omega)\left|{\hat{v}^{\delta}_{\alpha}(\omega)}\right|^{2}d\omega},

\displaystyle ii)\ p_{\alpha,j+1}(\omega)=(\omega-A_{\alpha,j})p_{\alpha,j}(\omega)-B_{\alpha,j-1}p_{\alpha,j-1}(\omega),

\displaystyle iii)\ B_{\alpha,j}=\frac{\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}p_{\alpha,j+1}^{2}(\omega)\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}(\omega)}\right|^{2}d\omega}{\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}p_{\alpha,j}^{2}(\omega)\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}(\omega)}\right|^{2}d\omega},

(218)

where we use the convention that
p_{\alpha,0}(\omega)=0
. It can be easily verified that the modes so generated are independent i.e. they satisfy the canonical commutation relation
[b_{\alpha,j},b_{\alpha^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{\dagger}]=\delta_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}
.

Finding the chain hamiltonian
. Having generated the
N_{m}
modes as in step 2, we then obtain a Hamiltonian of the bath in between these modes — for the
\alpha^{\text{th}}
bath, this is given by

H_{\alpha,E}=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{m}}\omega_{\alpha,j}b_{\alpha,j}^{\dagger}b_{\alpha,j}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{m}-1}t_{\alpha,j}\big(b_{\alpha,j}^{\dagger}b_{\alpha,j+1}+\text{h.c.}\big)\text{ with }\omega_{\alpha,j}=A_{\alpha,j}\text{ and }t_{\alpha,j}=\sqrt{B_{\alpha,j-1}}.

The approximate system environment Hamiltonian corresponding to
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
obtained after keeping
N_{m}
modes of the star-to-chain transformation would then be given by

\displaystyle H_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}h_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}\big(R_{\alpha}^{\dagger}A_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)+\text{h.c.}\big),

(219)

where

A_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)=\mathfrak{N}_{\alpha,1}e^{iH_{\alpha,E}t}b_{\alpha,1}e^{-iH_{\alpha,E}t}

Next, we consider the error between the regularized model with a frequency cutoff, given by the Hamiltonian
H_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(t)
, defined in Eq.
208
and the Hamiltonian
H_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)
in Eq.
219
. For this, we will need the following lemma from Ref.

Lemma 28

(Ref.
[
48
]
)
.

Suppose
\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(\omega;t)
is such that

A_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)=\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(\omega;t)a_{\alpha,\omega}\frac{d\omega}{\sqrt{2\pi}},

then

\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(\omega;t)-\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)e^{-i\omega t}}\right|^{2}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\leq 2N_{m}^{2}\bigg(\frac{2e\omega_{c}t}{N_{m}}\bigg)^{N_{m}}\bigg(\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\bigg)

Lemma 29
.

For any
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and
N_{m}>0
:

\left|{\llangle{O,I_{E}}|\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(t,0)-\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t,0)|{\rho_{S}(0),0_{E}}\rrangle}\right|\leq 2\sqrt{2}t^{2}\left|{\mathcal{B}}\right|\left\|{O}\right\|\textnormal{TV}(\textnormal{V})\frac{N_{m}}{\delta}\bigg(\frac{2e\omega_{c}t}{N_{m}}\bigg)^{N_{m}/2}

Proof.

We will again use lemma
26
. We begin by noting that

\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(0)}\right|=\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(0)}\right|\leq\frac{\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})}{\delta}.

To bound
\left|{[\delta A_{\alpha}(s),\delta A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(s)]}\right|
, where
\delta A_{\alpha}(s)=A_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(s)-A_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(s)
, we use lemma
28
:

\left|{[\delta A_{\alpha}(s),\delta A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(s)]}\right|=\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(\omega;t)-\hat{v}^{\delta}_{\alpha}(\omega)e^{-i\omega t}}\right|^{2}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\leq 2\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(0)N_{m}^{2}\bigg(\frac{2e\omega_{c}t}{N_{m}}\bigg)^{N_{m}}\leq\frac{2\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})}{\delta}N_{m}^{2}\bigg(\frac{2e\omega_{c}t}{N_{m}}\bigg)^{N_{m}}.

From these estimates together with
\textnormal{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})\leq\textnormal{TV}(\text{V})
, we obtain the lemma statement.
∎

XIII.3
Proof of proposition 2

Given a local observable
O_{X}
supported on region
X
, and the initial state being vacuum in the independent environment. We will consider two models — one is the original model described by the Hamiltonian
H(t)
, and the next is the model obtained by performing the star to chain transformation
H^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)
and we are interested in analyzing the error between the expected value of the local observable in both of these models. We will estimate this error,
\text{Err}_{O_{X}}
as a sum of three parts:

\text{Err}_{O_{X}}\leq\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,0;l)+\Delta_{O_{X}}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t,0;l)+\text{Err}_{O_{X}}^{l},

where
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,0;l)
is the error in the local observable on truncating the Hamiltonian
H(t)
locally as defined in Eq.
70
,
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,0;l)
is this truncation error but for the Hamiltonian
H^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)
and
\text{Err}_{O_{X}}^{l}
is the error in between the dynamics of the two truncated Hamiltonians which are both now supported only within the region of distance
l
around
X
.

The error
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,0;l)
can be directly estimated from proposition 1. Note now we have
L=2
. A good estimate of the error
\Delta_{O_{X}}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t,0;l)
is slightly more subtle, and requires a good estimate of the Lieb-Robinson velocity corresponding to the Hamiltonian
H^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)
, which in turn requires us to estimate the total variation of its kernels — more specifically, if
\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t-t^{\prime})=[A_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t),A_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}\dagger}(t^{\prime})]
, then we define the upper bounding kernel
\text{V}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}
as usual:

\text{V}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(\tau)=\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\ \left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(\tau)}\right|.

Note that since we are only interested in the dynamics from
0
to
t
, we will only estimate the quantity
\left\|{\bm{\mu}^{\textnormal{R}}[0,t]}\right\|_{\infty}
of the kernels corresponding to
H^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)
, which equals to
\text{TV}(\text{V}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}};[0,t])
. To do so, we will utilize the fact that
\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t)
is close to
\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)
. In particular,

\displaystyle\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t)-\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)}\right|\leq\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t)-\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(t)}\right|+\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha,\omega_{c}}^{\delta}(t)-\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)}\right|.

Now, note that from lemma
25
and
\text{TV}(\text{V}_{\alpha})\leq\text{TV}(\text{V})
,

\displaystyle\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(t)-\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(t)}\right|=\left|{\int_{\left|{\omega}\right|\geq\omega_{c}}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}}\right|\leq\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\delta^{2}\omega_{c}}\text{TV}(\text{V})\exp\bigg[-\bigg(\frac{\omega_{c}\delta}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg],

where we have used lemma
25
. Next, consider [
\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(\omega;t)
is defined in lemma ]

\displaystyle\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(t)-\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\left|{\hat{v}^{\delta}_{\alpha}(\omega)}\right|^{2}e^{-i\omega t}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}-\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\big(\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(\omega;0)\big)^{*}\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(\omega;t)\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}}\right|,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{=}}\left|{\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\big({\hat{v}^{\delta}_{\alpha}(\omega)}\big)^{*}\big(\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)e^{-i\omega t}-\hat{v}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}_{\alpha}(\omega;t)\big)\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}}\right|,

\displaystyle\leq\bigg[\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\bigg]^{1/2}\bigg[\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)e^{-i\omega t}-\hat{v}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}_{\alpha}(\omega;t)}\right|^{2}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\bigg]^{1/2},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu2\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{2}N_{m}\bigg(\frac{2e\omega_{c}t}{N_{m}}\bigg)^{N_{m}/2}\int_{-\omega_{c}}^{\omega_{c}}\left|{\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)}\right|^{2}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi},

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}N_{m}\bigg(\frac{2e\omega_{c}t}{N_{m}}\bigg)^{N_{m}/2}\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c}}(0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu3\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{2}N_{m}\bigg(\frac{2e\omega_{c}t}{N_{m}}\bigg)^{N_{m}/2}\frac{\text{TV}(\text{V})}{\delta},

where in (1) we have used the fact that
\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(\omega;0)=\hat{v}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(\omega)
for almost all
\omega\in[-\omega_{c},\omega_{c}]
— this is implied by lemma
28
, in (2) we have used lemma
28
and in (3) we have used Eq.
216
. Thus, we have that, for any
t
,

\displaystyle\text{TV}(\text{V}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}};[0,t])=\int_{0}^{t}\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(s)}\right|ds,

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{0}^{t}\sup_{\alpha}\left|{\text{V}_{\alpha}^{\delta}(s)}\right|ds+\frac{\gamma_{0}t}{\delta^{2}\omega_{c}}\text{TV}(\text{V})\exp\bigg[-2\bigg(\frac{\omega_{c}\delta}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg]+\sqrt{2}N_{m}t\bigg(\frac{2e\omega_{c}t}{N_{m}}\bigg)^{N_{m}/2}\frac{\text{TV}(\text{V})}{\delta},,

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\text{TV}(\text{V})+\frac{\gamma_{0}t}{\delta^{2}\omega_{c}}\text{TV}(\text{V})\exp\bigg[-\bigg(\frac{\omega_{c}\delta}{16e}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg]+\sqrt{2}N_{m}t\bigg(\frac{2e\omega_{c}t}{N_{m}}\bigg)^{N_{m}/2}\frac{\text{TV}(\text{V})}{\delta}.

which gives us that, for dynamics upto time
t
, a lieb-Robinson velocity of the model
H^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t)
is given by

\displaystyle v_{\text{LR}}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}=v_{\text{LR}}+O\bigg(\frac{t}{\delta^{2}\omega_{c}}\exp\big(-\Omega(\sqrt{\omega_{c}\delta})\big)\bigg)+O\bigg(\frac{N_{m}t}{\delta}\bigg(\frac{2e\omega_{c}t}{N_{m}}\bigg)^{N_{m}/2}\bigg),

(220)

where
v_{\text{LR}}
is the lieb Robinson velocity of the original model.

Now, we estimate the number of modes
N_{m}
required to reduce the total error in the observable,
\text{Err}_{O_{X}}
, to
\leq\varepsilon
. We first pick the length
l
such that
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,0;l),\Delta_{O_{X}}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t,0;l)\leq O(\varepsilon)
. Since our estimate for
v_{\text{LR}}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}
is larger than
v_{\text{LR}}
, it is sufficient to ensure that
\Delta_{O_{X}}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t,0;l)\leq O(\varepsilon)
— from proposition 1, this is obtained by making the choice

\displaystyle l=\Theta(\log\varepsilon^{-1})+\Theta(v_{\text{LR}}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}t).

(221)

For this choice
l
, we next estimate
\text{Err}_{O_{X}}^{l}
from lemmas
24
,
27
and
29
, we obtain that:

\displaystyle\text{Err}_{O_{X}}^{l}\leq O(l^{d}t\lambda_{0}(2\delta))+O(t^{2}l^{2d}\delta)+O\bigg(\frac{l^{d}t^{2}}{\sqrt{\delta^{3}\omega_{c}}}\exp(-\Omega(\sqrt{\omega_{c}\delta}))\bigg)+O\bigg(l^{d}t^{2}\frac{N_{m}}{\delta}\bigg(\frac{2e\omega_{c}t}{N_{m}}\bigg)^{N_{m}/2}\bigg).

(222)

We now make the following choices for the parameters
\omega_{c}
and
\delta
—

\omega_{c}=\frac{N_{m}}{2e^{2}t}\text{ and }\delta=\frac{N_{m}^{1-\epsilon}}{\omega_{c}}=2e^{2}tN_{m}^{-\epsilon},

where
\epsilon<1
is a small positive constant. With this choice together with Eqs.
220
and
221
, we then have that

\displaystyle l

\displaystyle=\Theta(\log\varepsilon^{-1})+\Theta(t)+O\bigg(\frac{t}{N_{m}^{1-2\epsilon}}e^{-\Omega(N_{m}^{(1-\epsilon)/2})}\bigg)+O\big(N_{m}^{1+\epsilon}e^{-N_{m}/2}\big),

and

\text{Err}^{l}_{O_{X}}\leq O\big(l^{d}t\lambda_{0}\big(4e^{2}tN_{m}^{-\epsilon}\big)\big)+O\big(t^{3}l^{2d}N_{m}^{-\epsilon}\big)+O\big({l^{d}tN_{m}^{(3\epsilon-1)/2}}e^{-\Omega(N_{m}^{(1-\epsilon)/2})}\big)+O\big(l^{d}tN_{m}^{1+\epsilon}e^{-N_{m}/2}\big).

Clearly, the most dominant terms in this upper bound for
\text{Err}_{O_{X}}^{l}
are the first two since the remaining two are exponentially suppressed in
N_{m}
. We can then pick
N_{m}
to make these two terms smaller than
O(\varepsilon)
to obtain

N_{m}=\Theta\bigg(\bigg(\frac{l^{2d}t^{3}}{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{1+o(1)}\bigg)+\Theta\bigg(\bigg(t\lambda_{0}^{-1}\bigg(\frac{\varepsilon}{l^{d}t}\bigg)\bigg)^{1+o(1)}\bigg),

where, since
\epsilon
can be chosen to be a constant arbitrarily close to 1, we have replaced the power of
1/\epsilon
in the
O
-notation as
1+o(1)
. With this choice of
N_{m}
, we see that as
t\to\infty,\varepsilon\to 0
, we can neglect the terms that are exponentially small in
N_{m}
in both
l
and
\text{Err}_{O_{X}}^{l}
— in particular, we then obtain that
l=\Theta(\log{\varepsilon}^{-1})+\Theta(t)
with which our estimate for
N_{m}
becomes:

N_{m}=\Theta\big(\big(\varepsilon^{-1}{t^{2d+3}}\big)^{1+o(1)}\big)+\Theta\big(\big({\varepsilon}^{-1}\log\varepsilon^{-1}\big)^{1+o(1)}\big)+\Theta\big(\big(t\mu^{\textnormal{L}}_{0}\big(\varepsilon^{-1}\Theta(t^{d+1})+\Theta(t\log^{d}\varepsilon^{-1})\big)^{1+o(1)}\big),

where we have introduced
\mu^{\textnormal{L}}_{0}(x)=\lambda_{0}^{-1}(x^{-1})
. Since we have ensured
\Delta_{O_{X}}(t,0;l),\Delta_{O_{X}}^{\delta,\omega_{c},N_{m}}(t,0,l),\text{Err}_{O_{X}}^{l}\leq O(\varepsilon)
, we obtain the proposition statement.

Appendix A
Complete proof of Proposition
4

We first present a useful technical lemma that we would need for proving proposition 4.

Lemma 30
.

Suppose
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
is continuous and differentiable with respect to either of its arguments then

(a)

Consider a closed interval,
[a,b]
. Suppose that
K\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
is
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}-
small over a test function space
\textnormal{PC}_{\{0,b-a\}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. then

\left|{\int_{a}^{b}\int_{a}^{s_{1}}K(s_{1}-s_{2})f(s_{1},s_{2})ds_{1}ds_{2}}\right|\leq(\lambda_{0}(b-a)+\lambda_{1})\sup_{s_{1},s_{2}\in[a,b]}\left|{f(s_{1},s_{2})}\right|+\lambda_{1}(b-a)\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\sup_{s_{1},s_{2}\in[a,b]}|{\partial_{s_{i}}f(s_{1},s_{2})}|.

(b)

Consider two closed intervals,
[a_{1},b_{1}]
and
[a_{2},b_{2}]
which may or may not intersect with each other. Suppose that
K\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
is
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}-
small over a test function space
\textnormal{PC}_{\{a_{1}-a_{2},a_{1}-b_{2},b_{1}-a_{2},b_{1}-b_{2}\}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
then

\left|{\int_{a_{1}}^{b_{1}}\int_{a_{2}}^{b_{2}}K(s_{1}-s_{2})f(s_{1},s_{2})ds_{1}ds_{2}}\right|\leq(\lambda_{0}(b_{2}-a_{2})+2\lambda_{1})\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1}\in[a_{1},b_{1}]\\
s_{2}\in[a_{2},b_{2}]\end{subarray}}\left|{f(s_{1},s_{2})}\right|+\lambda_{1}\big(b_{2}-a_{2})\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1}\in[a_{1},b_{1}]\\
s_{2}\in[a_{2},b_{2}]\end{subarray}}\left|{\partial_{s_{i}}f(s_{1},s_{2})}\right|.

Proof.

(a) For notational convenience, introduce

\displaystyle\phi_{0}=\sup_{s_{1},s_{2}\in[a,b]}|{f(s_{1},s_{2})}|\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{1}=\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\sup_{s_{1},s_{2}\in[a,b]}|{\partial_{s_{i}}f(s_{1},s_{2})}|.

(223)

First, we rewrite the integral under consideration, by a change of variables
s_{1},s_{2}\to s=s_{1}-s_{2},s^{\prime}=s_{2}
, as

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\int_{a}^{s_{1}}K(s_{1}-s_{2})f(s_{1},s_{2})ds_{1}ds_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{b-a}K(s)\bigg[\int_{a}^{b-s}f(s+s^{\prime},s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}\bigg]ds,

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K(s)\underbrace{\bigg[\Theta_{[0,b-a]}(s)\int_{a}^{b-s}f(s+s^{\prime},s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}\bigg]}_{F(s)}ds,

Clearly, we note that
F
has a discontinuity/non-differentiability only at
s\in\{0,b-a\}\subseteq t^{*}
, and consequently
F\in\text{PC}_{\{0,b-a\}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Furthermore, we also have that

\displaystyle\forall s\in\mathbb{R}:\left|{F(s)}\right|\leq\left|{\int_{a}^{b-s}f(s+s^{\prime},s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}}\right|\leq(b-a)\phi_{0}\implies\left\|{F}\right\|_{\infty}\leq(b-a)\phi_{0},

(224)

and for
s\notin\{0,b-a\}

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(s)=\begin{cases}-f(b,b-s)+\int_{a}^{b-s}\partial_{1}f(s+s^{\prime},s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}&\text{ if }s\in(0,b-a),\\
0&\text{ if }s\notin[0,b-a].\end{cases}

Therefore, we also have that

\displaystyle\forall s\notin\{0,b-a\}:\left|{F^{\prime}(s)}\right|\leq\phi_{0}+(b-a)\phi_{1}\implies\left\|{F^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\phi_{0}+(b-a)\phi_{1}.

(225)

From Eqs.
224
and
225
together with the fact that
K
is
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}
-small on
\text{PC}_{t^{*}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, it follows that

\displaystyle\left|{\int_{a}^{b}\int_{a}^{s_{1}}K(s_{1}-s_{2})f(s_{1},s_{2})ds_{1}ds_{2}}\right|=\left|{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K(s)F(s)ds}\right|\leq\lambda_{0}(b-a)\phi_{0}+\lambda_{1}\big(\phi_{0}+(b-a)\phi_{1}\big),

which proves the lemma statement.

(b) For notational convenience, introduce

\displaystyle\phi_{0}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1}\in[a_{1},b_{1}]\\
s_{2}\in[a_{2},b_{2}]\end{subarray}}\left|{f(s_{1},s_{2})}\right|\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{1}=\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\sup_{s_{1},s_{2}\in[a,b]}|{\partial_{s_{i}}f(s_{1},s_{2})}|.

(226)

We begin by rewriting the integral under consideration as

\displaystyle\int_{a_{1}}^{b_{1}}\int_{a_{2}}^{b_{2}}K(s_{1}-s_{2})f(s_{1},s_{2})ds_{1}ds_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K(s_{1}-s_{2})f(s_{1},s_{2})\Theta_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}(s_{1})\Theta_{[a_{2},b_{2}]}(s_{2})ds_{1}ds_{2},

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K(s)\underbrace{\bigg[\int_{a_{2}}^{b_{2}}f(s+s^{\prime},s^{\prime})\Theta_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}(s^{\prime}+s)ds^{\prime}\bigg]}_{F(s)}ds.

(227)

We will now establish that
F\in\text{PC}_{\{a_{1}-a_{2},a_{1}-b_{2},b_{1}-a_{2},b_{1}-b_{2}\}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. To do so, we need to show that all the points of discontinuity or non-differentiability of
F
are in
\{a_{1}-a_{2},a_{1}-b_{2},b_{1}-a_{2},b_{1}-b_{2}\}
. To do so, let us attempt to differentiate
F(s)
with respect to
s
— we obtain that

F^{\prime}(s)=\int_{a_{2}}^{b_{2}}\partial_{s_{1}}f(s+s^{\prime},s^{\prime})\Theta_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}(s^{\prime}+s)ds^{\prime}+\int_{a_{2}}^{b_{2}}f(s+s^{\prime},s^{\prime})\big(\delta(s+s^{\prime}-a_{1})-\delta(s+s^{\prime}-b_{1})\big)ds^{\prime},

with the derivative being only defined when
s\notin\{a_{1}-a_{2},a_{1}-b_{2},b_{1}-a_{2},b_{1}-b_{2}\}
. From this expression, it is clear that
F^{\prime}(s)
can only have a discontinuity/non-differentiability when
s\in\{a_{1}-a_{2},a_{1}-b_{2},b_{1}-a_{2},b_{1}-b_{2}\}
, thus establishing that
F\in\text{PC}_{\{a_{1}-a_{2},a_{1}-b_{2},b_{1}-a_{2},b_{1}-b_{2}\}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. We can also see from this expression that for
s\notin\{a_{1}-a_{2},a_{1}-b_{2},b_{1}-a_{2},b_{1}-b_{2}\}
,

\displaystyle\left|{F^{\prime}(s)}\right|\leq\phi_{1}(b_{2}-a_{2})+2\phi_{0}\implies\left\|{F^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\phi_{1}(b_{2}-a_{2})+2\phi_{0}.

(228)

Furthermore, it is also clear that
\forall s\notin\{a_{1}-a_{2},a_{1}-b_{2},b_{1}-a_{2},b_{1}-b_{2}\}
,

\displaystyle\left|{F(s)}\right|\leq\phi_{0}(b_{2}-a_{2}).

(229)

From Eqs.
228
,
229
and the fact that
K
is
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}-
small on
\text{PC}_{\{a_{1}-a_{2},a_{1}-b_{2},b_{1}-a_{2},b_{1}-b_{2}\}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, we obtain that

\left|{\int_{a_{1}}^{b_{1}}\int_{a_{2}}^{b_{2}}K(s_{1}-s_{2})f(s_{1},s_{2})ds_{1}ds_{2}}\right|=\left|{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K(s)F(s)ds}\right|\leq\lambda_{0}\phi_{0}(b_{2}-a_{2})+\lambda_{1}\big(\phi_{1}(b_{2}-a_{2})+2\phi_{0}\big),

which proves the lemma.

∎

Proof of proposition
4
.

It will be convenient to introduce, for
t>s
,

\displaystyle u(t,s)=\sup_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\text{TV}(K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}};[s,t])\quad\text{and}\quad u_{c}(t,s)=\sup_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{s}^{t}|{K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime};c}(s^{\prime})}|ds^{\prime},

(230)

where
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime};c}
is the continuous part of
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
. Note that since, by assumption,
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
are locally integrable (assumption
1
) and the indices
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
run over a finite set,
u(t,s),u_{c}(t,s)<\infty
. Furthermore, since
K_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime};c}
are continuous functions, so is
u_{c}(t,s)
with
u_{c}(t,t)=0
.

Now, consider
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
— we obtain that

\displaystyle\left|{\llangle{I_{E},O_{S}}|\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{U}}^{\delta_{1}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})\Omega_{i}\bigg)|{\rho_{E},\sigma_{S}}\rrangle-\llangle{I_{E},O_{S}}|\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{U}}^{\delta_{2}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(t_{i},s_{i})\Omega_{i}\bigg)|{\rho_{E},\sigma_{S}}\rrangle}\right|
